--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page is a descriptive catalog entry or label for a 15th-century manuscript of Marcus Manilius's *Astronomicon*. It provides bibliographical details, physical description, and a history of ownership (provenance).</meta>

# MARCUS MANILIUS. ASTRONOMY, FOUR BOOKS. <note>original Latin title: "Astronomicon, Libri IV"</note>
->On Vellum <term>Vellum: High-quality parchment made from calfskin</term>. Small Quarto <term>4º: A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</term>. 15th Century.<-

Manilius lived during the reign of Augustus <note>The first Roman Emperor, reigning from 27 BCE to 14 CE</note>. The first volume of his works treats of the spheres, the form of the earth, the division of the sky and the constellations. The remaining volumes are mostly astrological. The book was very popular during the Middle Ages. It was first printed in 1472, and often reprinted till the end of the 16th Century.

The manuscript in the Library was written in small, beautiful italic characters <note>A cursive style of handwriting developed in Renaissance Italy</note> by an Italian scribe, Peregrinus Allius, at Ferrara in 1461. It occupies 86 leaves <note>individual sheets of parchment</note>. Long lines, twenty-five to a page. Illustrated initials with marginal decorations on the first page. Bound in old vellum.

On the fly-leaf <note>the blank page at the very beginning of a bound book</note> is written: “Book of Lord Grimani, Cardinal of Saint Mark” <note>original Latin: "Liber D. Grimani Cardinalis S. Marci"</note>; and below, in a different hand: “M[arc], Patriarch of Aquileia” <note>original Latin: "M. Patriarchae Aquileiensis"</note>. The first name is that of Domenico Grimani (1461–1523), Cardinal of St. Mark and Patriarch of Aquileia, the owner of the famous Grimani Breviary and of a great library; the second name is probably that of his nephew, Marin or Marc Grimani, who succeeded him in the Patriarchate. Soon the volume became the property of Pierre Pithou (1496–1556), a French jurist and scholar <note>original: "savant"</note>, who lived at Troyes. Later it belonged to the de Rosny Library, the book-plate of which is on the inside cover.

From the Library of the Earl of Ashburnham, (Barrois Collection).

Size, 5 × 7½ inches.

<summary>This page describes a 1461 Italian manuscript of Manilius's "Astronomicon," detailing its classical content, its creation in Ferrara, and its distinguished chain of owners including Cardinal Domenico Grimani and Pierre Pithou.</summary>
<keywords>Marcus Manilius, Astronomy, Astrology, Renaissance manuscript, Peregrinus Allius, Ferrara, Domenico Grimani, Pierre Pithou, Earl of Ashburnham, provenance, vellum</keywords>

# Manilius’s Astronomy

->**The Astronomical Work of Manilius**<-

<note>original: "Manlii Astronomicum". Marcus Manilius was a Roman author who lived during the reigns of Augustus and Tiberius. His work, the *Astronomica*, is a <term>didactic poem|A genre of poetry designed to instruct the reader; here, Manilius explains the complexities of the celestial spheres and fate.</term> and is considered one of the most important surviving texts on ancient astrology and mathematical astronomy.</note>

<summary>The title page for the astronomical and astrological treatise written by the Roman poet Marcus Manilius in the early 1st century AD.</summary>
<keywords>Marcus Manilius, Astronomica, Roman astronomy, didactic poetry, Latin manuscript, title page</keywords>

The book of Domenico Grimani, Cardinal <gloss>of</gloss> San Marco <note>Domenico Grimani was a powerful Italian nobleman, cardinal, and a renowned patron of the arts and collector of books.</note>

Versus which Cicero cites in the book *On Fate* <note>original: "Ci. allegat in libro d fato"; while the text attributes these lines to Cicero's philosophical work *On Fate*, they are actually from the Roman satirist Juvenal.</note>

There are those who attribute all things to the accidents of fortune,
And believe that the world moves with no ruler:
Nature turning the cycles of both months and years.

<meta>This page follows an ownership inscription and a short excerpt regarding fate. It begins the primary text of the manuscript: Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*, an ancient Roman didactic poem about astronomy and astrology. The humanistic script and lavish decoration indicate this was a high-status Renaissance production, likely commissioned for a major library like that of Cardinal Grimani.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK OF THE *ASTRONOMICA* BY THE POET MARCUS MANILIUS ADRESSED TO OCTAVIAN AUGUS-
## TUS BEGINS.

<margin>Astronomical Matters <note>original: "Ἀστρονομικῶν"</note></margin>

<image-desc>A large illuminated initial 'C' starts the text, featuring white and blue vine-work (white vine-stem style) on a gold leaf background. This motif extends into a border along the top and left margins, decorated with blue, green, and red filigree and gold bezants with radiating pen-work.</image-desc>

**C**onscious of fate and the divine arts, the stars that vary the diverse fortunes of men through song—I undertake to lead down a work of <unclear>things</unclear> <note>original transcription "rorū" is likely a scribal error for "rerum" (of things/matters).</note> from the heavenly world. I am the first who dares to stir Helicon <note>A mountain in Greece, legendary home of the Muses; the poet claims he is the first to write about astrology in Latin verse.</note> with new songs and its forests with green nodding peaks, bringing sacred rites of hospitality mentioned by none of my predecessors.

Now for me, you, Caesar <note>While the title names Augustus, scholars debate if this was addressed to him or his successor Tiberius; regardless, it invokes the Emperor as a stabilizing, divine force.</note>—leader and father of the fatherland—who rule an obedient world with august laws, and who, as a god yourself, merit the world granted to your father, you give me courage and provide the strength to sing of such great things. Now, look with favor upon those scrutinizing the world itself from closer at hand; a passion seizes me to reveal ethereal meanings through poems. During this time of peace, there is leisure; so much does it please me to go through the very air and to live wandering in the immense sky, and to learn the signs and the opposing courses of the stars. 

To know these things alone is too little; it pleases me more eagerly to know the inner heart of the great world itself: by what law it rules and what creatures it generates with its signs <note>This refers to the zodiac signs and their influence on the birth and nature of living things.</note>, to perceive these things and to record them in meter as Apollo <note>original: "phoebo"; Phoebus Apollo was the god of both poetry and the sun.</note> guides the melody. Two altars shine for me with fires placed upon them; I pray at two temples, surrounded by a double passion: that of the song and that of the subject matter, as it sings with a fixed law. The world itself resounds around the poet with its immense sphere.

<summary>This page marks the grand opening of Manilius's "Astronomica," featuring a dedication to the Roman Emperor and an invocation of the Muses as the author prepares to explain the astrological laws of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Marcus Manilius, Astronomica, Augustus, astrology, Helicon, Apollo, celestial mechanics, Latin poetry, Renaissance illumination</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening of Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*. On the previous page, the poet invoked the Emperor (Augustus or Tiberius) and declared his intention to be the first to translate the "divine art" of astrology into Latin verse. This section transitions from the poet's personal mission to the mythological and historical origins of astronomical knowledge, credited to the god Mercury and the ancient priest-kings of Egypt and Mesopotamia.</meta>

Scarcely <gloss>13</gloss> <note>The number 13 likely refers to the line number in the original manuscript or source text.</note> set free, the song sends words into their own patterns,
Which first it was allowed <gloss>fully</gloss> <note>The scribal notation 'B' is an abbreviation for 'bene' (well or fully), indicating the completion of the thought.</note> for the lands to know deeply—
The gifts of the celestials. For who, while the gods were hiding the truth,
Could have stolen by theft the secret of the world by which all things are governed?
Who would have attempted so much within a human heart
That, with the gods unwilling, he would desire to seem a god himself?
You, Mercury <note>original: "cyllenie" (the Cyllenian), a common title for Mercury, who was born on Mount Cyllene and credited with inventing the lyre and the sciences.</note>, are the leader and author of so great a sacred mystery;
Through you, the inner heaven and the stars are now known,
Opening the lofty ways under one single vault,
And the stars obeying their own limits through the void,
And the names and courses of the signs, <gloss>fully</gloss> revealing their powers,
So that the face of the world might be greater and more venerable.
For [this was] not merely hope <gloss>and</gloss> <note>The shorthand '&' is the Latin 'et' (and).</note> but the very power of things,
That the nations might feel the presence of God, how great He was;
And Nature gave the strength and revealed herself,
Having first deigned <gloss>fully</gloss> to move royal minds
Which touch the summits of things closest to heaven.
These kings tamed wild nations under the rising sun itself,
Those lands which the Euphrates carries and which the Nile floods,
Where the sky turns back and flies over the dark cities <note>A reference to the peoples of the south and east, such as the Egyptians and Ethiopians, whose skin was said to be darkened by the sun's proximity.</note>.
And then they cultivated temples with sacred rites through <gloss>every</gloss> <note>The marker '+' often indicates a contraction or a specific reading, here likely referring to 'per omne' (through all).</note> age,
And chosen priests, for the sake of public prayers,
Joined themselves to the god by their office; in these men, the very presence
Of the powerful divinity <note>original: "numinis," here transcribed with a slight error as "huminis."</note> kindled a pure mind,
And the god himself raised them toward the divine, and stood revealed to his servants.

<summary>Manilius credits the god Mercury with revealing the secrets of the stars to humanity and describes how ancient priest-kings in Egypt and Babylon first studied the heavens. He argues that this knowledge was a divine gift intended to make the greatness of God and Nature visible to the world.</summary>

<keywords>Mercury, Cyllenius, Euphrates, Nile, astrology, divine revelation, priest-kings, ancient astronomy, Marcus Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the *Astronomica*. The previous page concluded with the god Mercury revealing the heavens to humanity. Now, Manilius describes the ancient kings and priests—the first human astrologers—who spent generations observing the stars to decode the laws of fate.</meta>

These men <note>Referencing the ancient priest-kings of Egypt and Babylon mentioned on the previous page.</note> alone were able to stir so great a glory, and through their art they were the first to see the fates hanging from the **wandering stars** <note>original: "sideribus uagis," referring to the planets, which appear to move independently against the fixed backdrop of the constellations.</note>. For they marked individual moments of time with their own specific outcomes, having embraced long ages through constant, diligent care. They sought to know what day of birth belonged to each person, what their life would be, under which laws of fortune they fell, what power each hour held, and how great a difference even the smallest movements of the heavens could make.

After the whole appearance of the sky was understood as the stars returned to their proper seats, and after the specific power of every heavenly form was assigned to the fixed orders of the fates, experience created this art through various applications. With history itself showing the way, long observation grasped the stars ruling by **silent laws** <note>The "silent laws" (tacitis legibus) refer to the Stoic belief in a rational, pre-determined order of the universe that operates without the need for constant divine intervention.</note>. They perceived that the entire world is moved by an alternating **reason** <term>Ratio: The underlying logic or divine intelligence that governs the cosmos in Stoic philosophy.</term>, and they learned to discern the turns of fate by certain signs.

For before their time, life was uncultivated and lacked any distinction; though humans had the appearance of men, they were devoid of reason. They stood amazed, hanging upon the new light of the world—now grieving as if the stars had been lost when they set, then joyful when they were born again. They could not yet distinguish the various lengths of days, the uncertain timing of the nights, nor why the shadows changed as the sun receded or drew closer to its own causes. Ingenuity had not yet created the learned arts, and the vast earth lay idle under unrefined farmers. In those days, gold still dwelt undisturbed in the deserted mountains.

<summary>Manilius describes how ancient observers transitioned from a state of primitive fear of the dark to a scientific understanding of planetary motion and astrological influence. He emphasizes that "experience" and "long observation" were the foundations of the art of astrology.</summary>

<keywords>planets, Fate, Stoic Reason, history of science, ancient kings, Golden Age, astrology, astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of human development. After describing how the first kings and priests observed the stars, Manilius now describes the transition from a primitive, isolated state of nature to a complex civilization defined by technology, trade, magic, and finally, scientific inquiry.</meta>

And the sea had withdrawn from the unmoving, new worlds.
No one dared to trust their lives to the deep or their prayers to the winds;
everyone thought it enough to feed themselves <note>Manilius refers to the "Golden Age," a mythical early period where humans lived simply off the land without seafaring or trade.</note>.
T<gloss>3</gloss>hus when the passing of long ages sharpened mortal hearts,
A<gloss>+</gloss>nd labor gave wit to those in need, and Fortune,
by pressing upon them, commanded each to look out for their own welfare.
Hearts, drawn apart, struggled with various concerns,
A<gloss>+</gloss>nd whatever wise experience discovered through trial,
I<gloss>n</gloss> joy they gave the invention to the common good.
Then even the barbarian tongue received its own laws <note>Referring to the development of grammar and structured language from primitive speech.</note>,
A<gloss>+</gloss>nd the fields were tilled with a variety of crops,
A<gloss>+</gloss>nd the wandering sailor pierced the blind sea.
<margin>They discovered</margin>
He established trade routes through unknown lands.
Then antiquity devised the arts of war and peace,
for experience always sows new things from old.
Lest I sing only of common things: they learned the languages of birds <note>Referencing augury, the ancient practice of interpreting the flight and song of birds to discern the will of the gods.</note>,
to consult the entrails <note>Known as haruspicy, this involved examining the internal organs of sacrificed animals to predict the future.</note> and to break snakes with their voices.
They learned to summon ghosts and stir the depths of the underworld <note>original: "Acheronta," referring to the Acheron, a river in the Greco-Roman land of the dead.</note>,
I<gloss>n</gloss>verting days into night and nights into light.
Such ingenuity conquered all things by daring all.
Nor did it set its hand or its limit to its work
U<gloss>13</gloss>ntil **Reason** <term>Ratio: In Stoic thought, the divine spark of logic in humans that allows them to understand the rational structure of the universe.</term> ascended to the heavens and sought out the deep
causes in the nature of things, and saw all that exists.
Why the clouds were shaken and struck with such a great crash;
Why winter snow was softer than summer hail;

<summary>This page describes the evolution of humanity from primitive survival to the mastery of crafts, navigation, and even the occult arts, culminating in the use of Reason to explore the heavens and the laws of nature.</summary>
<keywords>human progress, civilization, navigation, divination, Reason, Acheron, meteorology, Stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the development of early human civilization to the birth of scientific inquiry. Having described the origins of trade, language, and divination on the previous page, Manilius now focuses on the human mind's ultimate victory: the intellectual conquest of the universe and the rejection of superstitious fear in favor of rational causes.</meta>

[Why] the lands would burn and the solid orb of the earth would tremble; 
Why the rains fall and what causes the winds to move—
[The human mind] saw through these things, and for our souls, solved the miracles of the world <note>Manilius argues that scientific understanding removes the terrifying "miracle" or mystery of natural phenomena, replacing fear with knowledge.</note>.
It snatched the lightning bolt and the power of thundering from Jove <note>original: "iovi." Jupiter, the king of the gods. This line suggests that by understanding the physical causes of lightning, humans no longer had to fear it as a weapon of a fickle god.</note>,
And assigned the sound to the winds and the fire to the clouds.
After [the mind] traced these individual things to their own proper causes,
It aimed to know the massive structure of the universe from on high
And intended to encompass the whole of heaven within the mind.
It attributed to the constellations their own shapes and names,
And noted which cycles they performed under a fixed lot;
It saw that everything moves according to the number and appearance of the world, 
With the various stars changing our fates in a prescribed order.

This work of mine rises up, hallowed by no previous poems <note>Manilius is claiming to be the first to write a technical didactic poem on astrology in Latin, a common trope among ancient poets seeking to establish their authority.</note>.
May Fortune favor this great labor;
May a long life and a gentle old age be granted to me,
So that I may be able to bring forth such massive subjects,
And traverse great things with a care equal to that given to small ones.

### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE WORLD

And since this song descends from high heaven,
And the fixed order of the fates comes down to the lands,
First, the very form of nature must be sung by me;
The whole world must be set forth under its own image—
Whether you prefer to think of it as seeking its seeds from nothing,
Lacking a birth, always having been,
And destined to exist forever, lacking a beginning and a fate equally <note>Manilius here introduces the philosophical debate between those who believe the universe was created (like the Epicureans) and those who believe it is eternal and uncreated (like the Aristotelians or certain Stoics).</note>.

<summary>Manilius describes the triumph of human reason over superstition and announces his ambition to write a definitive poem on the cosmos. He begins his cosmological inquiry by debating whether the universe is eternal or had a specific beginning.</summary>
<keywords>Reason, Jupiter, Lightning, Scientific Inquiry, Constellations, Fate, Cosmology, Origins of the Universe</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s exploration of the origins of the universe. Following the previous discussion on the human mind's conquest of nature through reason, the poet now examines the various philosophical theories regarding how the world was first formed—moving from the concept of Chaos to the specific elements that compose the cosmos.</meta>

Whether mixed chaos once separated the first beginnings of things and, having labored to bring forth the world in its shining birth, the driven-out darkness fled into the shadows of the underworld;

Or whether the world was resolved into indivisible principles <note>original: "individuis... principiis." This refers to the atomist theory popularized by Epicurus and Lucretius, suggesting the universe is made of tiny, "uncuttable" particles called atoms.</note>, only to return to them again—for it remains after a thousand ages, having come almost from nothing and destined to become a final nothingness, and blind matter completed the heavens and the earth;

Or if fire fashioned the work, and the shimmering flames—which made the eyes of the world <note>The "eyes of the world" refers to the celestial bodies, particularly the sun and moon, which allow the universe to "see" and be seen.</note>—gape through the whole body and fix vibrating lights in the sky;

Whether water gave birth to this, or that earth from which dry matter thrives, and which calls for the fire by which it is dissolved;

But as neither earth, nor flame, nor air, nor water knows a master, they create a god through their four limbs <note>original: "quattuor artus." Manilius personifies the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) as the structural limbs of a divine, living universe.</note>. They constructed the globe of the world and forbid anything to be sought beyond themselves. Then all things are created through them, so that cold things are not lacking to the hot, nor moist to the dry, nor spirit to the solid. 

This is their <term>harmonious discord</term> <note>original: "discordia concors." A central Stoic concept where the tension between opposing forces (like fire and water) is exactly what creates the balance and stability of the natural world.</note> which fashions a clever bond and a generative work. And rather, they make all elements interconnected. There will always be a kind of struggle, and it will remain undecided on each side; and yet, there stands above them the [fate] of humans and gods.

Whatever the appearance was at the origin of things, it is agreed that the body of the universe was arranged in a certain order: swift fire raised itself into the ethereal breezes.

<summary>Manilius surveys different cosmological theories—including Chaos, Atomism, and the Four Elements—concluding that the universe is a balanced "harmonious discord" arranged in a rational order.</summary>
<keywords>cosmogony, Chaos, Atomism, four elements, discordia concors, Stoicism, origins of the universe, celestial fire</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s explanation of the physical formation of the universe. Having established the four elements (fire, air, water, and earth) as the building blocks of the cosmos, the poet now describes how they sorted themselves by weight and density to create the structure of the world we see today, from the outer heavens down to the central Earth.</meta>

And embracing the highest summits of the starry sky,
He made the walls of nature with a rampart of flames. <note>original: "flammarum vallo." This refers to the Stoic belief in a peripheral layer of fire—the Empyrean—that surrounds the universe.</note>
Next, the spirit <term>spiritus: translated here from the Latin "spiritus," representing the Stoic "pneuma," a divine breath or energy that animates matter</term> descended into thin breezes
And stretched out the air through the middle of the world's void.
The breath nourishes the fire, situated just below the neighboring stars.
The third lot spread out the waters and the floating winds;
It poured forth the newborn seas throughout the whole deep,
So that the liquid might exhale thin breezes and vomit forth air,
Nourishing the air which draws its seeds from that very source.
Last, the earth settled down with its gathered weight,
And mud mixed with sand came together more firmly,
While the thin liquid gradually fled toward the top.
And as the moisture withdrew into pure waves,
<margin>÷</margin> Then, enclosed on all sides by the vast sea, the land emerged;
And of all things, the lowest occupies the middle seat on every side. <note>In the geocentric model, the "bottom" of the universe is its center. Because all things fall toward the center, the Earth is considered the lowest point.</note>
<margin>•</margin> And for that reason it remains stable, because the whole world
Flees from it by an equal distance; it has ensured by falling
From every direction that it cannot fall, for it is the center and the bottom of the whole.
Thus, bodies stand firm in these contracted regions,
And by rushing together they prevent each other from going any further.
For unless the earth hung with a balanced weight,
The world would not drive its courses as the stars go under;
Phoebus <note>The sun god Apollo.</note> would go to his setting and never return to his rising,
Nor would the Moon guide her submerged chariot through the voids,
Nor would this Lucifer <note>original: "lucifer." The planet Venus when it appears as the Morning Star, literally "the light-bringer."</note> shine in the morning hours.

---

<margin>÷</margin> The dried-up seas more tightly constrained the lands,
And divided the fluid sea into hollow valleys;
The mountains emerged from the straits, and the world above the waves.

<summary>This page describes the Stoic cosmology of the four elements, explaining how fire rose to the perimeter, air and water formed the middle layers, and the heavy earth settled at the center. Manilius argues that the Earth remains stable at the center of the universe precisely because it is the "bottom" point toward which all things pull equally.</summary>
<keywords>cosmology, four elements, geocentrism, gravity, Stoicism, Phoebus, Lucifer, planetary motion</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the Roman poet Manilius’s discussion of the cosmos. Having previously explained how the Earth settled at the center of the universe as its lowest point, he now argues for the stability and permanence of the celestial system and begins his proof that the Earth is a sphere suspended in space.</meta>

Hesperus <note>The personification of the Evening Star.</note> had given light once Olympus <note>In this context, Olympus refers to the sky or the heavens, which seem to "sink" below the horizon at sunset.</note> was submerged.
And now the earth, not cast down into a bottomless depth,
But suspended in the middle, remains firm, so that everything
Might fall toward it, and the sky might go beneath it and rise again.
For I cannot believe that the rising stars are accidental births,
Nor can I believe the world is born so many times;
Cease thinking of constant births and daily destinies,
Since the face of the world remains the same with its constellations through the ages.
And that the Sun <note>original: "Phoebus," a common poetic name for the sun god.</note> travels from the same parts of heaven;
From there he winds his curves through the legs and feet
Of the second Centaur <note>The constellation Centaurus.</note> to climb the sky again;
He begins [to pass] the Greek ship <note>The constellation Argo Navis, representing the ship of Jason and the Argonauts.</note> by its highest stern-ornaments;
And the moon changes through as many lights and phases.
And nature follows the paths she herself has made,
Lest she wander in error like a novice <term>novice: translated from "tyrocinio," referring to a beginner or a soldier in training</term> as she is carried around.
The day, with its eternal light, shows the seasons;
Now to these regions of the globe, now to those, the same world [moves],
Always moving further from sunrise to sunrise,
Or from sunset to sunrise, the sky passes through with the sun.

-> THAT THE EARTH IS ROUND <-

Nor indeed should the nature of the hanging earth
Seem a wonder to you, since the world itself hangs
And sets its footprints on no foundation.
In part, this comes from the very motion and speed of its flight,
Since the Sun <note>original: "Phoebus"</note> travels suspended and bends back his course.

<summary>Manilius argues that the regularity of the stars and constellations proves the universe is eternal rather than being reborn daily, and asserts that the Earth is a sphere suspended in the center of a floating cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Stoicism, geocentrism, Hesperus, Centaurus, Argo Navis, spherical earth, celestial mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s astronomical proof that the Earth is a sphere. After establishing its central position in the previous page, the poet now describes how the Earth’s roundness is mirrored in the celestial bodies and explains why certain stars are only visible from specific latitudes, such as Egypt.</meta>

Hither and thither, they roll their swift and wild markers in the upper air,
together with the moon and stars, through the voids of the world;
And the Earth, unchanged, hangs suspended according to celestial laws.
Therefore, the Earth has been allotted a central station <note>The transcription reads "cauinam," likely a variation of "stationem" or "cathenam" (chain/position), referring to the Earth's fixed place in the center of the cosmos.</note>,
balanced by the axis and lifted equally from the whole depth.
It is not stretched out into wide flat lands, but is shaped into a sphere,
rising and falling equally on every side.
This is the face of nature; thus the universe itself,
as it wheels into its curves, makes its shapes smooth and rounded.
We see the orb of the stars and the sun, and the roundness of the moon,
as they seek their light with their swelling bodies.
Because a globe is whole, it does not receive the slanting rays all at once.
This shape remains eternal and most similar to the form of the gods;
it has no beginning anywhere, nor an end within itself,
but remains the same in its whole and is equal through all its parts.
Thus the stars remain gathered together and give shape to the universe.
For this reason, we do not see all the constellations from all lands;
nowhere will you find Canopus <note>A very bright star in the southern constellation of Carina; invisible to observers in northern Europe but visible from Egypt.</note> shining
until you reach the shores of the Nile <note>original: "Niliacas," referring to the river Nile in Egypt.</note> across the sea.
But those over whom that star rises will look in vain for Helice <note>A Greek name for Ursa Major, the "Great Bear" constellation, which is always visible in the north but can disappear below the horizon for southern travelers.</note>.
Because the expanses of the sides gape away, and by the swelling in the middle,
the lands snatch away the sky and hem in our sight.
The moon gives you proof of her own spherical <term>spherical: translated from "glomerabilis," meaning capable of being formed into a ball</term> orb;
when she fails <note>This refers to a lunar eclipse.</note> during the night, plunged into dark shadows,
she does not trouble all nations with her darkened star at the same time.
But the eastern <note>original: "eoa," referring to Eos, the goddess of the dawn; hence, the East.</note> peoples miss your light first...

<summary>Manilius argues that the Earth is a sphere, citing the circular nature of celestial bodies and the fact that different constellations are visible from different geographic locations. He specifically notes that the star Canopus is visible in Egypt but not further north, proving the Earth's curvature.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, sphere, Canopus, Helice, lunar eclipse, Earth's curvature, celestial mechanics, Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical discussion from the previous page. While the previous page focused on the central position of the Earth in the cosmos (attributed to Manilius), this text transitions into lines from Avienus’s *Aratea*. It provides a poetic proof of the Earth's sphericity by describing how the sun and moon rise at different times for different peoples and the existence of the "Antipodes" (people living on the other side of the globe).</meta>

Then, look at whatever stars dwell around the middle of the pole,
Those that fly to the furthest western <note>original: "hesperios," referring to Hesperia or the West.</note> regions with stained wings,
And late in the evening, they strike the air among the distant peoples.
For if the Earth were flat, snatched from all curvature,
The light would fail across the whole world at once in a wretched state.
But because the Earth is drawn out into a smooth swelling <term>curvature: the poet uses "tumor" (swelling) to describe the physical bulge of the Earth's sphere.</term>,
The Delian goddess <note>original: "delia," a common poetic name for the Moon, referring to the island of Delos where she and Apollo were born.</note> appears now to these lands and then to those,
Rising and setting at the same time as she is carried in her orbit;
She joins the upward slope and the downward slope of the Earth's belly alike,
Passing over some rotations and leaving others behind.
From this, it is concluded that the shape of the lands is round.
Around this sphere, various nations of men and wild beasts,
And the birds of the air, make their home; part of it faces the North <note>original: "arctos," referring to the northern constellations of the Great and Little Bears.</note>,
And a distant part is habitable in the southern regions:
It lies beneath our feet, yet it is seen above us,
Sloping and long, with the ground itself deceiving our eyes,
With the path rising and falling in equal measure.
When the Sun, having risen there, looks upon this Earth at our sunset,
The day, begun there, awakens the sleeping cities,
And with the light, it brings back the business of life <note>original: "vadimonia," literally legal guarantees or summons, used here to represent the start of the working day.</note> to those lands;
But we are in the night, and we settle sleep into our limbs.
The sea separates both peoples with its waves, yet binds them together.
This is the work constructed from the body of the immense universe,
The members of nature established in their distinct forms:
Of air and fire, of earth and the low-lying sea.

<meta>This page continues the astronomical discourse. A divine force of the soul rules, and by a sacred path
God breathes together and governs with silent reason <note>original: "tacita ratione," referring to the underlying logical order of the universe.</note>.
And He distributes many laws into all parts
So that one might create and bear the strengths of another,
And the whole may remain related through various forms.

### CHAPTER ON THE SIGNS.

Now I shall recount to you the flames of the signs shining from everywhere
In certain orders, and first those shall be sung <gloss>original Latin suffix -ur completes "canentur"</gloss>
Which gird the middle of the world in an oblique order <note>This refers to the ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun across the sky, which is tilted or "oblique" relative to the celestial equator.</note>
And carry the Sun in alternating turns through the seasons.
And other stars struggling against the opposing world,
All which you might see varied in a serene sky,
From which also the whole reasoning of the fates <note>original: "ratio fatorum," the foundational concept of astrology that the stars determine human destiny.</note> is drawn,
So that it may be that first part of the world which touches the heights.

### On the twelve constellations of the zodiac, and first on Aries

The golden-fleeced Aries <note>The Ram</note>, the leader, shining in his wool,
Looks back wondering to see the Bull <note>Taurus</note> rise opposite,
With lowered face and the Twins <note>Gemini</note> calling from his brow.
Whom the Crab <note>Cancer</note> follows; the Lion <note>Leo</note> follows the Crab, the Virgin <note>Virgo</note> the Lion.
Then, when the day is equal with the time of night <note>Referring to the Autumnal Equinox.</note>, the Scales <note>Libra</note>
Draws in the Scorpion <note>Scorpio</note>, shining with a burning star.
Into whose tail the Archer <note>Sagittarius</note> directs a drawn bow,
Joined with a horse <term>centaur: a mythical creature with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse, representing the constellation Sagittarius.</term>, even now about to send a winged arrow.
Then comes Capricorn, bent in a narrow constellation.
After him, Aquarius pours out his tilted urn.

...to the Fishes <note>Pisces</note>, entering the waters accustomed to the river,
Whom the Ram <note>Aries</note> touches, closing the final signs.
But where the sky rises up toward the shining Bears <note>Arctos; the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor</note>,
And all the stars look down from the highest part of heaven,
These stars know no settings <note>original: "obitus." In the Northern Hemisphere, these circumpolar stars never dip below the horizon.</note>: and only on one peak,
Undivided in their position, they twist the sky and the stars.
A thin axis is drawn through the icy air,
And it rules the balanced world with its divided pivot <note>original: "cardine," referring to the celestial pole or hinge upon which the heavens turn.</note>,
Around which the starry orb is rolled in the middle
And rotates the ethereal courses, itself unmoved. But that axis,
Through the empty spaces of the great world, reaches to the two Bears
And, directed through the orb of the earth itself, it looks out.
This axis does not stand with the solid strength of a body,
Nor does it have a heavy weight so as to bear the burden of the high ether;
But since all the air always rolls in a circle,
And where it once began, the whole flies from every side into itself,
Whatever is in the middle, around which all things move,
Is so slight <note>original: "tenue," meaning thin or ethereal.</note> that it cannot be turned within itself,
Nor can it now be tilted nor turn itself into a circle.
This they have called the Axis, which has no motion of its own,
While it itself is seen as all things flying around it move.
Its highest points hold signs most well-known to dispatched sailors,
Signs leading the eager mariners through the immense sea;
And Helice <note>Another name for the Great Bear, Ursa Major</note>, the greater, marks out the Greater Bear:
Seven stars mark her, vying with one another in their light.

And the Lyre, its horns drawn down through the sky, is conspicuous among the stars; with this Orpheus once captivated all that he touched with song, even reaching the spirits of the dead. Their war will always be an equal struggle between balanced forces. 

Next is the lot of the Swan, which Jupiter himself placed in the sky—the form he first took to seize his lover, when the god descended transformed into a snowy swan <note>original: "holorem," an archaic word for swan (cygnus)</note> and submitted his feathered back to the trusting Leda. at the stars that glide over parched lands,
and those which, between the icy sign of Capricorn and the pole,
display their lights in the lowest part of the world.
The other part of the orb lies beneath the waters, inaccessible to us.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the heavens from the previous description of the northern constellations and the zodiacal Bull. And unknown races of men and kingdoms not yet traversed,
drawing their common light from a single sun,
see different shadows, and signs falling to the left,
and witness the sun rising on the right of the visible sky.
The world is no smaller for them, nor is its light any worse,
nor do fewer stars arise in that part of the celestial sphere.
In other things they are not inferior, yet they are surpassed by one star:
the star that fell to our own world <note>original: "nostro... orbi"; the author is referring to the Roman world</note>,

Near the Twins <note>The constellation Gemini</note>, one may see Orion
stretching his arms across a great portion of the sky,
rising toward the stars with no less an extended stride.
Single lights mark his shining shoulders,
and his sword is drawn, hanging down with three slanted stars.
But the head of Orion, immersed in high heaven <note>original: "olympo"</note>,
is marked by three lights on his faint face;
not because they are less bright, but because they recede further into the heights.
With him as leader, the stars run through the whole world.
The <term>Canicula<note>The "Little Dog" or Dog Star, known to us as Sirius. Its rising was associated with the "Dog Days" of summer heat.</note></term> follows, strained in its rapid course;
no star arrives upon the earth more violently than this one.
It sets heavily, and it does not rise shivering with cold <note>The poet notes that unlike other stars, Sirius brings heat rather than winter chill.</note>.
Between these, and the paths of the sun and the hidden Bears <note>The constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor</note>,
is the axis which the weight of the world turns with a creaking sound.
There, the poet paints the sky with stars in a foreign circle—
stars which the prophets spoke of before they were even known.

The world could not have endured such immense fires

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical transition from the previous page, moving from a physical description of the constellations (the "Bears" and the "Dragon") to a Stoic argument for the divine, rational order of the universe. It contrasts the apparent chaos of the night sky with its underlying mathematical regularity, specifically attacking the Epicurean theory of a world made of random atoms.</meta>

Though many more stars may then be seen in the empty sky, they do not deceive in their number, nor are they carried along mixed together with smaller things. And so that you might better recognize the bright signs, they know no varied settings nor varied returns; but fixed stars rise into their own lights and keep their births and settings in order. This whole world is the more marvelous for its immense mass because reason <note>original: "ratio"; here referring to the Stoic concept of the 'Logos' or the rational principle that permeates and organizes the universe.</note> and laws are obeyed by all things. Nowhere does the crowd of stars cause harm, nothing wanders in those parts, nor is anything carried more widely or with a changed order. What appears so confused in its aspect, yet is so certain in its cycles?

## THAT THE WORLD IS ETERNAL.

And no reason seems more present to me, by which it is clear that the world is governed by divine power <note>original: "numine"; the divine will or presence that directs the cosmos.</note> and that it is itself a god; it did not come together by chance <note>Manilius is arguing against the Epicurean view—famously championed by the poet Lucretius—that the world formed by the random collision of atoms.</note> as he wished it to be believed who first built the walls of the world from the smallest seeds <note>original: "seminibus"; refers to the 'atoms' of Democritus and Epicurus.</note> and into those same seeds dissolves them again. And who would believe that the seas, the lands, the stars of heaven, and the ethers—while the Creator fashions immense spheres within their boundaries—could consist of dissolving parts, or that all things return to their own first principles and change the shapes of things? Who would believe that such great masses of power were created without divine will from the smallest things and by a blind pact? If chance gave these things to us, then chance would govern them. But why then do the signs rise in their arranged turns

<meta>catchword: and just as</meta>

<summary>Manilius argues that the perfect regularity of the stars proves the universe is governed by divine reason (Ratio) rather than the random collision of atoms proposed by Epicurean philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, cosmology, Stoicism, atoms, Epicureanism, divine providence, celestial order, ratio, numen</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the Stoic argument for the divine, rational order of the universe. Having argued against the Epicurean idea of a world formed by random atoms, Manilius now uses the permanence of the constellations—contrasted against the rise and fall of human empires like Troy, Greece, and Rome—to prove that the heavens are governed by an eternal, divine law.</meta>

And we see them return to their prescribed courses as if by command <note>original: "imperio"; implying a divine decree or "empire" of nature that regulates the stars.</note>, and with none of them hurrying, none are left behind. Why do the same stars always adorn summer nights and the same ones winter nights, and why does each day return a fixed shape to the world and leave a fixed one behind? 

Even in that time when the Greek nations overturned Troy <note>original: "Pergama"; the citadel of Troy, used here as a metonym for the city itself.</note>, the Bear <note>original: "Arctos"; the constellation of the Great Bear (Ursa Major), which rotates around the celestial pole.</note> and Orion moved with opposing fronts—the former content to bend its circles at the pole, the latter rising from the opposite side to meet it, running always through the whole world. Even then, they were able to mark the times of the dark night by these signs, and the heavens had already distinguished their own hours.

How many kingdoms have been uprooted since the destruction of Troy? How many peoples captured? How many times has Fortune brought slavery and empire throughout the world and returned in various ways? To what extent has she revived the Trojan ashes into empire <note>This is a reference to Rome; the Romans believed they were the descendants of Trojan refugees, thus "reviving" Troy's glory.</note>, having forgotten them for so long? Now Greece is crushed by the fates of Asia <note>This refers to the Roman conquest of Greece and the East; Manilius suggests a cyclical exchange of power where the East (Troy/Asia) eventually overcomes its former conqueror (Greece).</note>. 

It is a burden to count the ages, and how many times the returning fiery sun has surveyed the world in its varying circle. All things created are changed by the law of mortality <note>original: "mortali lege"; the Stoic concept that everything on Earth is subject to decay, death, and constant change.</note>; the lands do not recognize themselves as the years turn, and nations have cast off their various appearances through the ages. But the world <note>original: "mundus"; here referring to the cosmos or the celestial sphere, which Stoics believed was eternal and divine.</note> remains unharmed and preserves all its own things, which neither a long span of time increases nor old age diminishes. Nor does its movement run only for a moment, nor does its course grow weary.

<summary>Manilius argues for the divinity of the cosmos by contrasting the eternal, unchanging nature of the constellations with the volatile history of human civilizations like Troy and Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Stoicism, Troy, Orion, Great Bear, Roman Empire, Fortune, immortality of the cosmos, celestial order</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical argument for a divine, unchanging universe to a technical astronomical description of the celestial sphere. It reinforces the idea that while human history is chaotic, the stars follow a fixed, eternal law established by a divine mind.</meta>

It will always be the same, just as it has always been. Our ancestors did not see one world and our descendants another; it is God <note>original: "deus"; Manilius identifies the rational, unchanging order of the universe itself as divine.</note> who does not change throughout the ages. Never will the sun run across to the Great Bear <note>original: "arctos"; the northern constellations which never set in the Mediterranean sky.</note>, nor change its paths to turn its course back toward the sunrise, nor show the dawn rising over new lands. Nor will the moon exceed its fixed cycles of light, but rather it keeps the measure by which it waxes and wanes; nor do the stars, hanging from the heavens, fall to the earth.

### ON THE PARALLEL CIRCLES <note>Manilius begins describing the five principal circles of the celestial sphere: the Arctic, the Summer Tropic (Cancer), the Equator, the Winter Tropic (Capricorn), and the Antarctic.</note>

The circle toward the north supports the shining Bear and is distant by six solid parts <note>A technical measurement of the celestial sphere's division.</note> from the equator. But when spring runs to its end, the sun reaches the stars of the Crab <note>original: "cancri"; the constellation Cancer marks the summer solstice, the longest day of the year.</note>, in which the Sun <note>original: "phoebus"; a poetic name for Apollo, the sun god.</note> spends the greatest amount of light and time, and carries his slow rays through long, winding arcs. This circle takes the name "Summer" from the midday heat, or rather from the title of the season; it marks the goal-post <note>original: "metam"; the turning post in a Roman chariot race, used here for the point where the sun "turns" back toward the south.</note> of the flying sun and designates the furthest point of his fiery path, standing five parts away from the north.

The third circle, placed in the middle region of the world, encircles the whole of heaven <note>original: "olympum"; here meaning the celestial vault rather than the mountain of the gods.</note> with its vast sphere. Looking toward both poles from either side, from here the Sun balances night and day in equal measure, running through the blended seasons of spring and autumn, when he divides the middle of the sky with an equal boundary <note>This describes the Celestial Equator, where the sun's path creates the equinoxes.</note>.

<summary>Manilius asserts the eternal stability of the cosmos as proof of its divine nature before describing the Tropic of Cancer and the Equator.</summary>
<keywords>divine order, eternity, astronomy, Tropic of Cancer, Equator, Phoebus, solstice, equinox</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the celestial sphere begun on page 11v. Having previously described the northern Arctic circle and the Summer Tropic (Cancer), the text now moves southward to describe the Winter Tropic, the Antarctic circle, and the great circles known as colures.</meta>

By four degrees it draws back its threads from the heat <note>The poet uses "threads" (original: *fila*) as a metaphor for the paths or orbits of the stars, measuring the distance between the celestial tropics.</note>. Next, surrounding this one, is the circle called "Winter" <note>original: *brumalis*; this refers to the Tropic of Capricorn, which marks the winter solstice in the northern hemisphere.</note>. It marks the final limits of the shining sun’s light and provides the gifts of its rays with a slanted flame on an inaccessible path. It gives the shortest day to us, but in those lands over which it hangs, the time stands still with long light; the day hardly passes, stretched out through the glowing heat <note>The author correctly observes that when it is winter in the north, the southern regions experience their longest, hottest days.</note>. This circle lies removed by two parts of the sphere.

One remains after these, closest to the extreme pole: the Southern Circle, which binds and hems in the southern constellations <note>original: *arctos*; while this usually means the "Bears" of the north, the poet uses it here to refer to the stars surrounding the southern pole.</note>. This one also leaves the winter circle by five parts, and this fourth circuit is as far from our pole <note>original: *vertice*; literally "the top" or "summit," referring to the celestial pole or zenith.</note> as the one closest to the opposite pole is from its own. If indeed the pole is divided from the opposite pole by a double sum of parts, it surrounds the high heavens <note>original: *olympu*; a poetic term for the sky or the celestial vault.</note> and marks the boundaries that signal the seasons through five divisions. These circles have the same path as the world and rotate together; they match their tilted and shared risings with their settings.

Whatever paths the other circles draw as they accompany the sky—by which the whole world is turned in a sixth circuit—they always preserve their intervals with an equal boundary and maintain the divisions and the destiny once allotted to them. There are also two circles <note>These are the "Colures," the two great circles that intersect at the poles and pass through the equinoctial and solstitial points.</note> which the world receives, drawn from each pole; they are opposite each other and cut through all the previously mentioned circles and the six others, meeting at the twin poles of the world <note>original: *cardine mundi*; the "hinges" or axis of the universe around which the heavens revolve.</note>.

<summary>This page completes the description of the five parallel circles of the celestial sphere (Arctic, Summer Tropic, Equator, Winter Tropic, and Antarctic) and introduces the two colures that intersect at the poles. It emphasizes the mathematical symmetry and eternal stability of the heavens.</summary>

<keywords>Tropic of Capricorn, Antarctic circle, celestial poles, colures, solstice, celestial mechanics, Avienus, Aratea</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the celestial sphere from the previous page. Having finished describing the horizontal circles (the Tropics and the Arctic/Antarctic circles), the poet now describes the vertical circles that intersect at the poles and the divine order governing the cosmos.</meta>

And across the pole they are led in a straight line to the axis <note>The poet is describing the "colures," two great circles that intersect at the celestial poles and divide the zodiac into four equal parts, marking the equinoxes and solstices.</note>, marking the seasons of the year and the stars of heaven, divided into four parts with equal months. Another boundary, running down from high heaven <note>original: *olympo*; here used as a poetic term for the celestial sphere.</note>, cuts through the tail of the Serpent and the dry Bears <note>The "Bears" (Ursa Major and Minor) are called "dry" because, in the northern latitudes of the Mediterranean, they are "circumpolar" and never set beneath the horizon into the sea.</note>, and the yoke of the Claws <note>original: *chelarum*; the "Claws of the Scorpion" was the ancient name for the constellation we now call Libra, the Scales.</note> flying in the middle of the circle. But these signs complete their measured times; this is not the work of chance, but the order of a great divinity. These stars, therefore, weave their path with an equal course, through which they adorn the heavens with various patterns <note>original: *laqueantia*; literally "fretting" or "paneling," comparing the star-strewn sky to a decorative coffered ceiling in a grand Roman house.</note>. Nothing is higher than this; these are the summits of the world. The public house of Nature is held contained within these boundaries, embracing the sea and the lands lying below. All things rise and fall in a harmonious course, where the sky once sank and then rises again.

---
### On the appearance and the breadth of the universe and the signs
---

Behold then how much space the world occupies within the embrace of heaven, and within what boundaries the twelve stars <note>The twelve signs of the Zodiac.</note> are carried. Reason teaches us this, for no barriers nor immense masses can resist it, and even the most hidden places yield. Everything submits; even heaven itself is open to the mind <note>original: *penetrabile*; Manilius argues that the human mind, guided by reason, can "penetrate" or reach the secrets of the heavens.</note>. For as far as the signs of the zodiac are distant from the lands and the sea, they provide twice that measure; and where the sphere is cut through the middle, a third part of the circuit is produced, measuring the solid total with only a small difference <note>The poet begins a section on celestial geometry, attempting to calculate the proportions of the sphere and the width of the zodiacal belt.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the description of the celestial colures and transitions into a philosophical praise of human reason's ability to measure the vastness of the cosmos and the zodiac.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, colures, Libra, Ursa Major, celestial geometry, Stoicism, Zodiac, divine order</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the celestial sphere. Having established the general order of the cosmos, the poet now moves into specific measurements of the sky and the description of the "colures"—the great circles that cross the poles and divide the heavens.</meta>

Therefore, the highest part of heaven is four signs distant from the lowest, so that the other third part consists of twelve signs <note>The poet is describing the geometric division of the 360-degree celestial sphere. In ancient astronomy, the zodiac is divided into twelve "signs" of 30 degrees each.</note>. And if the earth is suspended in the middle of the deep, it is separated by two signs from the top and the bottom <note>This passage refers to the mathematical proportions of the sphere; "two signs" (60 degrees) or "four signs" (120 degrees) describe the distances between the various celestial circles relative to the observer on Earth.</note>. Hence, whatever you yourself look up at above you—wherever your eyes travel through the empty air or wherever they reach their limit—it must be measured as two signs, which is a sixth part of the round world. 

The zones through which the signs of the zodiac are carried form a circle, weaving through the heavens with twelve signs in equal space. Do not be surprised that the wandering planets move more quickly through the stars, and that the affairs of great fate are mixed together by their judgment; for while each sign holds so much space, they are carried along by such vast time that six constellations rise with every full period of light <note>Because exactly half of the zodiac—six signs—is always above the horizon at any given time.</note>. 

It remains for me to try to show you the boundaries of the heavens and the threads <note>The poet uses "threads" (*fila*) as a metaphor for the imaginary lines or circles—specifically the "colures"—that astronomers use to map the sky.</note> that mark the sky in an orderly sequence, by which the flaming path of the zodiac is guided. One circle, beginning from the highest peak where the world is born, passes through, seeking Arctophylax <note>Arctophylax is another name for the constellation Boötes, the "Guardian of the Bear."</note> across the back of the Dragon. It cuts through the tail of the Hydra and, passing beneath the stars in the middle, it meets the Centaur on the opposite side; then it returns to the axis and comes back to the Sea-Monster <note>original: *coetum*; referring to the constellation Cetus.</note>. It marks the scaly back of the Sea-Monster, the borders of the Ram <note>original: *lanigeri*; literally "the wool-bearer," a poetic name for the constellation Aries.</note>, and the bright Triangle. It beholds the lowest reaches and the peaks of Andromeda, and finally closes its own beginning when the pole is reached again. Another circle leans upon this middle and highest axis...

And it passes through the lowest feet and the neck of the Bear <note>Ursa Major</note>, those seven stars which, once the sun has retreated, lead out the first lights for the black night. It both divides the twin-born <term>Cancer</term> <note>The constellation of the Crab</note> and grazes the burning Dog <note>Canis Major, containing the bright star Sirius</note> at its mouth, and the tiller of the Ship <note>The constellation Argo Navis</note> which had once conquered the sea. From there, it touches the hidden axis <note>The South Pole, which remained "hidden" below the horizon for ancient Mediterranean observers</note> through the signs of the previous circuit, crossing over and reaching again from another boundary. It marks you, <term>Capricorn</term>, and the Eagle <note>Aquila</note> by your stars; and running through the unseen Lyre <note>Lyra</note> and the coils of the Dragon <note>Draco</note>, it passes the rear stars of the feet of <term>Cynosura</term> <note>The Little Bear, Ursa Minor</note> and cuts across the tail near the neighboring pole. Here it meets itself again, mindful of the place from which it set out <note>This describes a complete 360-degree circuit of a celestial circle</note>.

## CONCERNING THE CIRCLE OF THE HORIZON

And for this circle, the ages have fixed an eternal seat in unchanging ways through the signs, in a perennial station. They made these two circles swift; for the one, rising from <term>Helice</term> <note>The Great Bear, Ursa Major</note> herself, will reach the middle of <term>Olympus</term> <note>A poetic term for the sky or the residence of the gods</note>; it distinguishes the day and weighs the sixth hour <note>This describes the function of the Meridian, which determines midday or "the sixth hour" in the Roman system of twelve daylight hours</note>, and it reaches the settings and the risings with equal distances. From here, changing its turns through the signs—whether someone seeks the dawn or the <term>Hesperian</term> <note>Western</note> lands—the circle arcs above him, standing over the crown of his head and cutting the sky in the middle, marking the world with divided light. Where there is a different "middle" for different people, the hour flies through the circle, and as the location on the earth changes, so too do the sky and the times <note>The author is explaining that unlike the fixed celestial equator or zodiac, the horizon and meridian change depending on where a person is standing on Earth</note>.

<summary>This page describes the path of a celestial colure through various constellations and introduces the concept of the Horizon and Meridian, explaining how they divide the sky based on the observer's geographic location.</summary>

But where Phoebus <note>A poetic name for the Sun, original: "phoebus"</note> first raises himself from the waves
<margin>at the waves</margin>
the line remains cut where the golden disc then presses.

and drawing its name from the Greek, it is called the Horizon <term>orizon</term> <note>original: "orizon," from the Greek word for "limiting" or "bounding"</note>.

Among these are cycles <term>gyros</term> <note>original: "gyros"; here referring to the orbital paths or celestial circles</note> whose signs are shining.

<meta>This page continues the Roman poet Manilius’s technical and poetic description of the celestial circles. From there it winds its curves through the legs and feet
of the second Centaur, and again ascending the sky
it begins to pass through the ship <note>The constellation Argo Navis</note> by its highest stern-ornaments <note>Original: "aplustria"; the curved decorative fins at the rear of an ancient ship.</note>.
Through the middle circle of the world and through the lowest point of the Twins <note>The constellation Gemini</note>,

Nor will it need to be sought out; it meets the sight

For a gleaming track shines in the blue world,
as if about to suddenly bring the day and opening up the heavens,
and just as a path distinguishes green fields,
worn by a wheel-track renewing it by its constant passage.
Between the divided parts, the way is level,
just as the seas turn white when the keel draws a furrow,
and the waves receive a path in foaming surges,
which the twisting vortex has stirred from the upturned depths.
So does a white boundary shine in the dark heavens <term>Olympus</term> <note>Original: "olympo"; here used poetically to mean the sky or the dwelling of the gods.</note>,
cleaving the blue world with a vast light.
And as the rainbow <term>Iris</term> <note>Original: "iris"; the goddess of the rainbow and the phenomenon itself.</note> circles its arcs through the clouds,
so this white boundary rests above with its marked summit,
and makes the faces of mortals tilt back toward the sky
while new lights shimmer through the dark night.

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s exploration of the Milky Way. Having described its visible path across the constellations on the previous page, the poet now investigates various scientific and mythological theories to explain its origin: is it a physical crack in the universe, or a remnant of a cosmic disaster?</meta>

And they search for sacred causes within the human breast:
Does the great mass of the world attempt to dissolve itself,
as its atoms <term>seminibus</term> <note>Original: "seminibus"; literally "seeds," used here by Manilius to refer to the fundamental particles or constituent elements of the universe.</note> are pulled apart?
Does the hull <note>Manilius continues the metaphor of the universe as a ship, a common Stoic and poetic image.</note> fail with its structure weakened,
admitting a new light through the loosened gaps?
What is there that one would not fear, when one beholds
the wounds of the great universe <term>mundi</term> <note>Original: "mundi"; refers to the entire ordered cosmos.</note>
and the jagged injury to the heavens <note>Original: "ferrata... iniuria caeli"; literally "iron-clad injury," suggesting a harsh, metallic-looking scar or break in the sky’s surface.</note> before one’s eyes?
Or does the world meet here, where the two ends
of the celestial caverns come together,
touching the edges of the sky and the stars?
Does a visible scar <note>Original: "manifesta cicatrix"; a vivid anatomical metaphor describing the Milky Way as a healed wound where the two halves of the sky were fused together.</note> join them through these very bonds,
making a fissure in the world, even as the sphere is packed tight?
Or has the bright structure even turned into a cloud,
forcing the foundations of the high heaven into wedges?

Or does that faith remain better through the ancient ages:
that the horses of the Sun once went that way with their divided chariot,
and wore down another path through a long age?
The scorched dwellings and the stars baked by flames
changed their dark blue appearance, their color being overturned,
and ash was poured into the place, burying that part of the world.
The story is famous, and has come down to us from ancient years,
of Phaethon flying through the signs <note>The constellations of the Zodiac.</note> in his father's chariot.
While the boy marveled at the new sights of the world up close,
and played in the heavens; proud in the chariot,
he ran wild through the universe. He desired more than his father,
leaving the paths shown to him, and while he ruled the orb,
he imposed himself upon the pole <note>The celestial axis.</note> and could not master the unfamiliar signs.

<summary>Manilius presents various theories for the Milky Way's existence, ranging from a physical crack in the structure of the cosmos to the mythological scorched path left by Phaethon’s disastrous attempt to drive the Sun's chariot.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the Roman poet Manilius’s catalog of theories regarding the Milky Way. Following the suggestion that it might be a celestial crack or a seam in the fabric of the universe, he now turns to the myth of Phaethon’s disastrous ride and the legend of the goddess Juno’s milk, before shifting toward more philosophical and scientific explanations.</meta>

By the wandering whim of the flames and the runaway chariot,
turned aside from its usual course with its team of four.
But we complain that flames raged through the whole world,
and that a funeral pyre of lands burned through all the cities,
when the wandering lights of the scattered chariot flowed out
and the heaven was scorched. From this, the world itself caught fire,
and new lights flared up from the neighboring flames,
<margin>or "retaining"</margin> preserving the appearance of this past disaster.
Nor should I hide the well-known antiquity of the story:
that a softer light flowed from the snowy liquid of milk
from the breast of the Queen of the Gods <note>The "Queen of the Gods" refers to Juno (the Greek Hera). Legend says that while nursing the infant Hercules, some of her milk splashed across the sky, creating the Milky Way.</note> and stained the sky
with its own color; for which reason it is called the Milky Circle <note>Original: "lacteus orbis"; the Roman term for what we call the Milky Way galaxy.</note>
and the name descends from the cause itself.

Or does a greater crowd of stars, in a dense crown,
weave their flames together and glow with a thick light,
and the circle shines brighter with their combined brilliance? <note>This "scientific" theory—that the Milky Way is actually composed of many tiny, distant stars—is remarkably close to our modern understanding of the galaxy.</note>
Or do brave souls, and names deemed worthy of heaven,
released from their bodies and released from the earth,
migrate here from the world and, reaching the sky they longed for,
live through ethereal years and enjoy the universe?
And here we venerate the descendants of Aeacus <term>Aeacidas</term> <note>Original: "aeacidas"; refers to the lineage of Achilles and Ajax, heroes of the Trojan War.</note>, and here the sons of Atreus <term>Atridas</term> <note>Original: "atridas"; refers to Agamemnon and Menelaus, the leaders of the Greek expedition against Troy.</note>,
and the fierce son of Tydeus <term>Tydiden</term> <note>Original: "Tydiden"; the hero Diomedes.</note>, and the man of Pylos <term>Pylium</term> <note>Original: "pylium"; refers to Nestor, the oldest of the Greek leaders, famous for having lived through three generations.</note>,
distinguished by his triple old age and his triumphs of land and sea,
the nature of a victor; and the kings of the Danaans at the citadel of Troy,
the camps of the leaders, and Troy conquered beneath the feet of Hector— <note>Manilius suggests the Milky Way is a "Hero's Heaven," where the great figures of the Trojan War now reside as stars.</note>

<summary>Manilius explores the Milky Way's origins, ranging from the myth of Phaethon's scorched path and Juno’s spilled milk to the scientific theory of clustered stars and the philosophical idea of it being a dwelling place for the souls of heroes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the "fixed" stars and the Milky Way (where the souls of Roman heroes dwell) to the transient and unpredictable phenomena of the upper atmosphere. Having just mentioned the planets, the author now turns his attention to the nature, origin, and varied appearances of comets.</meta>

### The Nature and Forms of Comets

Or perhaps, as the earth breathes out a massive vapor, a moisture-filled <term>spirit|Latin: *spiritus*; in ancient science, this refers to a gaseous exhalation or "breath" of the earth.</term> is overtaken by a dry breeze. When the clouds, driven away by a long period of clear weather, cease to be, and the parched air catches fire from the sun's rays, the fire—once released—seizes upon suitable fuel, and the flame finds material capable of sustaining itself. And because it is not a solid body, but rather scattered, wandering principles of the breezes, very much like fleeting smoke, it lives only for a brief task; the fires stop as soon as they have begun, and they fall just as the comet shines forth. 

<note>Manilius is explaining the "exhalation theory," popularized by Aristotle, which argued that comets were not stars, but actually dry gases from the earth that caught fire in the upper atmosphere.</note>

For unless these comets occurred so near to the sun's setting, and if the time for these kindled flames were not so short, there would be a second day in the midst of the night; the immense sky would return and catch the whole world while it was deep in sleep. 

Furthermore, because not every blaze is arranged in a single form, and the vapor of the earth is caught by fire in different ways, these lights are carried along, set ablaze in various shapes, appearing suddenly out of the darkness. For now, as if long hair flowed from a head, the flame flies in imitation of locks <note>original: *comas*; the word "comet" comes from the Greek *kometes*, meaning "long-haired."</note>, and with thin tresses, it unfolds its fires, spread out by burning rays. This first appearance emerges with scattered hair, and a ball of fire follows in the likeness of a beard <note>original: *barba*; ancient astronomers categorized comets by their shapes, such as "bearded" comets.</note>. Sometimes, drawn along by an equal joining of its sides, it forms either a square <term>beam|Latin: *trabs*; a comet that appears long and rectangular.</term> or a rounded <term>column|Latin: *columna*; a comet that appears as a vertical pillar of light.</term>.

<summary>This page discusses the scientific origins of comets, attributing them to earthly vapors ignited by the sun, and describes their various visual forms such as hair, beards, and pillars.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, comets, atmospheric phenomena, exhalation theory, celestial omens, ancient meteorology</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of various comet types based on their visual appearances (the "Pitheus" or jar-shaped, the "Aristatus" or bearded, etc.) and transitions into a broader scientific discussion on the omnipresence of fire in the natural world—from lightning to friction in trees.</meta>

### The Various Shapes of Comets

...unfolds its hair. Sometimes Nature even matches swollen jars <note>original: *dolia*; in ancient meteorology, these were "Pithei," comets that appeared short and thick like storage vats.</note> with fires that seem to bulge like a distended womb. At other times, it feigns small hairs with tiny points of light gathered into spheres; from these, it shapes the likeness of harvest-fields <note>original: *messes*; likely referring to "bearded" comets whose rays look like stalks of grain.</note> under a trembling light. It pours forth branch-like fires into fixed torches, and "shooting stars" are seen flying everywhere when wandering lights sparkle through the clear world and thin fires are tossed with long tresses. They burn far off, imitating swift arrows, when a dry path is narrowed to a slender thread.

### The Omnipresence of Fire

There are also fires mixed into all things. These are the fires that manufacture the thunderbolts within the heavy clouds of winter, penetrate the depths of the earth, and imitate the volcanic fires of Etna in the heights of heaven. They make the water run hot in the very springs and find a dwelling place within hard flint and even in green bark—which is why the forest burns when its branches are rubbed together.

Nature is so abundant in these elements that you should not wonder when sudden torches burst from the sky and the kindled air shines with flashing flames, embracing the dry seeds breathed out by the earth. These seeds can generate swift-moving fires into comets. This happens whether Nature created those torches as attendants for the stars that shine in heaven with thin flames, or whether the <term>Titanic heat|referring to the Sun, often called "Titan" by Roman poets</term> draws them toward itself and wraps the flaming comets in its own fire. 

<margin>o-</margin>There, the feeding fire follows its course, pursuing what it puts to flight; just as you see lightning vibrating with trembling light, and thunderbolts bursting toward the sky even in the midst of rainstorms.

<summary>Manilius describes the diverse visual forms of comets—likening them to jars, harvests, and arrows—before explaining that fire is a universal element found in lightning, volcanoes, and even friction between trees.</summary>
<keywords>comets, meteorology, types of comets, Aristotle's theory, elemental fire, lightning, Mount Etna, friction, solar heat</keywords>

### The Celestial Omens

> A nd now it departs, just as the sphere of the Cyllenian <note>original: *cyllenius orbis*; a reference to the planet Mercury, born on Mount Cyllene.</note>

> A s burning pyres blaze without end, while the world and Nature

## THE FIRST BOOK OF M. MANILIUS’S ASTRONOMY <term>Astronomicon: The title of Manilius's didactic poem on astrology and the mechanics of the universe.</term> ENDS.
## THE SECOND BOOK OF THE SAME BEGINS.

The greatest poet <note>The author refers here to Homer, the legendary author of the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey*.</note> of the Trojan race sang of the struggles
Of fifty kings, and of the king who was also a father <note>Priam, the King of Troy, who was said to have fifty sons.</note>,
And the torch of Hector, and Troy safe while Hector lived,
And the wandering of the leaders, which he stretched across as many years <note>Refers to the ten-year journey of Odysseus in the *Odyssey*.</note>,
Pressing on in war with doubled ranks upon the sea,
And the final arms in the fatherland and the captured household gods;
He sang with a sacred mouth: and from his overflowing lips,
All posterity has drawn the waters for their own songs,
And has dared to lead streams into a slender river,
Fertile from the riches of one man. But next to him,
Hesiod <note>An early Greek poet whose *Theogony* describes the origins of the gods and the world.</note> commemorates the gods and the parents of the gods,
And Chaos brought forth, and the lands, and the world beneath it,
The infant world, and the stars as the first staggering body,
And the aged Titans, and the cradle of great Jove <note>Jupiter (Zeus), who was hidden as an infant in a cave to prevent his father Saturn from swallowing him.</note>,
And the name of "man" under a brother, and a father without a brother <note>Refers to the complex and often asexual primordial genealogies found in Hesiod's creation myths.</note>.
