--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the Twelve-Parts (Duodecatemoria) of the zodiac. While the previous page established the 2.5-degree division of each sign, this text focuses on how to assign these segments to the planets (the "five" stars) and concludes with a poetic justification for teaching these complex rules step-by-step, comparing the study of astrology to a child learning to read.</meta>

<page-num>34v</page-num>

Let it be placed in two portions with an added part;
Let a half be assigned to the remaining signs in order,
In whichever sign it stops, the Moon will see you are correct.
The Twelve-Part <term>Duodecatemorion: A sub-division of a zodiacal sign</term> signs are led after the rest;
In their own order, the stars thus remain located.
Lest this logic <note>original: *ratio*; referring to the underlying mathematical principle of the universe</note> also fail you, perceive it in a few words:
It is greater in its effect than it is small in its parts themselves.
What is this that is said to be a Twelve-Part?
For that Twelve-Part is marked by five
Parts, for throughout the whole sky the stars shine forth,
Which are called "wandering" <note>original: *vaga*; the planets, from the Greek *planētēs* meaning "wanderer"</note> and lead their individual lots;
And they take up half-strengths and rights within them.
In whichever of the five stars <note>The five planets known to the ancients: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury</note>, therefore, a planet is located,
It will be fitting to look at which Twelve-Part they were in;
For in whichever sign those stars
Have fallen, it will produce effects in the minds of that person.
Everywhere the logic <note>original: *ratio*</note> through which all things consist must be mixed together,
But you shall render all these things later in their own proper order.
It is enough to have taught this through these unfamiliar practices,
So that when confidence stands firm in the parts already taught,
Then the whole body of work may be noted by an easy logic;
And let the song <note>original: *carmen*; Manilius wrote his astronomical treatise in verse</note> of the whole come well after the individual parts.
Just as a letter is first shown to unrefined boys,
Then by its appearance and its name, usage is composed,
And through their own feet <note>A pun on poetic "feet" (meter) and physical steps</note>, budding poems arise.

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical calculations of the "Twelve-Parts" (Duodecatemoria) to a philosophical defense of the poet’s methodical approach. The author, Manilius, argues that complex systems must be taught from their foundations upward, using an extended metaphor of building a city to explain why he must first cover basic "raw materials" before moving to advanced astrological predictions.</meta>

<page-num>35r</page-num>

It is beneficial to have learned each of the previous steps in full,
For unless these have first been established upon fundamental elements,
Whatever instructions teachers may have given hurriedly in verse,
The disordered sequence of things would flow away into emptiness.
So for me, as I fly through the entire universe in song,
And for the fates unearthed from the deepest gloom,
I must strive to sing in measured tones.
While my mind recalls the divine power <note>original: *numen*; the presiding will or divinity of the cosmos</note> by which God reigns,
Faith must be led through the individual parts, and each thing
Must be delivered in its proper stages. When all things stand firm
In the knowledge of certain facts, they may then be called back to their own uses.
Just as cities rise upon bare mountains,
And a founder intends to encircle empty hills with walls:
Before his hand attempts to dig the trenches,
The labor begins. Behold! The forest falls, and ancient groves
Are leveled; they see a new sun and new stars <note>By clearing the dense "forest" of ignorance or physical trees, the light of the sun and the stars becomes visible to the builder for the first time</note>.
Every kind of bird and beast is driven from the place;
They abandon their ancient homes and familiar dens.
But the builders search for flint for the walls and marble
For the temple, and the strength of iron through long-known methods.
From this, the arts are sought; from this, every practical use comes together.
Then the divine work <note>original: *divinum opus*; Manilius compares the construction of a temple-city to the construction of his own astronomical poem</note> rises when all materials are at hand,
Lest a misplaced concern should break the progress midway.
So it is for me as I take up the burden of so great a structure:
First the raw material of things, separated by logic <note>original: *ratio*; the underlying rational system of the universe</note>...

<summary>Manilius justifies his step-by-step teaching method by comparing the study of astrology to the building of a city, which requires clearing land and gathering materials before the grand structures can rise. He emphasizes that "divine work" requires a solid foundation in basic principles to avoid failure.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, educational theory, city-building metaphor, foundations, cosmology, divine work, logic</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s didactic poem on astrology. Having established that one must learn the foundations first (using the city-building metaphor on the previous page), he now introduces the four "Cardines" or "pivots"—known today in astrology as the Angles. These points (the Ascendant, Descendant, Midheaven, and Lower Heaven) form the structural framework of the celestial sphere and the basis for all fate-based predictions.</meta>

<page-num>35v</page-num>

The method must be delivered, lest it later become void, or
Lest the arguments, coming to birth, be dazed by the novelty of things.

->On the pivots of the world<-

Come then, prepare a keen mind to learn
About the pivots <note>original: *cardines*; literally "hinges" or "pivots." In astrology, these are the four crucial points of the sky: the Ascendant, Descendant, Midheaven, and Imum Coeli</note>, which are four in total throughout the world,
Arranged forever, catching and turning the flying signs.
One is from the rising of the sky, at the birth of the east,
Where the earth first perceives the sky at the level horizon.
Another, corresponding from the opposite edge of the ether,
From where the world flees and is stretched headlong toward the underworld <note>original: *tartara*; the Greek/Roman underworld, here used to describe the region below the western horizon where stars appear to "fall" out of sight</note>.
The third marks the summit of the high heaven,
Where Phoebus <note>A common name for the sun god, Apollo</note>, weary, halts with his panting horses,
And turns the day downward, balancing the midday waters.
The fourth, noble for its established station, holds the depths,
In which there is the beginning of the return and the end of the descent
For the stars; it perceives both settings and risings alike.
These places report through the art <note>The "art" of astrology</note> special powers and the greatest
Effects of the Fates, because the entire
Ethereal sphere rests upon them as if on joints.
Unless they, with perpetual and alternating lot, should catch the world
As it flies on its courses and bind it in double chains
Across the sides and through the bottom and the top,
The machinery would flow apart, dissociated, with the world dissolved.
But nevertheless, power is divided within each pivot,
And they vary according to the strength of their place and differ in their order.

<vocab>Manilius, Astronomica, cardinibus mundi, Phoebus, fatum, cosmographia</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the four "Cardines" (pivots) of the cosmos—the Eastern, Western, Southern (Midheaven), and Northern (Lowest) points—which Manilius describes as the structural "hinges" or "chains" that hold the universe together. He argues that these points possess the greatest influence over human Fate and are the primary focus of astrological study.</summary>

<keywords>cardinal points, angles of the chart, Phoebus, Ascendant, Midheaven, cosmic structure, celestial mechanics, Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the four "Cardines" or pivots of the zodiac, which were introduced on the previous page. Having established their locations, the author now explains the specific influences and "spheres of life" associated with the Midheaven (highest point), the Imum Coeli (lowest point), and the Ascendant (eastern point).</meta>

<page-num>36r</page-num>

The first shall be that which reigns at the summit of the high heaven
And seeks the center of the world along a narrow boundary;
A throne occupies this exalted seat with high favor.
This guardian provides enough dignity and the highest peaks of success,
And whatever claims prominence for itself; and every power
Brings and bestows various honors for reigning.
From here comes favor and hope and all the grace of the common people:
To deliver justice in the forum, to settle the world with laws,
To join foreign nations together by one's treaties,
And to exalt the name of each man according to his lot.

The next is that located in the lowest station <note>The *Imum Coeli* or "Bottom of the Sky," the point directly opposite the Midheaven</note>,
Which soon supports the world of the other with its radiant beams;
It is lesser in its effect upon one's hopes, but greater in practical use.
It holds the foundations of the commonwealth <note>original: *reip[ublicae]*; the public affairs or the state</note> and governs wealth <note>original: *census*; the registration of property and social standing</note>.
Through it, men examine how certain their prayers are by means of mined metals <note>referring to mining and the discovery of hidden treasures or minerals</note>
And how much you are able to attain from that which is hidden.

And the third, raising its shining part toward the first,
Is that which holds the rising where the stars first emerge;
A single day gave it birth, and it divides time into hours.
From here the horoscope <term>Horoscopus: The "Ascendant," or the specific degree of the zodiac rising over the eastern horizon at the moment of birth. It was considered the most vital point for determining character.</term> brings forth its welcome births;
It does not take the last place, for it rejoices in its own name.
This point holds the judgment of life, and here is the rule of character;
It will give fortune to affairs and the guidance of the lot,
And the first moments of time which receive those being born—
What upbringing they might receive, and under what station they are created.

<summary>This page defines the astrological influence of three of the four "pivots": the Midheaven governs public honors and law, the Lower Heaven governs foundations and hidden wealth, and the Ascendant (Horoscope) governs character and the conditions of birth.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, Midheaven, Ascendant, Horoscopus, Imum Coeli, fate, social status, Roman law, mining, character, birth</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the four "Cardines" or pivots of the zodiac. Having discussed the Midheaven, the Imum Coeli, and the Ascendant on the previous page, the author now describes the fourth pivot—the Descendant—and explains how the four quadrants of the sky represent the four stages of human life.</meta>

<page-num>36v</page-num>

Both stars <note>referring to the stars at the horizon points</note> subsist with their powers intermingled.
The last pivot, which hides the stars once the world has been traversed
And, holding the Sunset, looks down upon the submerged world,
Reaches the very summit of affairs and the end of labors.
It joins together banquets and the final seasons of life,
As well as leisure, the gatherings of men, and the worship of the gods.

**The division of age into the parts of heaven.**

You will be satisfied with these instructions for each pivot;
However, the intervals between them must also be noted with a mindful heart,
As they yield their own specific powers when measured across a larger space.
Whatever part curves from the Rising point <note>the Ascendant</note> toward the highest summit
Claims the years of our first age and our birth.
That which is pressed downward, sloping from the world’s highest peak
Until it reaches the Sunset, follows the years of childhood;
It succeeds them and governs tender youth under its station.
The part which descends between the Sunset and the lowest point of the sphere
Rules the times of mature life,
And it sows its own fruits, worked through a varied course.
And that which, as the course returns from the bottom,
Slowly ascends the tilted arc with fading strength,
Finally completes the late years,
The waning day of life, and trembling old age.
Every sign, under whatever figure it is formed,
Is assigned its place by these parts of the world, which impart their nature to the stars,
Creating both blessings and harms as they come into the circle.

<vocab>astronomy, Manilius, Astronomica, stars, heaven, ages of life, zodiac</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the fourth astrological pivot (the Descendant/Sunset) as the governor of life's end and social gatherings, and then outlines how the four quadrants of the sky correspond to the four stages of human life from infancy to old age.</summary>

<keywords>Descendant, Sunset, ages of man, quadrants, astrological houses, maturity, old age, Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius's discussion of the "cardines" (pivots) of the zodiac. Having established the four main points of the sky—the Ascendant, Midheaven, Descendant, and Lower Midheaven—the author now explains how the spaces between these pivots (the "houses" of the zodiac) exert their own influence, focusing on the "cadent" or falling regions which he associates with misfortune and the mythological struggle of the giants against the gods.</meta>

<page-num>376</page-num>

Each part receives its own powers and returns them to the heavens. For Nature prevails; she administers the species and the laws within their proper boundaries, and she compels all things passing through to adopt her own character. At one moment, a region is rich with varied honors; at another, the places are sterile, and the stars within them must endure the penalty of their station.

That region which sits above the Rising point <note>original: "exortum"; this refers to the Twelfth House, located just above the eastern horizon or Ascendant</note> from the highest heaven is an unhappy district, hostile to future success and all too fruitful in vice. Nor is it alone in this; equal to it is that seat which shines with an opposing star, joined just below the Sunset <note>original: "occasu"; this refers to the Sixth House, located just below the western horizon or Descendant</note>. Lest it stand prominently at a pivot of the world, each is said to be cast down in a ruin of the past. 

For mortals, there must be a climbing and a falling. The region above the Sunset <note>the Eleventh House</note> is better, as is the one below the Rising <note>the Second House</note>. Here, the lot of the world is driven headlong: one hangs from above and fears an approaching end from the neighboring pivot, or, having once been firmly established, it shall fall. 

Rightly do these places hold the name of Typhon <note>A monstrous giant in Greek mythology who attempted to overthrow Zeus; he represents disorder and is often associated with volcanic forces</note>, a horrendous seat which the cruel Earth poured forth when she gave birth to war against heaven. Nor did her offspring emerge smaller than their mother; but, driven back into the deep by the lightning-bolt, the falling mountains returned upon them. The war ended, and the defeated Typhon yielded to his tomb; the Earth itself now trembles as he moans beneath flaming Mount Etna. He reaches for the summits of the shining heaven, so close that he might not yield to the very stars, seeking the palm of victory for his defeated race.

<summary>Manilius describes the "cadent" houses of the zodiac (the 12th and 6th houses), characterizing them as unlucky regions that "fall" away from the pivots. He uses the myth of the giant Typhon, buried under Mount Etna, as an allegory for these declining astrological positions.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, Manilius, Typhon, Mount Etna, zodiac houses, cardines, celestial pivots, Greek mythology, Ascendant, Descendant</keywords>

It is this seat that calls and hides Phoebus, receives him and returns him,
and consumes the day; these things are to be noted by such a law.

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical conclusion of Book 2 of Manilius’s *Astronomica*, which focuses on the "houses" or "temples" of the zodiac, into the poetic opening of Book 3. The author concludes his technical summary of celestial influence and begins the next stage of his work with a traditional invocation of the Muses, distinguishing his scientific subject matter from the well-worn paths of Greek mythology.</meta>

<page-num>39r</page-num>

For you there are the powers of Time, through which the entire 
sequence of stars flies past; Time leads them and lends to them
its own laws. The stars, following a fixed order, 
traverse the various powers of the places as they define them.
They create influences, and in doing so, they take hold of foreign 
realms and occupy hospitable camps in external territories.
These matters are certain to me regarding the stars’ influence.
For now, it is enough to have marked the titles of the parts of heaven, 
the effects of each place on its own, and the gods 
who gave each part its name and established its stronghold. 
I have described the eight divisions <note>original: "Octo tropos"; referring to the various ways the celestial circle is divided</note> through which the flying stars are separated. 
The motions they return will follow in the proper order of things.

### THE SECOND BOOK OF M. MANILIUS’S ASTRONOMICA 
### ENDS. THE THIRD BOOK BY THE SAME AUTHOR BEGINS.

Lead me, O Muses <note>original: "pierides"; a name for the Muses, the daughters of Pierus</note>, as I rise toward new themes—
daring things greater than my strength, and unafraid 
to journey through inaccessible glades. Extend your boundaries; 
I am attempting to guide these songs into my poetry.
I will not sing of wars waged for the destruction of the newborn heavens, 
nor of thunderbolts and flames, nor of children buried within their mothers <note>This likely refers to the birth of Dionysus or the myths of the Titans</note>. 
I shall not tell of Hector being sold for his own ashes, or of Priam bringing the ransom <note>The poet is stating he will not write about the Trojan War, a common subject for other poets</note>.
Nor will I recount the woman of Colchis <note>term: Colchida; refers to the sorceress Medea, who helped Jason and the Argonauts</note> selling her father's kingdom, 
and the brother torn apart by her crime; nor the harvest of armed men <note>referring to the warriors who grew from the dragon's teeth sown by Jason</note>, 
the trials of the bulls, the flames, and the sleepless dragon,
nor of years restored to youth <note>Medea’s rejuvenation of Jason’s father, Aeson</note>, or the fires caused by the golden fleece.

<summary>This page concludes Book 2 of the Astronomica, summarizing how stars interact with specific celestial regions, and opens Book 3 with a poetic "recusatio"—a statement where the author rejects common mythological themes in favor of his scientific astronomical subject.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the proem (introduction) to Book 3 of Manilius’s *Astronomica*. Having invoked the Muses, the poet now explicitly rejects the standard themes of classical Greek and Roman epic poetry—such as the myths of Thebes, the Persian Wars, or even the historical rise of Rome—in favor of the more difficult and "pure" subject of celestial science and the laws of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>39v</page-num>

& and of offspring wrongly conceived and even more wrongly slain.
**N** I shall not sing of the long years of war at mighty Messene <note>referring to the Messenian Wars between Sparta and Messenia</note>,
**S** Nor of the seven leaders <note>The "Seven Against Thebes" from Greek tragedy</note> and the walls of Thebes destroyed by lightning and fire,
**M** The walls of Thebes <note>original: "thebaꝝ"; thebarum</note> and the city that was conquered even as it conquered.
**G** Nor shall I tell of brothers <note>Eteocles and Polynices, the sons of Oedipus</note> who were equally the grandsons of their own mother <note>Jocasta</note>,
**N** Nor of the banquets of children <note>the myth of Thyestes, who was unwittingly fed his own sons at a feast</note> and the stars that turned back in horror,
**E** And the day snatched away; nor do I recount ancient wars on the deep <note>original: "p̃fundo"; profundo</note>
**I** where the sea was hidden beneath a massive fleet <note>likely referring to the Persian fleet at Salamis</note>,
**A** And how the strait was lost to the land, as if it were the ocean's waves <note>referring to Xerxes’ bridge of ships over the Hellespont</note>.
**N** It is not of great kings I must sing; my themes require a greater space.
**E** Even if I were to speak of destiny and the origins of the Roman people,
**T** and so many leaders of the world, so many wars, and the peace found in every land,
**I** showing how the whole world yielded to the laws of a single people <note>Rome</note>.
**D** That task is set aside. It is easy to spread one's sails to favorable winds,
**F** and to water fertile soil through various arts,
**A** or to add beauty <note>original: "decuſ"; decus</note> to gold and ivory. When the raw
**M** material itself is already shining, it is easy to build a poem upon such splendid things.
**C** Writing such songs is a common task, a work already often undertaken.
**A** But for me, the path lies through bare places and the unknown names of things,
**T** dealing with the times and various chances and the turning points of the universe <note>original: "moñtaq mundi"; momenta que mundi</note>,
**Q** of which I must struggle with each change in its own specific part.
**L** How great an effort is required! How grand it is to know these things, and how grand to explain them!
**C** To join them in their proper song and with a fixed meter <note>original: "pedibus... certis"; literally "certain feet," referring to dactylic hexameter</note>.
**H** Be present here, whoever you are who pays attention to my ventures;
**A** lend your ear and your eyes, and perceive these true words.

<summary>In this opening to Book 3, Manilius distances himself from traditional mythological and historical epics, arguing that his scientific subject—though "bare" and difficult—is a more noble and original pursuit than retelling well-known legends.</summary>

<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, didactic poetry, Roman epic, Theban cycle, Persian Wars, poetic originality, celestial laws</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the prologue of Book 3 of Manilius’s Astronomica. Following the rejection of traditional epic themes like the Trojan War or the history of Rome, the poet now justifies his choice of a technical, scientific subject. He moves into the "Physika" or the physical construction of the universe, explaining how Nature organized the elements into a rational whole governed by fate.</meta>

<page-num>40r</page-num>

Do not apply your mind to sworn kings <note>the Greek leaders who swore an oath to Menelaus to retrieve Helen</note> and falling Troy,
Nor should you seek out sweet songs;
The subject itself refuses to be adorned, content only to be taught.
And if any names are cited in a foreign tongue <note>original: "externa lingua"; referring to the Greek technical terms used in astronomy that were difficult to fit into Latin poetry</note>,
This will be a requirement of the work, not a fault of the poet; not all things
Can be bent to meter, and they are better noted under their own specific names.
Come now, examine this supreme matter with subtle care,
So that, once shown, it may provide you with the chiefest benefits
And give certain paths in the art for seeing the fates <note>the "art" refers to astrology as a predictive science</note>,
If it is well-established and stored by an ever-watchful sense.

The Principle of things and Nature, the guardian of what is hidden,
When she was building such great masses through the walls of the world <note>original: "moenia mundi"; a phrase suggesting the boundaries of the universe</note>
And was enclosing the orb with stars poured around it,
With the world hanging from every side toward the center, she joined its divided limbs
Into one single body by fixed orders.
She commanded the air and lands, the flames and swimming water,
To provide mutual nourishment to one another forever,
So that harmony might rule so many battling causes,
And the world might stand bound by an eternal pact <note>original: "aeterno foedere"; the Stoic idea that the universe is held together by a rational, law-like contract</note>,
So that nothing should remain excluded from supreme reason,
And that which belonged to the world should be ruled by the world itself.
She hung the fates and lives of men from the stars,
Which grant the highest portions of wealth and the honor of light,
Which might grant fame that would fly, never wearying,
And which govern the origins through the middle part of the world...

<summary>Manilius argues that his scientific subject requires plain teaching rather than poetic embellishment and describes how Nature created a balanced, rational universe where human destiny is inextricably linked to the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, Didactic poetry, Cosmology, Stoicism, Fate, Natural Law, Elements</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Book 3 of Manilius's Astronomica. Having established that the universe is governed by a rational, divine pact, the poet now describes how the specific details of human life—occupations, ages, and fates—are mapped onto the celestial sphere. This section introduces the concept of the "Twelve Houses" or "Places," which allow the general influence of the zodiac to be applied to individual births.</meta>

<page-num>110v</page-num>

She <note>Nature</note> ensured that the Sun, the Moon, and the wandering stars <note>original: "uagasque stellas"; referring to the planets, which "wander" across the fixed backdrop of the constellations</note> occupy their appointed places, so that they might both conquer and be conquered in their turns. To these signs she gave dominion, hallowed their specific roles to each, and ordained that all things in their sum, gathered from every side, should be drawn into a single system by Reason <note>original: "ratio"; here meaning the underlying logic or mathematical order of the universe</note> to govern Fate.

For every kind of thing, every type of labor, every work or art—whatever accidents or lot could befall a human life—she embraced and divided into as many parts as she had established stars <note>here meaning the signs of the Zodiac</note>. She assigned specific matters and ages of life to their own turns, gave a name to each, and directed the entire census of human existence through the stars in a fixed order, so that one part of life always stands adjacent to its neighbor in the neighboring signs.

->She distributed the Hours of affairs among the individual signs<-

This was done not so that these "Hours" <note>Manilius uses "Hours" (Horae) to describe what later astrologers call "Houses"—twelve divisions of the sky that govern specific areas of life like wealth, health, or marriage</note> would remain forever fixed in one station of the heavens, drawing all human births into the same pattern from the same spots. Instead, the dwellings of those being born receive their own character based on the time of birth; the Lot <note>original: "fors"; likely referring to the "Lot of Fortune" or the specific starting point of a horoscope</note> migrates through the signs and approaches different stars for different people.

Thus, a birth-chart <note>original: "genitura"</note> takes on a new form through the stars, yet the system is not thrown into confusion by this combined motion. When the first "part of affairs"—which was established as the primary part—receives its own dwelling at the moment of birth, all the others follow in order and cling to the subsequent signs. The sequence follows its leader until the full circle of life returns upon itself.

<summary>Manilius explains how Nature mapped all human activities and life stages onto the twelve signs of the Zodiac. He introduces the "Houses" (which he calls Hours), explaining that while the order of life's events is fixed, their specific application to an individual depends on the exact time of birth.</summary>

<keywords>zodiac, houses, horoscopy, fate, nature, ratio, planetary motion, astrology, life cycles</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition from the previous section where the poet explains the "Twelve Houses" (or Places) of the zodiac. Having established that the universe is a rational system, Manilius now details which specific human activities—from farming and family to war and law—are governed by the first four of these celestial divisions.</meta>

<page-num>41r</page-num>

These appearances of things <margin>but: of nature</margin> are located through the signs,
In which the entire sum of **fortune** will be stored.
So that when the seven stars <note>The "seven stars" refers to the seven wandering bodies known to the ancients: the Sun, the Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> either harm or help,
Or when the divine power of the world moves them from the **cardinal points** <note>original: "cardinibus"; these are the four pivotal "hinges" of the sky: the rising point, the setting point, the highest point (Midheaven), and the lowest point.</note>,
Thus a happy or sad fate comes through each individual part.
Such is the lot to be expected for that particular affair.
All these things must be sung by me in a solemn order,
And marked with their own titles and the shapes of actual affairs,
So that the placement of tasks, their names, and their kinds may be clear.

The first lot is given to **Fortune**. This place, by their art,
They <note>the ancient astrologers</note> designate by this title, and whatever follows next within it:
The foundations of the **house** and all things clinging to the home,
What the limit is in one’s own fields <note>original: "aruis"; referring here to land ownership and agricultural boundaries.</note> and the agreement of the lands,
And how much is granted to build up great masses of wealth;
All this according to how the wandering stars of the shining heaven harmonize.

After this is the place of **military service**, where whatever occurs in the fields <note>original: "aruis"; though modern editors often suggest "armis" (arms) here, the transcription preserves the agricultural metaphor for the site of battle.</note>
And what happens to those traveling among foreign cities
Is accustomed to be received; it is all comprised under this one title.

The third station must be counted for **urban labors** <note>This refers to civil service, public life, and the management of a city’s affairs.</note>.
This too is a kind of "military service" carried out in civil acts,
Composed of laws and holding the enduring bonds of trust.
It forms friendships and shows if duties and devotion—
Which often fall in vain—attain their rewards and gratitude.
It teaches these things when the world harmonizes with the stars placed nearby.
For she <note>Nature</note> has located the work of **legal judgments** in the fourth place.

It emphasizes that these earthly fates are determined by how the planets interact with these specific "stations" in the sky.</summary>

And the fortune of the forum <note>The "forum" refers to the center of Roman public and legal life; this house governs success in law and public speaking.</note> pouring forth words in a rush,
And the defendant hanging on the tongue and the speaker's rostrum
Placed there, and revealing the hidden laws to the people,
And paying out his own efforts with a furrowed <note>original: "uirgis"; likely a transcription error for "rugis" (wrinkles), referring to the stressed brow of a lawyer or orator.</note> brow.

The house grants all this to one part,
And however the distant stars flow as they carry out their motions.

The fifth degree is dedicated through the signs to **marriage**;
It holds both partners and companions, and joins one with guests,
And binds similar friends together in a treaty.

In the sixth seat, a rich abundance flows,
And joined matters, of which one shows what uses
May happen for a short time, and the other what is long-lasting,
According to how the stars incline their powers and govern the seasons.

The seventh is judged to be dreadful with fierce dangers,
If the stars located through the signs are badly aligned.

Nobility holds the eighth, in which consists the condition of **honor**,
And reputation, manner, and lineage; and with a beautiful
Pretext, the ninth place occupies everything:
The doubtful lot of the Fates and former fears,
And all the mixed nature of the crowd entering in.

Neighboring this will be the house which touches the **action of life** <note>This refers to the 10th House or Midheaven, which in ancient astrology governs one's career, public life, and "actions."</note>,
In which the lineage of one's own people is found, and by what examples
The whole house is formed, and in what fixed <unclear>certain</unclear> order.

Let the slaves depart, arranged for their assigned duties.
A preeminent **eleventh part** is located in this lot,
Which always governs our peak and our powers,

Oppressed things move this house, as do the world and the stars;
In no other seat is the time or the type of healing found,

Help, and send forth the healthy juices of life.

The **last house**, concluding the whole sum in order,
Concerns the labor of finishing affairs, which falls to all;
The fulfillment of vows and what each person
Proposes for their own studies and arts, lest they be in vain—
Whether favor brings a charming nod of approval to all,

Of their effects. For now, lest mixed details

Since we have completed all the labors distributed
Throughout the whole circle, along with their names and powers.

**A**thla <term>Athla</term> <note>original: "Athla"; from the Greek *athloi*, meaning contests or labors. **I**n their natures, and they are held together, divided into six parts.

**P**erpetual seats they do not have, for these same
**S**tars of the newborn do not keep their place, but the time changes them.
**N**ow here, now there, the goal-post <note>original: "meta"; the turning post in a Roman chariot race, here used metaphorically for the shifting positions of the signs.</note> of the signs moves through the circle,
**I**ntact, however, the established order remains.
**E**rgo, come then, lest your birth-chart <note>original: "genitura"; the configuration of the heavens at the moment of birth.</note> deceive you with a false figure,

**F**ortune's <term>Fortune</term> <note>Referring to the "Lot of Fortune" (*Fortuna*), a mathematical point in a horoscope used to determine prosperity and fate.</note> place must be sought through all the stars,
**Q**uite the first part, as it is called by number beneath the stars.
**Q**uite so, when it has been discovered by this method,
**C**onjoin the rest in the order of the signs following after,
**C**onnecting them so that each may hold its own proper seat.

**I**nstead, take up this double method:
**C**um <gloss>When</gloss> the form of the heavens has been established for you,
**C**onstituted by the stars summoned to their signs at the prescribed time of birth:
**T**ransversing the sky, if Phoebus <note>A common name for the Sun god in Roman poetry.</note> travels higher than the pivot <term>pivot</term> <note>original: "cardine"; refers to the "hinges" of the sky—the Ascendant, Descendant, Midheaven, or Lower Midheaven. **Q**uite between where he rises and where he sinks into the waves,
**P**erfectly the light affirms the birth was by day.
**A**t night, if he shines hidden beneath the stars,
**I**nferior, with the pivots of the night <note>The cardinal points below the horizon.</note> holding the world
**C**onfined on the right and left, he was born during the hours of night.
**H**owever, when these have been noted by that distinction—

Then, if by chance the nurturing day has received the newborn child <note>original: "nascentem"; referring to the infant at the moment of birth.</note> with its light,
You shall count the degrees in order from the Sun to the Moon;
You shall then lead just as many degrees from the rising hinge <note>original: "cardine"; the 'hinge' or 'pivot' refers to the Ascendant, the easternmost point of the horizon.</note>,
Which those well-skilled in the stars call the Horoscope <term>Horoscope</term> <note>original: "horoscopon"; in ancient astrology, this term specifically refers to the Ascendant (the rising sign) rather than the entire birth chart.</note>.
In whatever sign, therefore, that number arrives,
Give that place to Fortune <term>Fortune</term> <note>The "Lot of Fortune" (Tyche), representing prosperity, health, and the physical body.</note>; then you shall join the other signs in order,
With all the stars quickly following in their proper sequence.

If there is someone who has then come forth from their mother’s womb,
Reverse the paths just as the order of nature is reversed:
Consult Phoebe <term>Phoebe</term> <note>A poetic name for the Moon, sister of Phoebus (the Sun).</note>, who imitates her brother’s light,

This same number the shining Horoscope orders to be counted from itself.
Let Fortune hold this place, with the following stars
Positioned in the order of nature, if all things are correctly placed.

Perhaps you might also ask, with a mind eager to note such a thing,
By what method you can, at the moment of a person’s birth,
Determine the Horoscope rising from the vast circle of the world;
For unless the observation is held by a reasoned method under the stars,
The foundations of the art crumble, and the order does not harmonize,
Because with false hinges <term>Hinges</term> <note>original: "cardinibus"; the four cardinal points of the zodiac: the Ascendant, Descendant, Midheaven, and Imum Coeli.</note>—which govern all things—
The universe presents a false face, the origin of life is not certain,
And the stars of the celestial temple <note>original: "sidera templi"; the 'temple' refers to the sacred architecture of the sky or the specific 'houses' of the zodiac.</note> shift with every passing moment.
If a matter so great in its effect is so full of labor...

The world, flying through the signs in its eternal courses,
so that it may illuminate the whole globe with its curved arcs,
aims to express its certain features and arrange them properly,
and to grasp the smallest point of such a vast mass:
namely, which part holds the rising <note>original: "exortum"; referring to the Ascendant or eastern horizon.</note>, which holds the summits of the world <note>The Midheaven, or the highest point the sun reaches.</note>,
which carries away the settings, or which remains fixed in a single circle.
The order of my work forbids me to pass over this common knowledge:
that which grants <gloss>two</gloss> hours to the shining signs
and arranges the stars in equal spaces with equal intervals.
So that from that part where the Sun's <note>original: "phoebi"</note> rising began,
the <gloss>three</gloss> number <gloss>three</gloss> departs; and let it assign <gloss>one</gloss> totals to <gloss>three</gloss> the stars,
until the time of birth <gloss>three</gloss> arrives at <gloss>four</gloss> those very points,
and where it has stopped, let that <gloss>m</gloss> sign be said to be rising.
Thus <gloss>three</gloss> therefore, because the circle of signs is on an oblique path,
and <gloss>but</gloss> other stars rise with bent limbs <note>A poetic way of describing constellations that appear to rise sideways or at an angle.</note>,
whereas <gloss>but</gloss> because there is a more upright order for those as they rise,
so that some <gloss>or</gloss> star is nearer to us and another further away.
The winter solstice <gloss>but</gloss> <term>bruma</term> <note>The shortest day of the year, occurring when the Sun enters Capricorn.</note> scarcely seems to end the daylight;
and that <gloss>but</gloss> path is short <gloss>or</gloss> while this circle is so long.
The Balance <note>Libra</note> and the Ram <note>Aries</note> restore day and night as equals <note>These are the equinoxes, where the duration of day and night is exactly the same.</note>;
thus the middle ground conflicts with the extremes, and the extremes <gloss>three</gloss> of light.
Nor do the times of night vary less than the hours of light;
yet <gloss>three</gloss> this order remains the same for the opposite months.
In <gloss>in</gloss> so dissimilar a space and with the varying lengths of days,
who could believe in what <gloss>but</gloss> ways the shadows [move] into the breezes?

<summary>This page explains the astronomical concept of "oblique ascension," noting that because the zodiac is tilted, different constellations take different amounts of time to rise above the horizon. This variation is why the lengths of days and nights change throughout the year, except during the equinoxes of Aries and Libra.</summary>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 43v regarding the rising of the zodiac signs. While the previous page introduced the concept that signs do not rise at equal speeds because of the "oblique circle" (the Earth's tilt), this section explains how this affects the measurement of time and why we cannot simply assume each sign takes exactly two hours to rise.</meta>

<page-num>440</page-num>

All the signs remain under an equal law of the world; 
add to this that the measurement of the hour is uncertain, nor does any 
other part stand still. But just as the sum of the days 
is turned, the parts both rise and then recede. 
Yet, in whatever constellation the day is drawn out, 
six <note>original: "sex"; referring to the six signs of the zodiac visible at any one time.</note> are held above the lands and six hours <note>The text uses "hours" here poetically to mean the time-blocks associated with the signs.</note> beneath them. 
From this, it happens that all signs cannot be born <note>original: "nasci"; meaning to rise over the horizon.</note> in two-hour periods, 
since there is not so much space for the struggling hours; 
even if they now preserve the twelve <note>original: "bissena"</note> by every right, 
which number the matter requires, yet common usage does not grasp it.

# On the measurement of time and the signs

These traces of truth will be established for you: 
measuring out the light and the night into equal hours. 
I have learned to what extent they lie open in various times. 
First, let a rule be formed for exact hours, 
which may reach the slow days and the slow shadows. 
This will be in the Scales <note>The constellation Libra.</note> when the nights begin to conquer 
the light, and when they begin to yield in the middle of spring. 
For then only are twelve equal hours of time 
open to the day, when the Sun <note>original: "phoebus"</note> runs through the middle of the heavens. 
But when he is placed at the summit in the constellation of icy winter, 
and shines in the eighth part of the two-formed <note>original: "biformis"; referring to Capricorn, which is half-goat and half-fish.</note> Capricorn, 
then the narrow spring day turns into nine hours 
and a half <note>original: "dimidium"</note>; but do not be forgetful of the day, 
for fourteen <note>original: "bis septem"</note> are added to the numbers lest the hour should be closed off.

<summary>Manilius explains that while there are twelve signs and twenty-four hours, the time it takes for a sign to rise varies by season. He establishes the Equinoxes and Solstices as the benchmarks for calculating these "seasonal hours."</summary>

<keywords>zodiac, seasonal hours, equinox, solstice, Capricorn, Libra, Manilius, Astronomica, measurement of time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the varying lengths of days and the rising times of the zodiac signs. <page-num>41v</page-num>

Thus, the day and night each divide into twelve hours,
and the sum established by nature returns to a whole. <note>The author explains that regardless of how long the day or night is, they always combine to form a full cycle of 24 hours.</note>
From there, the nights shorten and the daylight increases in time,
until the stars of glowing Cancer <note>The constellation marking the summer solstice.</note> have finished their struggle;
And when, by changed turns, the hours are transformed
into those of winter, and day becomes night and light becomes
winter darkness, the alternating seasons conquer in turn.
Now here, now there, you move by steps through the stars
driven along: the logic of which has been made manifest through art,
collected and presented here through the text of my poems.
And this, at last, is the measurement for those hours
during which the river Nile <note>original: "Hilus," a common scribal variant for "Nilus."</note> waters the land with its burning summer steps,
and bursting forth, it mimics the stars of the world
through seven mouths and openings that drive back the sea.

---

Come now, learn with a wise mind by how many *stadia* <note term="stadia">A "stadium" (plural: stadia) was a Greek unit of distance, here used by Manilius as a unit of time to measure the "width" of a constellation as it crosses the horizon.</note> and in what time
the stars rise and fall,
lest these great principles be missing from my brief words.
The noble constellation of the Wool-bearer <note>Aries, the Ram.</note>, which all things follow,
takes forty <note>original: "dena quater"</note> *stadia* as it rises, and leads double that
when it sets; and rising, it occupies an hour and a third <note>original: "trientem"</note> of an hour.
When it sets, it doubles this; and as for the other signs,
they increase by eight *stadia* each as they rise into the world,
and they lose just as many as they sink into the shadows;
the hour grows for each subsequent sign by one quarter <note>original: "quadrante"</note>.

<meta>This page continues Manilius’s mathematical exposition on the zodiac. Following the discussion of the varying lengths of days as the sun moves through different constellations, the poet now explains the symmetry between the rising of Aries and the setting of Libra, and introduces the concept that the "standard" two-hour rising time for signs only truly applies at the Earth's equator.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>

And these parts are led out into the third and fourth fractions.
When the stars rise toward the constellation of **Libra**, <note>Libra, the Scales, marks the autumnal equinox when day and night are equal.</note>
the increases in daylight are withdrawn with equal weight.
When they return to the orb and the stars of **Libra** once more,
the order is changed, and they return with equal speed through those same times.
For through as many stars as the daylight had increased,
or as many hours as it had lingered, **Libra** recedes by that same amount.
**Aries** <note>The Ram, the constellation of the spring equinox.</note> sets, and the duration and time of its falling
holds as much as the **Claws** <note term="chelae">The "Claws" (original: *chelae*) was the ancient Greek name for the constellation Libra, as they were originally seen as the pincers of the neighboring Scorpion.</note> take up when they rise.
The signs that follow turn themselves in the opposite direction.

Once these things have been settled and stored in a watchful mind,
it is now easy to know which star is the **ascendant**, <note term="horoscopus">In ancient astrology, the *horoscopus* is the "hour-marker"—the specific degree of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of a person's birth.</note>
since it is permitted to track the rising signs at certain times
and to assign them to their proper hours,
so that the calculation may be drawn in parts from those signs
in which **Phoebus** <note>A poetic name for the Sun.</note> will be, the total sum of which I have given.

->**Regarding the Tilts of the World**<-

But the measurement of days is not the same across all lands,
nor are the shadows known by the same position or time.
The mode is varied, though they change under a single principle.
Never do the **Fleece of the sign of Phrixus** <note term="Phrixei vellera">The "Fleece of Phrixus" is a mythological reference to the Golden Fleece, representing the constellation Aries.</note>
and the **Claws**, and the just balance of the **Scales**—
all rise for exactly two hours each,
except where the middle of the world <note>The Equator.</note> encircles in a straight line
and turns equally upon the axis poured through it.

<meta>This page continues Manilius’s explanation of the celestial sphere. Having established the "ideal" measurements of the zodiac at the equator, he now describes the physical reality of the Earth as a sphere and how a traveler’s movement toward the poles changes their perspective of the stars from a "straight" rising to an "oblique" one.</meta>

<page-num>45v</page-num>

☿ <note>The symbol for Mercury is used here likely as a section marker or a shorthand for "nota" (note well).</note> Let a perpetual force be joined in peace with the days;
The dark nights stand in an equal treaty of time,
Nor does the manifest deception of the false world lie open,
Nor is night returned in a similar way through the entire year,
When there is one measure for all the signs of Autumn,
Nor does it matter then where **Phoebus** <note>A poetic name for the Sun.</note> runs among the stars—
Whether he burns the shore-dwelling **Crab** <note>The constellation Cancer, marking the summer solstice.</note> or is carried against
the middle stars, or those four that lie between,
and through each arc of the circle of three signs.
Behold, whether the zone lies in an oblique or a straight order,
they rise above the head and are carried over the lands,
and they rise again with equal spaces through each period of time.
And the world lies open wide with its sphere well divided;
at the same time as you depart from that part of the lands,
<margin>but it is turned</margin>
whenever you turn yourself from the furthest axis
through the convex steps and the footprints of the earth—
for he <note>The Creator or Nature.</note> binds the world together with a smooth ground
into a swelling shape, suspended in the middle of the whole universe.
Therefore, when you climb the round and creaking sphere,
one part of the earth will flee even as you depart,
while another will be revealed; as much as the sky has bent in its circuit,
so much will the position of the flying heavens incline.
And the stars which just now were rising in a straight path
are now led into the heavens in a curved track,
and the belt of the signs <note term="zodiac">Original: *baltheus signorum*. The "belt of signs" is the Zodiac, the band of sky containing the twelve constellations through which the Sun appears to move.</note> will be tilted in the sphere.

<summary>Manilius describes the Earth as a perfect sphere suspended in space and explains how its curvature alters a viewer's perspective of the heavens. He notes that while the zodiac rises "straight" at the equator, it appears increasingly tilted or "oblique" as one moves toward the poles.</summary>

<keywords>spherical earth, zodiac, equator, Phoebus, Cancer, celestial mechanics, obliquity, ancient astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius's discussion on how latitude affects the perception of time and the movement of the stars. The previous page ended as the observer moved away from the equator toward the poles, causing the celestial sphere to appear tilted rather than "straight."</meta>

<page-num>46r</page-num>

And it was transverse in its space, because its measure is one.
**Our** dwelling-places change; therefore, the sky itself seems to move.
**Time** and reason now compel us to record the various periods
**Under** such a region, when the stars complete their courses
**In** a bent order, and their paths are oblique and difficult.
**Further** away some lie, while another reclines closer;
**According** to the distance, a great delay is granted. Those that rise
**Nearest** to us are seen moving in the long circles of the sky,
**While** those that shine at the very edge are plunged into hidden shadows.
**And** when one comes closer to the icy **Bears** <note>original: "arctos"; referring to the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor, which mark the North Pole.</note>,
**The** **winter signs** <note>original: "brumalia signa"; the constellations associated with the winter solstice.</note> obstruct the eyes even more.
**There** will scarcely be a rising or a setting; if one proceeds
**Further** from there, the constellations will be hidden in all their limbs.
**They** will drag out nights for thirty days <note>original: "trictenas"; literally "thirty-fold," referring to the long polar night where the sun does not rise for a month or more.</note> of continuous time,
**And** take away just as many days of light. Thus the specific duration
**Of** days is created, and time is spent in somber hours,
**And** the light gradually fades in space even while the stars shine.
**More** signs appear in fragments during this disrupted time;
**Heavenly** periods are sought with a sense of dread in their midst;
**They** will bring the **Sun** <note>original: "phoebum"</note> and weave shadows together at once,
**With** the months broken apart, until the year is weakened.
**If** indeed nature should allow one to pass beneath the very summit of heaven,
**Where** the icy axis shines with its rigid joints,
**To** travel over eternal snows and the freezing world,
**Looking** upon the downward-turned limbs of the **Lycaonian girl** <note>original: "Lycaonie... puellae"; a poetic reference to Callisto, the daughter of Lycaon, who was transformed into the constellation Ursa Major (the Great Bear). To be "under" her is to be at the North Pole.</note>

<summary>Manilius describes the astronomical phenomena of the far north, explaining how the curvature of the Earth leads to oblique star paths and the extreme conditions of the polar night. He poetically details the "weakened year" where the sun disappears for months near the celestial axis.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astronomical calculations regarding the rising times of the zodiac signs. They receive and pass on equal portions of the yielding lot. This path will show how to set the sums of the stages <term>stadia; units of measurement for the zodiac's rotation, roughly equivalent to degrees</term> and how to count their risings through all the stars. When we have correctly received these alongside their proper hours, the **Horoscope** <note>original: "horoscopus"; the specific point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a given moment</note> will never fail in any region, since individual signs can then be calculated from that position held by **Apollo** <note>original: "phoebus"; the Sun</note>.

### On the rising and setting times

Now learn in what moments the winter months begin to rise; for they do not proceed by equal steps through all the stars. Until they touch the fleeces of the **snowy sign** <note>The Ram, or Aries, which marks the spring equinox</note>, forcing light and shadows to bear an equal yoke <note>An allusion to the equinox, where day and night are of equal length</note>, the great logic of their brevity must be taught. 

First, you must take the measurement of the days—specifically the shortest day, which **Capricorn** <note>The sign of the winter solstice, the shortest day of the year</note> drives—and the measure of the hour of the night.
<margin>but the hour</margin>
By as much as the longest nights surpass the light, and the light in turn will reclaim those shadows, a third part of that difference must always be assigned to the middle sign. Once that portion is held, join half of it to the first sign, and join the same amount to the last sign. In this way, distribute the whole time into parts. By this method, three signs are joined to the following stars. If the winter night happens to be three hours longer than the day, let **Capricorn** and its hour <note>meaning its specific rising time</note> take up half a portion of the light, and let **Aquarius** <note>the Water-Bearer</note> take a full hour.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical rules for calculating the 'rising times' of zodiac signs during the winter months, specifically how to distribute the difference between the shortest day and the equinox across Capricorn and Aquarius.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Manilius, Capricorn, Aquarius, zodiac, rising times, winter solstice, Apollo, Horoscope</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the increasing and decreasing lengths of days and nights as the Sun moves through the zodiac. While the previous page focused on the shortest day in Capricorn and the transition toward spring, this page explains how the "surplus" of light is distributed through the signs of Pisces, Taurus, and Gemini until reaching the summer solstice in Cancer.</meta>

Let each sign lead its own sum properly and join to it the total of the signs that came before. Let **Pisces** <note>The Fishes; the final sign of winter leading into the spring equinox</note> add to themselves only as much time as they preserve from the portion received from the sign before. Once three hours have been completed—balancing both night and day—let them now pass the time on to be made equal at the season of spring.

From the sixth part, the time begins to advance. The **parched signs** <note>original: "arentia signa"; likely referring to the signs of the ascending Sun as the heat begins to increase</note> triple their strength by a half-measure, and beyond the hours they received, they double the total for the days; thus the gliding stars yield according to a divided law. This array of signs draws away as many hours from the nights as **Pisces** <note>original: "pisces"</note> had previously taken away under its own name.

An hour is given to **Taurus** <note>The Bull</note> so that, like the previous accumulations, the **Gemini** <note>The Twins</note> add a half-measure to it. Thus the last signs are returned equal to the first, and the nights are paid off as if with interest <note>original: "foenore"; the poet uses the metaphor of a debt being settled to describe the cyclical exchange of daylight and darkness</note>. Again, they begin to respond from their own lot for the days, and that sign which shines nearest responds equally; the middle stars are reckoned with equal forces. They carry out their distinct movements for the varying seasons.

By this turn, the nights depart toward the stars of midwinter <note>original: "brumae"; the winter solstice</note>, while the days rise up and the circle of the year is changed. When the solstice is slow <note>The Sun appears to "stand still" at the solstice, hence it is called "slow"</note> under the sign of **Cancer** <note>The Crab; the sign marking the summer solstice and the longest day</note>, then the night equals the length of the midwinter day, and the long day returns, similar to the length of the night that revolves in that season. A rising path will be able to lead toward the south, and five steps <note>original: "vadis"; literally "steps" or "shallows"</note> return what was sent out to the world.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the positions of the heavens. While the previous page discussed the seasonal variation in daylight hours, this page moves into the specific calculation of the Horoscope (the Ascendant), which is the specific point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</meta>

### Calculating the Rising Sign

For if you ask what hour of the day it is when the light is present, you shall observe the time and refer back to this specific number. Once these values have been multiplied and then reduced, you take five; for in any given hour, **fifteen parts** <note>original: "ter quinas... partes"; fifteen degrees, as the sky rotates 15 degrees every hour (360 degrees ÷ 24 hours)</note> of the world lift the stars. 

When this number is settled, you must also join to it those degrees which the **Sun** <note>original: "phoebo"</note> has already passed through the signs. Remember this total: you will distribute it in **sums of thirty** <note>original: "tricenas summas"; each zodiac sign spans exactly 30 degrees</note> across the constellations. Begin this count in the very sign where the **Sun** <note>original: "phoebus"</note> was shining, and then continue through the other signs that follow. 

In whichever sign the consumed number finally halts, or in whatever degree it leaves its final remainder, **that shall be the rising part** <note>The Horoscopos or Ascendant</note>, both in its degree and its zodiacal form. When it has produced a single sum containing all the parts, it will grant thirty to each sign until the number is exhausted. In whatever sign and degree that number falls, you may believe that it was "born" alongside the human body, having seen the whole world in that same instant.

#### The Foundation of the Chart

If, therefore, the swift movement of the stars must be sought at the moment of birth, the **Horoscope** <note>original: "horoscopus"; the specific degree of the zodiac rising in the East</note> must be found with a certain calculation of the rising. Thus, when its seat is established under the **first pivot** <note>original: "cardine primo"; the Ascendant, the first of the four "hinges" or angles of an astrological chart</note>, the heights of the midheaven cannot be mistaken. 

Nor shall it be forgotten: the foundations must stand firm under a single point. Let the true risings and the true secondary risings of the stars be established, so that the **ten powers** <note>original: "denaq₃"; likely referring to the divisions of the houses or planetary strengths</note> may recede into their own proper and powerful stations.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical formula for determining the Ascendant (Horoscopos) by calculating the rotation of the sky (15 degrees per hour) and distributing degrees across the zodiac signs.</summary>
<keywords>Ascendant, Horoscopos, degree calculation, zodiac signs, Sun, first pivot, midheaven, celestial rotation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for astrological calculation from the previous page. While the previous section detailed how to find the specific degree of the Ascendant (Horoscope) based on the Sun's position and the time of day, this section explains how that point in the sky governs the division of time—assigning specific signs of the zodiac to rule over years, months, days, and hours of a person's life.</meta>

### How Each Sign is Assigned to a Year, Month, Day, or Hour

From here, the specific times for those being born <note>original: "gnatum"; referring to the subject of a birth chart or nativity</note> shall be assigned to the signs. These times are divided and channeled into their own years, their own months, their own lights of day, and the hours of those days, through which individual signs exert their particular powers.

The first year will belong to that sign in which the **Sun** <note>original: "sol"</note> shone at birth; it is a year that, in its circling, consumes the various seasons of the world. Then, the signs that follow in order take up the subsequent years. The **Moon** <note>original: "luna"</note> will determine the months, and these signs in turn establish the monthly cycles. 

The **Horoscope** <note>original: "horoscopus"; the Ascendant or the sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> claims the first hours for its own protection and then hands the following days over to the stars that succeed it. In this way, the heavens revolve your years, your months, and your days, and the signs themselves are counted through the passing hours. This ensures that all time is divided among the stars, and that the constellations vary their movements through alternating turns, so that each might fulfill its role as it returns in its celestial circuit.

This is why there is such great discord in the affairs of time: good things are crowded by evils, and tears often follow our prayers. Nature does not maintain a steady course in all things; life flows along so thoroughly mixed and never remains constant. It loses its predictability by varying everything for everyone. 

Years do not always match years, nor do months agree with other months. Even the day itself changes and leaves a different one behind; no hour is produced that is exactly like any other hour. Thus, the times of our lives are established, distinguished by their own specific signs. <note>The OCR reads "carentia" (lacking), though standard versions of Manilius often read "variantia" (varying), suggesting that time consists of signs that are constantly changing.</note>

<summary>This passage describes the astrological concept of "time-lords," explaining how the zodiac signs govern different scales of time—from years down to hours—starting from the position of the Sun, Moon, and Ascendant at birth.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Manilius, Horoscope, Ascendant, Sun, Moon, time-lords, zodiac signs, chronocrators, fate</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how the Ascendant (the Horoscope) serves as the "starting point" for a life's timeline. While the previous page discussed the general concept of celestial influence, this page details the specific mathematical hand-off of power from one sign to the next across different units of time: years, months, days, and hours.</meta>

Through the many signs, all of fleeting time is divided as it flies past. In this way, the signs produce the months and warn of the various fates that threaten us <note>original: "casusque minantur"</note>. As the stars turn in their cycles, there are methods by which the hours of the heavens are made open to those being born. They achieve this by finding the star which they call the <term>Horoscope<note>original: "horoscopon"; here meaning the Ascendant, or the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of birth.</note></term>.

From that specific point, the character of the hours and days is mapped out. The entire system <note>original: "omne rationem"</note> operates through these shifting times and stars. From this single starting point, the months, years, days, and hours begin their course, and are handed over in sequence to the stars that follow. 

And since all things are born from a shared origin, their turns are nevertheless diverse: some complete their circuit more slowly, while others move more quickly, but everything eventually arrives at its appointed star. An hour passes twice in a day <note>A reference to the way signs or planetary influences cycle through the day</note>, a day once in a month, and one month in a year. Once twelve suns have completed their course, the year is done. 

It is difficult for all these cycles to align perfectly at the same time, such that the month and the year are both governed by the power of a single sign. A harsher sign might drive the month, even if the year's sign is broader; a sorrowful sign might fall upon the day, even if fortune favors the day overall; or perhaps a harder hour strikes during a lucky day. 

For this reason, it is not right to believe that any period of time is entirely uniform. The months do not remain fixed to the sign of the year as the years turn, nor do the days to the months, nor the hours to the days. This is because sometimes these influences hurry too much, and at other times they linger. Now one influence is missing, now another is present; time recedes in its turn, returns, or is changed by the arrival of a different time.

It emphasizes the fluid and often contradictory nature of fate, as different celestial "clocks" move at different speeds.</summary>

And adds two courses <note>2 years, totaling 42 years</note>, and leaves [the native] a young man.
But those [stars] which precede the hour of the rising pivot point <note>The "Ascendant" or first house</note>
Will grant twenty-three years to those being born,
Snatching away youth, scarcely tasted, in its flower.
And above the setting [pivot point] <note>The "Descendant" or western horizon</note> it is diminished and gives back ten
Spaces of years, and joins a tenth to three increased <note>13 years</note>.
Killing the boy within, and with twelve <note>original: "bissexq;" (two times six)</note> settled,
It will drag immature bodies to a fated death.
**What the tripled signs are and what powers they possess**
**But nevertheless, in the first place, they must be noted by a mindful soul**
Those signs established which rise in opposite parts
And hold the divided heaven with equal distinction,
Which they call "tropical" <note>original: "tropica"; these are the cardinal signs—Aries, Cancer, Libra, and Capricorn—which mark the solstices and equinoxes</note>, because for them the four
Seasons of the year turn with these signs and loosen the nodes <note>the turning points of the sun's path</note>.
They change the whole world as the pivot point is overturned
And bring in new forms of works and things.
Cancer shines at the peaks of the summer zone
And extends the longest day, and with a small retreat
It diminishes [the day], and by as much time as it has cheated the light,
By so much it increases the nights; it stands as the highest over all
When the field hastens to be threshed with the fragile stalk of Ceres <note>The goddess of the harvest; here used as a metaphor for grain or wheat</note>,
And men stretch their limbs in various wrestling-grounds <note>original: "palestras"; referring to the Greek tradition of summer athletics</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the mathematical formulas for predicting lifespan based on the houses of a birth chart and begins a new section on the Tropical (Cardinal) signs, specifically describing the influence of Cancer and the summer solstice.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, lifespan, horoscopus, cardinal signs, tropical signs, Cancer, Ceres, summer solstice, Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "tropical" or cardinal signs, specifically detailing how the Sun's passage through Cancer, Capricorn, and Aries dictates the changing seasons, the length of days, and the behavior of the natural world.</meta>

And the warm sea flags with tossed waves.
Then also bloody wars are waged with fierce Mars <note>Mars is the Roman god of war; here used to signify the onset of military campaigns in the spring and summer</note>,
Neither does winter defend Scythia, nor does parched Germany;
Now the earth flees from itself, and the ground swells into the breezes.
Here again Phoebus <note>original: "phoebus"; a poetic name for the Sun</note> is positioned with the constellation of Cancer;
He creates the solstice and turns at the height of Olympus <note>In this context, Olympus refers to the highest point in the sky or the celestial vault</note>.
From the opposite side, Capricorn governs the sluggish midwinter <note>original: "bruma"; the winter solstice, the shortest day of the year</note>,
He drives through the shortest nights and longest days <note>The poet here describes the transition or the balance of the solar cycle; as the Sun moves from Capricorn toward the summer signs, the days begin to lengthen</note>,
He extends the times, leads forth the day, and dissolves the shadows,
And in turn, he now fills up the losses, now the times.
Then the countryside, every field, the closed sea, and established camps—
These endure the shivering rocks of midwinter,
And Nature stands in one place and rests for a little while.
Next in effect is the relation of motion,
For when Aries receives Phoebus, and the stars of Cancer are sought again,
You constrain the times between the beginning of the return and the end,
Joining harmonious times in a divided world.
He orders the established changes and the stars of midwinter
To let the day overcome and the nights yield,
Until they come to the stars of summer Cancer.
Then first the sea is spread with quiet waves,
And the earth dares to send out various flowers;
Then the race of cattle and birds through broad pastures
Rushes into love <note>original: "venerem"; referring to Venus, the goddess of love, used here to describe the mating season</note> and giving birth, and the whole grove
Speaks with a musical voice and grows green in every leaf.

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and poetic description of the cardinal points of the zodiac, moving from the summer and winter solstices (Cancer and Capricorn) to the autumnal equinox (Libra) and the spring equinox (Aries), explaining how these specific degrees of the zodiac regulate the agricultural calendar and the length of the days.</meta>

The world is moved by powers within these signs.
Opposite to the former position, when by chance it shines,
Libra leads the day and night with an equal bond <note>This describes the autumnal equinox, the point in late September when day and night are of equal length globally</note>.
She then commands the nights to conquer thereafter, until they reach their own turn
From the very time of the winter solstice <note>original: "bruma"; the shortest day of the year</note> when the seasons are overcome.
Then Liber <note>original: "liber"; a name for Bacchus, the Roman god of wine, here used to represent the grape harvest</note> descends full from the heavy elm tree,
And the rich new wine foams from the pressed clusters.
They also entrust Ceres <note>original: "cererem"; the Roman goddess of agriculture, here used to represent the sowing of grain</note> to the furrows while the earth,
Loosened by the warmth of autumn, lies open as it draws in the seeds.
These four signs are powerful in the art <note>The "art" refers to the practice of astrology and the calculation of celestial movements</note> and they change the seasons.
Thus, as they bend these or those outcomes of affairs,
They do not remain in their first positions, enduring in vain.
Unless the measure is equalized through the whole figures,
For not all times are turned by the full signs.
One day under each season <note>Aries in spring and Libra in autumn</note> makes the night equal.
But Libra and Aries represent autumn and spring.
One day is the longest in the whole sign of Cancer,
Just as an equal night is said to be carried by the constellation of Capricorn.
Other times now press forward in turns, now yield.
Therefore, one specific degree must be identified in the <term>tropical signs</term> <note>From the Greek 'tropos' meaning 'turn'; these are the signs where the sun appears to turn back in its path, marking the seasons</note>
Which moves the world and changes the seasons of things.
The fates move deliberately, turning toward other uses,
Bending and revolving all things into their opposites.
Some place these powers in the eighth degree <note>Manilius refers to a historical debate: some ancient traditions, like the Babylonian or those followed by the poet Aratus, placed the start of the seasons at the 8th or 10th degree of a sign, rather than at the 0 degree used in modern astrology</note>.
There are those to whom the tenth degree is pleasing, and an authority for this was not lacking...
