--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical calculations of the zodiac in Book 3 to the philosophical introduction of Book 4. The previous page concluded the discussion on how specific signs of the zodiac regulate the lengths of days and the transition of seasons.</meta>

Who gave the first movements and the reins of the days.

# MANILIUS’S ASTRONOMY <note>original: "Astronomicon"</note> BOOK THREE
## ENDS. THE FOURTH BOOK OF THE SAME BEGINS.

Why do we consume our lives in such anxious years,
And why are we tortured by fear and the blind desire for things?
Forever old, we lose our time while seeking an eternity of care;
Never satisfied despite our many prayers,
We always act as if we are about to live, yet we never truly live.
A man is poorer in his goods the more he requires of them.
This man counts only what he has; that man desires what he does not.
And since nature demands so little for its own needs,
We build up the material for a great ruin through our prayers,
Buying luxury with our profits and plunder with our luxury.
The highest value of wealth is simply to pour that wealth away.
Release your mortal minds and lift your cares;
Cease to bewail life with so many superfluous complaints.
<margin>For</margin> <note>original: "Nant," likely a scribal error for "Nam," a conjunction used here to introduce the core argument of the book</note>
Fate rules the world; all things stand by a certain law. <note>original: "Fata regunt orbem"; this is the central tenet of Manilius’s Stoic philosophy—that the universe is governed by a predetermined divine order</note>
Long ages are marked by fixed courses.
From the moment we are born, we are dying; our end hangs from our beginning. <note>original: "Nascentes morimur"; a famous Stoic maxim emphasizing that the length of a life is determined at the moment of birth</note>
From Fate, wealth and kingdoms flow; from Fate, poverty more often arises,
And skills are given, characters are created,
Along with vices, disasters, losses, and the shortcuts of life.
No one can avoid a loss, nor can anyone obtain what is denied,
Nor seize an unwilling Fortune with his prayers,
Nor flee from her when she looms: each must bear his own lot.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Book 4 of Manilius's poem, pivoting from technical astronomy to a powerful philosophical defense of Stoic fatalism. It contains the famous phrases 'Fate rules the world' and 'we are born to die,' arguing that human effort is secondary to the predetermined law of the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Manilius, Stoicism, Fate, predestination, Book 4, Nascentes morimur, Fata regunt orbem, Roman philosophy, astrology, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical prologue of Book 4. Manilius is moving from the general principle that "Fate rules the world" to specific historical proofs, arguing that the survival and success of Rome were not the result of human effort or chance, but of a predetermined destiny.</meta>

But if the Fates did not give laws for life and death,
Troy’s fires would have escaped Aeneas, <note>original: "Aenean"; the Trojan hero and mythical ancestor of the Roman people</note> and for the sake of one
Man, the city, though overturned, would not have sufficiently conquered in its ruins.
Or if the wolf of Mars had shunned the brothers she carried, <note>Romulus and Remus, the legendary founders of Rome, who were said to be suckled by a she-wolf</note>
And Rome, thus born, would have remained a place for masters of flocks,
Who would have increased the power of thunderbolts on the Capitoline hills;
Lest he could be shut in his own citadel, he broke out—
And this head of the world <note>Rome</note> would have been captured and buried in fire.
The victor would have sought the city again with mutual wounds,
And Horatius alone would have closed the bridge with opposing arms, <note>Horatius Cocles, who famously defended the Sublician Bridge alone against the Etruscan army</note>
And the maiden would have broken the bridge and the city’s treaties at once; <note>A reference to Cloelia, a Roman hostage who escaped her captors by swimming across the Tiber</note>
And three brothers fell by the virtue of one; <note>The Horatii, three Roman brothers who fought three Curiatii from Alba Longa to decide a war; only one Horatius survived to claim victory</note>
No battle-line then hung upon one man who had been conquered,
When Rome and the sovereignty of the world, allotted by lot, lay in that man’s hands.
Why should I mention Cannae and the arms brought to our walls, <note>Cannae was Rome's most disastrous defeat against Hannibal during the Second Punic War</note>
And Varro’s delay, <note>Gaius Terentius Varro, the consul blamed for the defeat at Cannae</note> and that a great man could be conquered?
And after your lakes, O Trasimene, and the delaying Fabius, <note>The Battle of Lake Trasimene was another Roman defeat; Quintus Fabius Maximus "the Delayer" saved Rome through a strategy of attrition</note>
How the arts of conquered Carthage finally accepted the yoke,
And how the long-awaited Hannibal fell into our chains,
And how exile and a stealthy death were the lot of that king.
Add too the Italian forces and Rome fighting against its own
Limbs; add also the civil wars,
And the Cimbrian defeated by Marius, and Marius himself conquered in prison, <note>Gaius Marius, a famous general and seven-time consul who was later forced into exile and imprisoned</note>
He who was so often consul, and then an exile, and in exile still a consul,
And he lay as a loss comparable to the ruins of Libya. <note>original: "Libycis... ruinis"; refers to the famous image of the exiled Marius sitting among the ruins of Carthage, comparing his fallen state to the fallen city</note>

<summary>Manilius uses a series of "what-if" historical scenarios from Roman history—including the legends of Aeneas, Romulus, and the Punic Wars—to argue that Rome's survival was destined by Fate rather than human merit or luck.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Roman History, Aeneas, Romulus, Horatius Cocles, Hannibal, Punic Wars, Marius, Stoicism, Destiny</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s argument for the supremacy of Fate by citing more recent and famous historical examples. On the previous page, the author discussed early Roman history (Aeneas, Romulus, Horatius Cocles); here, he transitions to the fall of the Republic and the dramatic reversals of fortune experienced by the greatest men of Rome and the ancient world.</meta>

And he <note>original: "cepit kartaginis urbem"; referring to Scipio Aemilianus, who destroyed Carthage in 146 BCE</note> captured the city of Carthage from its very foundations.
Unless the Fates had granted this, Fortune would never have brought it to pass.
Who would believe that you, O Great One, <note>original: "magne"; referring to Pompey the Great (Pompeius Magnus)</note> would perish on the Nilotic shore,
After the power of Mithridates was conquered and the sea was reclaimed, <note>Pompey defeated King Mithridates VI of Pontus and cleared the Mediterranean of Cilician pirates</note>
And after earning three triumphs from a traversed world, <note>Pompey famously celebrated three triumphs, one for each of the three known continents: Africa, Europe, and Asia</note>
And when you were able to establish yet another "Great" man—
Who would believe that a shipwreck’s fire would bury your body,
And that collected fragments of a hull would make your funeral pyre? <note>After being assassinated in Egypt, Pompey's body was reportedly cremated on the beach by a loyal freedman using wood from a broken boat</note>
Who can change so much without the divine will of Fate?
That man <note>Julius Caesar</note> was born of heaven and received by heaven, <note>A reference to the deification of Julius Caesar after his death</note>
When, as a victor with civil arms well settled,
He ruled the laws of the toga; <note>The toga represents civilian life and government, as opposed to the military cloak</note> yet to beware the so often predicted
Wounds he could not, while the whole Senate watched.
Holding the decree in his right hand, and with his own blood
He blotted out the name, <note>A reference to the petition Caesar was holding when he was stabbed; Manilius suggests Fate overrode his legal power</note> so that the Fates might be able to conquer.
Why should I count the ruins of overturned cities and kings?
And Croesus on the pyre, <note>The wealthy King of Lydia who was nearly burned alive after his defeat by Cyrus the Great</note> and Priam as a headless trunk on the shore, <note>The King of Troy, whose death at the city's fall was a common symbol of fallen greatness</note>
For whom Troy itself was not even a pyre. Why mention Xerxes, on the sea and the deep itself, <note>The Persian King whose massive fleet was defeated at the Battle of Salamis</note>
Shipwrecked on the ocean? Why mention kings captured in a military procession,
Placed before the Romans? Or the fires snatched from the fires, <note>A reference to Lucius Caecilius Metellus, who saved the sacred objects and the eternal fire from the burning Temple of Vesta in 241 BCE</note>
And the flames giving way to the man who carried the temple’s treasures?
How many sudden deaths come to strong bodies,
And they themselves flee back again and wander through the fires; <note>A reference to "near-death" experiences or those thought dead who revive</note>
Some have even returned, carried out from their very graves,
And a double life happened to those for whom one was scarcely enough.

<summary>Manilius provides a series of historical "shocks"—the humble death of Pompey the Great, the assassination of Julius Caesar, and the falls of Kings Croesus and Priam—to prove that human success is entirely subject to the unpredictable but fixed decrees of Fate.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Pompey the Great, Julius Caesar, Carthage, Mithridates, Croesus, Priam, Xerxes, Metellus, Roman History, reversals of fortune</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s argument for the supremacy of Fate by citing more recent and famous historical examples. On the previous page, the author discussed early Roman history (Aeneas, Romulus, Horatius Cocles); here, he transitions to the fall of the Republic and the dramatic reversals of fortune experienced by the greatest men of Rome and the ancient world.</meta>

Behold, a slight illness kills, while a more serious one relents.
Medical arts fail; experience is conquered by vows.
Care harms; it helps to do nothing. Often the delay of evils
Grants a lucky turn; foods harm, and poisons spare.
Children degenerate from their fathers, and parents are surpassed.
They retain their own character and pass on their very nature <note>original: "pillum"; likely a variant of 'pium' (disposition) or 'pilum' (a hair), here referring to the transmission of innate traits</note>.
From Fate comes fortune: one man rages with love
And can swim across the sea and overturn Troy <note>References to the myths of Leander, who swam the Hellespont for love, and Paris, whose love for Helen led to the fall of Troy</note>.
Another’s brow is suited to the writing of laws.
Behold, children slay their father and parents their children,
And armed brothers clash in mutual wounds.
This war is not our own; they are compelled to set such things in motion
And to be driven toward their own punishments and the tearing of their limbs.
Not every age has produced Decii, nor every age Camilli <note>The Decii were a family of Roman commanders famous for "devotio"—sacrificing their lives to the gods in exchange for victory; Camillus was a legendary hero who saved Rome from the Gauls</note>,
Or a Cato unconquered even by death <note>Cato the Younger, a famous Stoic who committed suicide rather than submit to the tyranny of Julius Caesar, thereby "conquering" death through his steadfastness</note>.
The material for action is abundant, but the Law <note>The "Law" here refers to the preordained Law of Fate</note> resists.
Neither does poverty receive shorter years,
Nor is Fate for sale to those with immense wealth.
If fortune snatches away the proud in death
And leads them to the highest tombs of kings—
How great is this kingdom <note>The domain of Fate</note> that commands kings themselves!
And because unhappy virtue and harmful luck exist,
And there is a reward for ill-counsel while wisdom fails,
Fortune does not approve merit, but chance follows the deserving <note>Manilius is arguing that if outcomes were based on merit, the world would look different; since they aren't, Fate must be the cause</note>—
If she is carried wandering through all people with no distinction.

<summary>This page from Manilius’s Astronomica argues that human efforts, medicine, and moral virtue are all subordinate to the inescapable power of Fate. He uses examples of tragic family strife, the unpredictable nature of health, and the deaths of Roman heroes like Cato to prove that a divine, fixed law governs all outcomes regardless of human merit.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Fortuna, Stoicism, Cato the Younger, Roman Heroes, Decii, Camillus, Medical Arts, Predestination, Ancient Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the inevitable Law of Fate. Having listed historical examples of Roman heroes and tragedies, the author now addresses the philosophical implications of a fated world, specifically regarding morality, and transitions into a technical discussion of the Zodiac signs.</meta>

Indeed, there is something else that compels and rules us—something greater—which leads mortal affairs according to its own laws, assigns to those born of it their own span of years, and often mixes the turns of Fortune. It brings together bodies and human limbs; that birth is not merely the work of a seed <note>original: "seminis... partus"; Manilius argues that biological reproduction is secondary to the "seed" of Fate</note>. For what do we have in common with wild beasts? Or what adulterer has ever sinned so greatly as to produce a monstrous crime? <note>Referring to the birth of "monsters" or children with deformities, which the author argues are caused by the stars rather than human sin</note>. The stars renew forms, and heaven inserts new countenances. Finally, if there is no Fate, why is its order handed down to us? Why are all things that are to come prophesied <note>original: "canuntur," literally "sung," as ancient oracles were often delivered in verse</note> at fixed times?

Yet, this reasoning might seem to defend crime or rob Virtue of her rightful rewards. For no one hates deadly herbs any less because they do not grow by their own choice, but from a specific seed; nor is gratitude given any more lightly to sweet foods because no conscious will provided the fruit. In this same way, let the glory for the human mind be so much greater because it comes with the blessing of heaven; and on the other hand, we shall hate the guilty more, who were created for crime and its punishment. It matters from whence the crime descends, though the crime must still be confessed as such. Since it is fated, this is the very way to weigh Fate itself <note>A core Stoic argument: even if our actions are fated, our character remains ours to be judged. We do not stop hating a poison just because the plant cannot help being poisonous</note>.

### On the properties and effects of the signs

Now that I have taught this, it remains for me to construct in a fixed order the celestial steps which, by a winding path, may be able to lead the wise seer <term>vatem: a poet-prophet or inspired teacher</term> to the stars.

<summary>Manilius argues that the existence of prophecy proves the existence of Fate and addresses the Stoic paradox of moral responsibility in a predetermined world. He then begins a new section on the specific astrological influences of the Zodiac signs.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Stoicism, moral responsibility, prophecy, Zodiac, Marcus Manilius, astrology, virtue and vice</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of Fate. Having argued that human character and destiny are determined by the stars rather than mere biology, the author now begins a detailed technical description of the individual signs of the Zodiac—starting with Aries and Taurus—and the specific professions and personalities they bestow upon those born under their influence.</meta>

Now I shall recount to you the character of the signs, their dominant traits, <note>original: "colorem," literally "color," but used here to mean the distinctive "complexion" or nature of the signs' influence</note> and I shall set forth in order the various skills and arts they inspire.

Aries, rich with the wool of its fertile fleeces, though shorn of its coat, will always have hope for new growth. Between sudden shipwreck and blessed riches, the fortunes of those born under him will fall as they grow, and they will be carried into loss by the very things they know well. <note>This refers to the volatile nature of Aries, where wealth is easily gained but just as easily lost, like a merchant's cargo.</note> He will provide fruits to the common people, and through a thousand arts, his fleeces will produce diverse ways to seek profit. Now to wind the raw wool into balls, now to loosen it again; now to thin it into a delicate thread, and now to guide the looms. He inspires the making and selling of various garments for profit—works without which no nation could subsist, nor could luxury even exist. Even Pallas <note>The goddess Pallas Athena, patron of weaving and wisdom</note> herself claimed these arts for her own hands and thought them worthy; she considers her victory over Arachne to be a great triumph. <note>A reference to the myth where Athena transformed the mortal weaver Arachne into a spider after a weaving contest.</note> These interests and similar arts will the leader of the Zodiac <note>Aries is the first sign of the traditional Zodiac</note> fix in those being born; he will place an uncertain heart within a trembling breast, and create people who are always eager to sell themselves with their own self-praise.

Taurus has granted the countryside to simple farmers. To them, peaceful labor will come, though not the rewards of high praise; instead, the sign grants the various births of the earth. He submits his own neck to the plow and demands the yoke for his own shoulders. He carries the orb of Phoebus <note>The Sun. This refers to the Sun entering the constellation of Taurus in the springtime.</note> upon his horns; he summons military service to the lands and calls back the sluggish fields to their ancient cultivation—a leader of labor himself.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influences of the signs Aries and Taurus, associating Aries with the textile industry and a volatile temperament, and Taurus with the steady, humble work of agriculture.</summary>
<keywords>Aries, Taurus, Pallas Athena, Arachne, weaving, textiles, agriculture, astrology, Phoebus, Zodiac, fate</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zodiac signs and their influence on human character and profession. Having concluded the description of Aries on the previous page, the text now transitions from the agricultural virtues of Taurus to the artistic and intellectual pursuits of Gemini, before moving into the mercantile nature of Cancer.</meta>

Nor does the Bull <note>Taurus</note> lie idle in the furrows, nor does its breast crumble into the dust of the field. It bore men like the **Serrani** and the **Curii**, and produced those who are easily guided through the countryside; from his own plow, a **Dictator** was delivered. <note>This refers to the Roman ideal of the farmer-statesman, specifically figures like Cincinnatus who were called from their farms to lead the state in times of crisis.</note>
The love of praise drives their quiet minds, and their bodies, though slow with great weight, are powerful. The boy **Cupid** dwells beneath their brow. <note>original: "cupido," desire or the personification of love.</note>
From the **Twins** <note>Gemini</note> comes a softer pursuit and a gentler age: through various songs and voices shaped by melody, and slender pipes <note>original: "calamos," reed flutes</note>, and words set to strings.
To produce a mighty sound is both a labor and a pleasure in itself.
They desire that weapons and war-trumpets <note>original: "lituos," curved trumpets used by the Roman cavalry</note> be far away, as well as somber old age.
They pass their leisure in peace and achieve an eternal youth even in death.
They find paths to the stars by many rhythms and modes; <note>original: "nuisq; modisq;" likely *numeris modisque*</note>
They encompass the world, and afterward, the stars themselves are left behind by them.
Nature is less than their genius, through which she completes all things.
In so many inventions are the fertile Twins celebrated.

When **Cancer** appears, shining at the burning turning point of the sky, <note>The summer solstice, the "hinge" of the year when the sun reaches its highest point.</note>
Whom **Phoebus** <note>The Sun god</note> circles when recalled at the peak of his chariot’s path;
Holding the joint of the world and turning back the light.
That tenacious sign, though seemingly poured out for no practical use,
Bestows various ways of gain and the art of profit:
To carry a fortune through cities with foreign merchandise;
To trust one's wealth to the world and, watching for fires and winds,
To be able to sell goods to the whole world;
To join commerce through so many unknown lands,
And to seek out new plunder under a different sun.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influences of Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer, linking the signs to specific human achievements in agriculture, music, and global commerce.</summary>
<keywords>Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Roman history, music, commerce, astrology, Cincinnatus, Cupid, summer solstice</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zodiac signs and their influence on human character and profession. Following the description of the mercantile nature of Cancer, the text now transitions into the fierce nature of Leo and the intellectual pursuits of Virgo.</meta>

...and assembling sudden wealth from the price of things. 
He sails, hoping for sterile years in the lotteries of fate, 
so that he may sell at the right time with sweet interest. 
He possesses a clever mind, fighting for its own profits. 

**Q**uo could doubt the nature of the vast Lion? <note>original: "leonis," the constellation Leo</note>
Or what skills he dictates to those born under his sign? 
He always prepares new fights and new wars against beasts; 
he lives on the spoils of the herds and by plunder. 
This is his goal, this his pursuit: to decorate the proud 
with skins and to fix captured prey to his house, 
and <unclear>      </unclear> the woods with fear and live by his desire. 
There are some whose similar spirits even city walls cannot restrain, 
if they should slaughter the limbs of cattle in the middle of the city 
and hang the mangled parts in front of shops, <note>This refers to the profession of butchers, which Manilius links to the predatory nature of Leo.</note>
and through luxury prepare slaughter and profit from deaths. 
A mind suited to sudden paths and easy retreats, 
and a simple feeling in their breast, like that of children. 

**At** for those to whom Erigone <note>Erigone is another name for the constellation Virgo.</note> has declared their life at birth, 
the Virgin—who restrains her face with authority and goes naked— 
leads them to study, and shapes their character and hearts 
with learned arts. She will not grant so much the accumulation of wealth 
as she will give laws for the courts or for the republic. <note>original: "iura... prove resp," likely *iura... proque res publica*</note> 
She will make them the glory of the tongue and the kingdom of speech, 
and provide eyes for the mind through which one can see all things, 
whichever are hidden in the secret causes of nature.

<summary>This page details the astrological influences of Leo, associated with hunting and butchery, and Virgo (Erigone), associated with law, eloquence, and the study of nature's secrets.</summary>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Zodiac signs and their influence on human character and profession. Having previously described the intellectual and linguistic gifts of Virgo, the text now transitions into the technical skill of stenography, the legal and scientific precision of Libra (the Scales), and the agricultural and martial nature of Scorpio.</meta>

Here also will be a happy scribe to whom a single letter is a whole word, <note>original: "lra uerbum ē," likely *littera verbum est*. This refers to shorthand or stenography, where one character represents a full word.</note> 
who overcomes the tongue with signs and catches the speed of the speaker, 
taking down long phrases through new shortcuts. 
A goodness is in his face, and a modest shame restrains his tender years, 
reining in the great gifts of nature by holding them back. 
From here she will be fruitful—what a wonder, a birth from a Virgin! <note>A poetic play on the name of the constellation Virgo (the Virgin).</note> 
When the Claws <note>original: "chelæ." In early Roman astrology, the "Claws" of the Scorpion were treated as the sign of Libra (the Scales).</note> balance the night with the duration of light, 
through the new gifts of ripe Bacchus <note>original: "bacchi," referring to the autumn wine harvest.</note> after a year, 
they will grant the use of measures and the weights of things, 
and a birth rivaling the powers of Palamedes, <note>Palamedes was a legendary Greek hero credited with the invention of counting, weights, measures, and several letters of the alphabet.</note> 
who first imposed numbers on the highest things 
and gave them a fixed measure and their own proper shapes. 

This person will know the tables of the laws and established rights, 
and words hanging on slight marks, 
and what is divinely permitted and what is forbidden, and what punishment follows. 
He will be a perpetual judge for the people, even in his private life. 
No other star would better suit the birth of the Divine One <note>original: "Diuus." This likely refers to the Emperor Augustus, whom Manilius frequently praises as being favored by the stars.</note> 
who established laws rather than unravelling rights. 
Finally, whatever was placed in ambiguity 
and lacking a ruler, the examinations of the Scale resolve. 

Scorpio, with its tail ending in an armed point, 
with which it leads the chariot of Phoebus <note>original: "phœbi," meaning the Sun.</note> through its own stars, 
searches the earth and mixes seeds in the furrows. 
It creates minds burning for war and martial camps, 
and a citizen rejoicing in much blood.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influences of Virgo, Libra, and Scorpio, linking them to professions such as stenography, law, and agriculture, as well as the invention of weights and measures.</summary>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius's astrological poem, moving from the legal and civic concerns of Libra into the more physical and violent pursuits associated with the transition toward Sagittarius and Capricorn. The previous page concluded with the judicial and administrative gifts of the Scales; here, the focus shifts to the arena, the taming of wild animals, and the subterranean heat of the forge.</meta>

Nor is there less joy in the prey than in the slaughter, and even when
**Peace** itself is kept under arms, they take to the glades and wander the woods.
Now they wage war on men, now on the violent beasts of the wild.
Now they sell their lives to death and the lap of the arena, <note>original: "sinus arene." This refers to gladiators who "sold their heads" (their lives) for the sake of public spectacle in the Roman amphitheater.</note>
and each man prepares an enemy for himself when wars are at rest.
There are those whom mock battles and games in arms also please;
so great is their love of fighting that they learn war through leisure,
and whatever pursuit is carried out with similar skill.

**BUT** for those whose lot of birth is granted in the double-bodied form of the Centaur, <note>original: "bifero centauri corpore." This refers to the constellation Sagittarius, depicted as a creature half-man and half-horse.</note>
it is their joy to yoke chariots
and lead fiery horses with soft reins,
and to follow herds grazing across all the fields,
to subdue every kind of four-footed beast with appointed masters,
to soothe the rage of the tiger and take away the lion's fury,
and even to speak with the elephant and, by speaking, adapt that
great mass to human ears for various spectacles. <note>Manilius suggests that those born under Sagittarius have a supernatural affinity for training and communicating with large, exotic animals.</note>
Indeed, because the body of a beast is mixed with a man's among the stars,
and hands are placed upon it, he reigns over those things.
He grants the arrows to be launched from the curved bow, <note>original: "curvato spicula cornu," literally "darts from the curved horn," referring to the composite bow used by the Archer.</note>
and gives strength to the limbs and sharpness to the mind,
and swift movements and a breast that does not tire.

**Y**ou, Capricorn, cherish summer fires in your innermost chambers; <note>original: "Estiuos capricorne foues penetralibus ignis." This refers to the "internal" heat of the earth during the winter solstice, over which Capricorn presides.</note>
you draw here the study and pursuit of art. For whatever
fire does for our use, and whatever flames it moves toward the stars,
must be reckoned under your sign: to search out hidden metals... <note>original: "caeca metalla," literally "blind metals," meaning ores buried deep underground.</note>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s exploration of the zodiac’s influence on human character and professions. Following the previous discussion on Sagittarius and the taming of beasts, the text now transitions through the metallurgical arts associated with the heat of the forge, the winter solstice of Capricorn, and primarily the hydraulic mastery of Aquarius and the temperament of Pisces.</meta>

### The Arts of the Forge and the Earth

And to burn out the buried riches and the veins of the earth,
and to double the material with quick manual skill;
whatever is crafted from silver and whatever from gold,
and when the hot furnaces melt iron and bronze
and the hearths consume them: **Ceres**, <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture and grain. Manilius suggests that the tools forged in the fire are what allow the harvest to rise.</note> your gifts will then rise.
They also apply their zeal to clothing and fleeting wares,
distributing the lot of winter through the stars with cold,
where you draw back the nights that have been led to their highest peaks <note>original: "summa ad fastigia noctes." This refers to the winter solstice, the longest night of the year, occurring when the sun enters Capricorn.</note>
and make the year be born again as the light is summoned back.

### The Influence of Aquarius: The Water-Bearer

Here, too, is a mobility of things, and the mind often floats 
in change; old age is better under the **bound Fish**. <note>original: "vincto sub pisce." A reference to the constellation Pisces, depicted as two fishes tied together by a cord.</note>
The former pair <note>Likely referring to the preceding signs or the two fishes.</note> ends, and the youth who looks down 
from his tilted urn at the fountain he pours,
**Aquarius**, bestows his kindred arts:
To perceive waters beneath the earth and to lead waves onto the lands,
and to sprinkle the very stars with overturned floods;
to play with the sea upon new shores for the sake of luxury,
to construct various lakes and artificial rivers,
and to <term>suspend</term> <note>In the Roman context, this refers to the sophisticated engineering of aqueducts and lead piping (fistulae) that brought running water into private villas and upper stories.</note> traveling streams within houses.
A thousand citadels are buried under this sign, which the water tempers;
indeed, he will change the face of the world and its dwellings.

### The Heavens and the Fish

He shall visit the stars and the heavens in a new circle,
where through the waters come the riches of the sea that follows;
the silent race <note>original: "mute genus." A poetic term for fish.</note> and their sweet offspring flow toward the stars.
Their hearts are not stingy; they are easily led into losses. <note>Manilius characterizes those born under Pisces as generous or perhaps prone to financial carelessness, "flowing" like water away from their owners.</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition from the metallurgical crafts of the forge to the hydraulic engineering associated with Aquarius, concluding with the generous but spendthrift nature of those born under Pisces.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the descriptions of the individual zodiac signs, specifically focusing on the final sign, Pisces (the Fishes). It continues the theme from the previous page, which described the winter signs of Capricorn and Aquarius. This section emphasizes the maritime professions and the transition into the broader astrological theory that no sign possesses its power in isolation.</meta>

### The Children of the Fishes: Pisces

This water does not fail, nor does wealth remain stationary; thus the wave flows on. Last are those whom the twin stars of the **Fishes** <note>original: "pisces." This is the twelfth and final sign of the zodiac, marking the end of the solar cycle before spring begins again.</note> bring forth. For these people, their zeal will be for the sea and their life spent in the deep. They will trust in ships, or prepare the equipment for ships, and provide whatever the sea requires for its own uses. 

Innumerable arts arise; names scarcely suffice for the objects, so many are the small parts of a **hull**. <term>hull: original "carine" (carina). While it literally means the keel, it is used here metonymically for the entire ship and the complexity of its construction.</term> Add to this the study of steering, which reaches even to the stars and joins the sea to the heavens. Such a person shall know the globe: the rivers and the harbors of the world, and the winds that must be known. And now, he must turn the **tiller** <note>original: "clavum." The steering oar or helm of a Roman vessel.</note> here and there through the darkness, checking the ship and pouring straight through the waves, or driving with oars and bending the supple blades.

They also know how to clear the calm sea with drawn nets and to set out the ready catch upon their own shores; or how to hide the hooks with bait or <insert>how to</insert> live without such deception. They also assign to this sign naval battles and wars hanging over the sea, and waves stained with blood. It is a race prolific in sailors, fond of pleasure, characterized by quick movements and a life where everything is in constant flux through the ages. These are the characters and these the arts they grant to those being born under them. These are the six signs powerful in their own specific matter. <note>This refers to the final six signs of the zodiacal wheel, often associated with the "watery" or "earthly" elements in this specific sequence of the poem.</note>

***

### The Interwoven Powers of the Stars

But nothing is entirely powerful in itself; all things share their strengths with certain signs in equal parts. <note>Manilius transitions here from the individual traits of signs to the "dodecatemories" or the complex system of how signs interact and subdivide their influence.</note>

<summary>This page describes the influence of the constellation Pisces, focusing on navigation, shipbuilding, and fishing, before transitioning into the theoretical mechanics of how zodiac signs share their powers.</summary>
<keywords>Pisces, navigation, shipbuilding, maritime law, fishing, naval warfare, astrological influence, zodiac cycle</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific descriptions of the zodiac signs (ending with Pisces on the previous page) to the complex system of "decanates"—the internal subdivisions of the signs. It explains how signs share their influence and power with one another, suggesting that no single sign acts entirely on its own, but rather hosts the "hospitality" of others.</meta>

& as if by the hospitality of the world, they join in commerce and yield their own roles to the stars that hold these parts. It is never placed in number alone that the stars, established in thirty parts <term>parts: "partibus," referring to the thirty degrees that make up each zodiac sign.</term>, are said to be under a triple fate; they grant ten parts each to the stars that meet within them. In turn, these constellations are inhabited by three signs. 

For thus the truth remains woven into deep darkness; truth exists in the heavens, yet through many windings of things. This practice is brief, and the sky does not love shortcuts. But one image is opposed to others and the image deceives; it lies about its own powers and hides its names. This deep darkness, however, must be dispelled not by the eyes but by the mind; the divinity <note>original: "deus." Here Manilius refers to the divine intelligence or the "God" inherent in the structure of the cosmos.</note> is not to be noted merely by the surface. 

Now you must report which signs are joined to which, and in what order, so that other powers do not remain hidden through foreign stars. One lot falls to the **Bull** <note>original: "tauro" (Taurus)</note>, a third part yields to the **Twins** <note>original: "geminis" (Gemini)</note>; thus the sign is led, divided among the crowded degrees, carrying as many powers and masters as it has received. 

The system is divided in the **Bull**; and under that part they are reckoned: the first part to the **Crab** <note>original: "cancro" (Cancer)</note>, the middle to the **Lion** <note>original: "leoni" (Leo)</note>, and the last he grants to **Erigone** <note>Erigone: A poetic name for the constellation **Virgo**. In mythology, Erigone was the daughter of Icarius who was placed among the stars.</note>. For through the sign it stands, yet mixes its own powers through each individual thing. The **Balance** <note>original: "libra" (Libra)</note> takes up the first ten parts of the **Twins**; the **Scorpion** <note>original: "scorpius" (Scorpio)</note> is joined to the second, and the **Centaur** <note>original: "centauri" (Sagittarius)</note> takes the third part.

<summary>This page introduces the concept of decans, where each 30-degree zodiac sign is divided into three 10-degree segments ruled by different signs. Manilius argues that true astrological understanding requires looking beyond the surface "image" of a sign to see these hidden, overlapping influences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the "decans"—the three ten-degree subdivisions within each of the twelve zodiac signs. Each of these segments our system distinguishes in its proper order, 
Directing the star toward opposing **Capricorn** <note>original: "capricornum."</note>. 
Ten <note>original: "bis quinas," literally twice-five.</note> parts are first deemed worthy in that sign, 
At that joint of time <term>joint of time: "articulo temporis," referring specifically to the winter solstice.</term> under which the sign itself is reckoned; 
Because it makes the daylight equal to the winter shadows, 
And carries a kindred law at the divided pivot <term>pivot: "cardine," one of the four cardinal points of the sky where the seasons turn.</term>. 
The **Water-bearer** <note>original: "aquarius."</note> pours out the fires of the second part, 
Whom the **Fishes** <note>original: "pisces."</note> follow at the final star of the **Crab** <note>original: "cancri" (Cancer).</note>. 
But the **Lion** <note>original: "leo."</note> remembers its partner by the law of the triangle <term>triangle: "trigoni," a relationship between three signs of the same element; here, the Fire signs Leo, Aries, and Sagittarius.</term>, 
And receives the **Wool-bearer** <note>original: "lanigerum," a poetic name for Aries.</note> as its leader, and the **Bull** <note>original: "taurum" (Taurus)</note> 
Joined in a square <gloss>1</gloss>; under the **Twins** <note>original: "geminis" (Gemini).</note> the third <gloss>3</gloss> part <gloss>2</gloss> is borne. 
A line touches these through six turnings; 
**Erigone** <note>A poetic name for Virgo.</note> grants the chief honor to the **Crab** <note>Cancer.</note>, 
When she bestows the first <gloss>1</gloss> part <gloss>2</gloss>; the neighbor <gloss>3</gloss> is left behind. 
To the neighboring grove <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Likely referring to the Nemean Lion (Leo).</note>, one part <gloss>3</gloss> of her own belongs to you <gloss>2</gloss>, 
Which is granted to be possessed by a law that was once disdained. 
But the **Balance** <note>original: "libra."</note> rejoices in the equinox <gloss>equinox</gloss> and produces equal 
Nights and days within itself at a different time of year; 
It follows the **Wool-bearer** <note>Aries, the sign of the Spring equinox.</note> so that she, too, joins and balances the seasons twice. 
This shining sign composes the autumnal hours; 
It yields the first part to no stranger, but hands it to the next, 
The end of the other, whose name was made from the waves <note>Referring to the watery signs, likely Pisces or Aquarius.</note>, 
And willed the furthest parts to be under the **Fishes** <note>original: "piscibus."</note>. 
But he who threatens with arrows on a taut string <note>The Archer, Sagittarius.</note> 
Hands the first parts to the **Wool-bearer** <note>Aries</note> under the law of the triangle.

<meta>This page continues the technical sequence of "decans"—the 10-degree subdivisions of each zodiac sign—explaining how each sign's influence is distributed within others. It follows the previous page's discussion of Virgo (Erigone) and the Crab (Cancer).</meta>

The third part <note>The final ten degrees of Virgo.</note> the neighboring region of the **Centaur** <note>original: "centauri"; referring to Sagittarius as the ruler of this decan.</note> claims as the highest.
**Scorpius** has placed its influence in the first part of **Capricorn**.
From the middle <gloss>2</gloss> parts it gives to the **Bull** <note>Taurus.</note> and the final <gloss>3</gloss> parts to the **Twins** <note>Gemini.</note>.

If it returns the gift <gloss>2</gloss> of the **Crab** <note>Cancer.</note>, having been received and then receiving in turn,
It gives its own beginning <gloss>3</gloss> <note>The first decan of Capricorn.</note> joined to the **Lion** <note>Leo.</note>.
They say the final kingdoms and highest parts belong to the **Virgin** <note>Virgo.</note>.
Rejoicing in dark springs and a single flow <note>A poetic reference to **Aquarius**, the Water-Bearer.</note>,
It permits the first laws of **Libra** to be ruled by its own sign.
And it claims for itself ten noted parts <gloss>2</gloss> for **Hercules** <note>original: "Herculeis"; referring to the constellation of the Kneeler, often identified as Hercules, which rises or shares influence here.</note>.
The **Centaur** <note>Sagittarius.</note> retains the final parts as a youthful pasture.
Now the twin **Fishes** <note>Pisces.</note> remain, which close the circle of the stars.

And through <gloss>3</gloss> ten middle parts, you, **Bull** <note>Taurus.</note>, are received.
What remains <gloss>3</gloss> they take for themselves and are carried through the orb.
In this way, the final part yields to the end of the strong.
This system <gloss>3</gloss> reveals the hidden strengths of the world,
And divides the heavens into many modes and repeated names;
It distributes the circle better the more often it is divided.
Alas, let your hearts not be deceived by the labels and known titles;
The stars conceal themselves; they do not show their true faces to mortals.
The sharp edge of a sagacious mind must be applied,
And the truth remains to be sought in another way, and followed by all
With all one's strength. For in whatever part <term>part: "pte" (parte), referring specifically to the decan or degree of the zodiac sign.</term> of a sign a person is created,
He possesses the character of that part and is born under its star.

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical sequence regarding "decans"—the 10-degree subdivisions of each zodiac sign. It follows the previous page's discussion of the specific signs (Virgo, Capricorn, Cancer, etc.) and their subdivided influences. This page transitions from the technical details of the decans to a philosophical defense of the complexity of the astrological art.</meta>

Thus, through the tens <note>Referring to the "decans," the 10-degree subdivisions of the 30-degree zodiac signs.</note>, the lot of each sign shall be carried;
Varied offspring born under the selfsame star will bear witness to this.
And though there are so many thousands of living beings under a single
Sign, there come as many different characters as there are bodies.
They reflect a foreign nature through these "outside" stars <note>original: "aliena per astra"; referring to the influence of the signs ruling the specific decans rather than the primary zodiac sign.</note>,
And the births of humans and beasts flow together in a mingled stream.
Clearly, things established are joined in several parts,
And they carry divided laws under their own specific name.
The **Ram** <note>Aries.</note> will not love only wool, nor the **Bull** <note>Taurus.</note> only the plow,
Nor the **Twins** <note>Gemini.</note> only the Muses, nor will the **Crab** <note>Cancer.</note> love only merchandise;
Nor is the **Lion** <note>Leo.</note> solely a hunter, nor the **Virgin** <note>Virgo.</note> merely a mistress
Of measures <note>original: "mensuris"; Virgo was associated with the liberal arts and measurement.</note>, nor is **Libra** only powerful in scales, or the **Scorpion** <note>Scorpius.</note> in arms;
Nor the wild **Centaurs** <note>Sagittarius.</note> with their arrows, nor **Capricorn** with fire and waves <note>Referring to Capricorn's dual nature as a "sea-goat" and its association with the winter solstice hearth.</note>,
Nor the **Youth** <note>Aquarius, the Water-bearer.</note> with his own waters, nor the twin **Fishes** <note>Pisces.</note> through the seas.
Instead, the stars' powers are distributed in a mixture among several parts.

"Much labor," you say, "and a subtle work you bid me bear,"
Just when I seemed to be seeing the light with an easy understanding.
Why do you complain? You are attempting to scale the heavens, for you are a god <note>original: "deum est"; Manilius often argues that the human mind is a small part of the divine world-soul.</note>,
And though born under the law of Fate, you strive to know the Fates themselves,
To transcend your own heart and take possession of the universe.
For such a great prize there must be labor; such things are not granted freely.
Do not wonder at the twists of the path or the chains of cause and effect;
It is enough to have been permitted entry—let the rest of the discovery be ours.
But unless the mountains are dug through, the gold will escape you;
Otherwise, you will remain again in the great darkness of the mind.

<summary>This page explains that the diversity of human character is caused by the mixing of zodiacal influences within decans, and provides a famous philosophical defense of the difficulty of studying astrology as a divine labor.</summary>
<keywords>decans, zodiac, character, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpius, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces, Fate, Stoic philosophy, Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical sequence from the previous folio. Having detailed the specific influences of the "decans" (the 10-degree subdivisions of the zodiac), the author now shifts into a moralizing discourse on human greed versus the pursuit of divine knowledge, eventually returning to the complexity of astrological influences.</meta>

And the earth, piled high with its own riches, will stand in the way <note>A reference to the physical obstacles and the moral burden of material wealth.</note>
So that these riches may be reached, the whole world will be crossed twice over;
Men will regret having captured the sea for the sake of the price of gemstones.
The anxious farmers will consume their yearly prayers in vain,
And how great a reward will the deceptive countryside provide?
We shall seek a gain greater than the death that follows.
One should be ashamed to desire such fleeting goods for the sake of plunder—
Goods that belong to luxury and keep watch over its own ruins.
Descendants often sigh for the destruction of those yet to come.
What shall we give to heaven, from which everything comes?
Man must be expended <note>original: "impendendus homo est"; implying a total devotion of the self to study and contemplation.</note> so that God may be found within him.
By this law, you must note the characters of those being born.
It is enough to learn these ruling signs within the signs <note>Referring to the "decans" or subdivisions within a single zodiac sign that hold their own planetary or zodiacal power.</note>,
Through the groups of ten degrees and what is implanted in each,
If you remember to look at the specific parts themselves.
Either the stiff ice through which fire had been driven out,
And those areas sterile in both, which yet a more bountiful moisture fosters;
And where the nature fails in habits; for all the stars rise
With mixed forces and in a varied texture.
Nothing is uniform: look at the terrestrial regions,
And the sea, and the rivers fleeing from their known banks.
Crime <note>original: "Crunen," likely a transcription error for "crimen."</note> is frequent everywhere, and harmful things are joined to praise.
Thus a barren year is found even in broad lands,
And it destroys sudden offspring with little distinction;
Where just now was a harbor of the sea, there is now a vast Charybdis <note>A mythological whirlpool from the Odyssey, used here to illustrate the sudden and violent shifts in nature and fortune.</note>.

<summary>Manilius argues that humans should prioritize the divine study of the stars over the pursuit of material wealth and warns that the influences of the zodiac are complex and "mixed," much like the unpredictable nature of the Earth itself.</summary>
<keywords>greed, divine nature, Stoicism, decans, Charybdis, astrology, character, fate</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Manilius’s technical exposition of the zodiac. After discussing the decans (ten-degree subdivisions) on the previous page, the author now addresses the "dodecatemoria" or individual degrees, using metaphors of flowing water to explain how a single sign's influence can shift from moment to moment. He also pauses to reflect on the difficulty of turning such complex mathematical data into poetry.</meta>

And the grace of the praised sea <note>original: "ponti"; refers to the transition from the previous page's discussion of the sea's variety.</note> falls away after a little while, and the river glides now through rocks, now through plains, either making its way or seeking whether it comes or returns. So too the parts of heaven vary among the stars; just as one sign differs from another, so does a sign differ from its very self. In a single moment, it denies its strength and healthy use. And whatever is produced through these specific parts is created without fruit, or it falls, or it feels its benefits mixed with many complaints.

These parts must be marked by me in their own specific song—if anyone could report so many numbers so many times under the law of verse, or could repeat so many parts and learn so many totals. To change the face of speaking through various causes: we have envied this task, and thus the words are a burden, for the grace of poetry will be lacking, and labor falls in vain when the ear despises it. 

Yet if I, through this song, am bringing the laws of fate and the sacred movements of heaven must be spoken according to its commands, this figure <note>The structure of the cosmos or the poem itself.</note> is not given to be fashioned by me, but only to be shown. To have shown God is enough; He himself gave himself to me. Nor is it right to hang the weight of the world upon mere words. Our voice’s grace is neither greater than the subject nor is it small, if only it can mark out the things that must be sung.

Receive now which parts are to be condemned throughout the stars:
In the **Wool-bearer** <note>original: "Lanigeri"; the sign of Aries.</note>, the fourth part is harmful, nor is the sixth healthy; the seventh part belongs to them, as well as the tenth and the twelfth; then five, then the double of seven <note>The 14th degree.</note>, and nine.

<summary>Manilius explains that zodiac signs are not uniform, as specific degrees within them possess malefic or 'condemned' influences. He also reflects on the divine nature of his task, arguing that he is merely a vessel for revealing the celestial order rather than a creator of fiction.</summary>

<keywords>Manilius, Astronomica, Aries, malefic degrees, astrology, cosmic fate, Wool-bearer, poetic inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical catalog of the "condemned degrees" (partes damnandae) within each zodiac sign. Having previously established the difficulty of this mathematical task, the poet now moves systematically through Aries (finishing), Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, and Virgo, identifying which specific degrees bring misfortune or "poisonous" influence.</meta>

The twenty-first part <note>Each zodiac sign is divided into 30 degrees or "parts."</note> is added with praise, 
and the seventh consumes both the fifth and the harsh part.
The ninth part of **Taurus** is evil, as is the position of the third;
after the tenth, the seventeenth part is also bound in gloom.
Both the twenty-second is harmful and the twenty-fourth is hurtful,
and that which doubles thirteen but is cheated of two <note>mathematical shorthand for the twenty-fourth or twenty-eighth degree.</note>.
Among the thirty numbers, you too, the thirtieth, are the final total of ill.

In the **Twin signs** <note>Gemini</note>, the first and third parts are pestilential;
the seventh is no better, nor is the fifth, which is harmful in itself.
The one that is shorter than twenty <note>the 19th degree</note> does harm, and the one that is larger <note>the 21st degree</note>,
and a similar injury comes with the twenty-fifth.
When two more follow, they bring four others with them.

In **Cancer**, the first part is not free from ill, nor is the third;
the sixth, the eighth, and the tenth are joined in this thirst for harm.
The eleventh <note>original: "prima post decima"; the first after ten.</note> snatches away hope, nor is the use of the fifteenth more merciful;
the seventeenth after the tenth brings grief, as does the twentieth,
and the twenty-fifth, the twenty-seventh, and the twenty-ninth as the sum.

You too, **the Nemean one** <note>Leo; referring to the Nemean Lion killed by Hercules.</note>, are to be feared at the first touch,
and at the first part after the first ten <note>the 11th degree</note>; though the fifth is healthy.
The thirteenth <note>original: "ter [decima]"; three plus ten.</note> is lacking in heaven's favor, and the twenty-second hurts,
along with the three added to twenty and the three that follow.
The thirtieth part, the last, is no better than the first.

In **Erigone** <note>Virgo; named after the daughter of Icarius who became the constellation.</note>, neither the first part, nor the sixth, nor the first 
past the tenth <note>the 11th degree</note>, nor the fourteenth, nor the eighteenth is ever useful.
The one closest to the number twenty <note>the 21st degree</note> and the twenty-fourth are to be feared.

<margin>chance</margin> <note>original: "fors"</note> and the last part that closes the thirteenth degree <note>The poet continues the list for **Virgo**.</note>;
The seventh and eleventh are joined with the thirteenth,

The fourth after twice-ten is done <note>the 24th degree</note>, and the seventh <note>the 27th</note>, and both

**Scorpio** is accused in its first part, to which the third is added,

Doubling the eleventh <note>the 22nd degree</note> and that which is the twenty-fifth;

The air is said to be feared at twice-eight <note>the 16th degree</note> or in the tens <note>the 20th</note>,

or the seventh, or when the fourth figures thrice with the twelfth <note>The math here is symbolic; likely referring to the 7th, 4th, and 12th degrees.</note>.

This part of **Capricorn** is to be chosen <note>The poet uses "optanda" (desirable), perhaps ironically or as a rare positive degree amidst the "condemned" ones.</note>: the seventh and the ninth;
the one following the tenth agrees, which the third marks <note>the 13th degree</note>,

To be condemned is the first following the tenth <note>The poet moves to **Aquarius**.</note>,
and the third and the fifth, which is not hidden in the count;
and after twenty, the first <note>the 21st</note> and the twenty-fifth,

The third, the fifth, and the seventh of the **Fish** <note>Pisces</note> are like the Twins <note>Referring back to the dangerous degrees of Gemini</note>;
the eleventh must be feared, and the seventeenth joined to it,
and the fifth recalled into five numbers plus two <note>A poetic calculation for the 27th degree: (5x5)+2.</note>.

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical catalog of "condemned degrees" (the specific portions of the zodiac considered dangerous) to a more descriptive section on the character traits and destinies imparted by each of the twelve signs. We conclude the previous list of degrees—likely for the final signs of Aquarius and Pisces—and begin the character portraits for Aries (the Ram) and Taurus (the Bull).</meta>

Receiving [these] beyond the final degrees, it will bring things to be feared;
these parts lead a sterile life by both cold and fire,
stars <note>original: "vel," meaning "or"</note> dry <note>original: "vel," meaning "or"</note> or what moisture has overcome.
If swift Mars <note>original: "mauors"</note> hurls his fire into them,
Saturn takes up the ice, and Phoebus <note>The Sun</note> the heat.

**What all the signs bring about**

**Let this concern for the teachings of the signs leave you now.**
In their parts, certain things are changed by time and by their rising;
they receive their own strengths and give them back of their own accord.
For when **Aries** <note>The Ram</note> raises himself from the highest waves
and goes rich with his horns previously bent,
he will produce hearts content with their own wealth,
and will give spirits over to plunder and dissolve shame.
It delights [them] only to hear; thus he himself is carried onto his horns,
and he rushes and wavers, staying in no fixed seat.
Soft leisure delights them more than peaceful cares,
but it delights them to always cross through an unknown world,
to search out a new sea, and to be a guest
of the whole world. The wool-bearer <note>Aries</note> himself is your witness,
when, cleaving the glassy sea with his golden fleece,
he carried Phrixus <note>The mythological son of Athamas who escaped on the Golden Ram</note>—deprived of his sister <note>Helle, who fell into the sea now called the Hellespont</note> by the fates—
back to the banks of the Phasis and the kingdoms of Colchis.
But those whom the first stars of the rising **Taurus** <note>The Bull</note> create
lie feminine; and the cause is not to be sought far off,
if only it is lawful to seek through our causes.

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the zodiac signs and their influences on human character and destiny. Picking up from the description of Aries (the Ram) on the previous page, we move through the sequence of Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, and Virgo.</meta>

And when **the Bull** <note>Taurus</note> turns and enters the sky, rich with the maidens of the **Pleiades** <note>A cluster of seven stars in Taurus, often personified as the daughters of Atlas</note>, bringing back that small, clustering constellation.
Wealth returns once more, and their own resources assist them;
through the dew, he adorns the upturned fields with the plowshare.
If he brings forth the **Twins** <note>Gemini</note> with equal bonds,
this part will grant studious pursuits and lead the soul to the learned arts.
Nor does he create a gloomy disposition, but rather hearts steeped
in sweet charm; he trains them in the voice and the sounding lyre,
and joins these talents with a deep and noble soul.
But when the black **Crab** <note>Cancer</note> is carried along with a dark cloud—
in which the Sun <note>original: "phoebus"</note> seems to lose his fire, fire against fire,
and the star fails and is darkened by much gloom—
the eyes <note>original: "lumina"</note> will fail the limbs, and the fates will grant
a double death to those born then; yet each one both lives and carries
himself out, like a poor man eager to rise above the highest waves.
Should the **Lion** <note>Leo</note> climb the world with gaping jaws,
that man will be a debtor to both his father and his son; the wealth he himself
has seized, he will not bequeath, but will submerge his riches in himself.
So great a hunger for gold and such a harsh desire
seizes him that he may take for himself but never be satisfied,
and even the price of his own tomb might be recalled to provide a feast.
Rising from the opposite region, she who ruled the ancient ages—
**Justice** <note>The goddess Astraea, identified with the constellation Virgo</note>, who once fled the falling world—
now grants high summits and the loftiest peaks;
she will provide a ruler of laws and of sacred justice.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of the zodiac signs, picking up from the description of Virgo (Justice/Astraea) and moving through the celestial influences of Libra (the Balance), Scorpio, Sagittarius (the Archer), and the start of Capricorn (the Horned Goat).</meta>

...holy maidenhood inhabiting the temples of the gods <unclear>original: "divorum"</unclear>.
But when the autumnal **Claws** <note>original: "chelae"; refers to the constellation Libra, which was historically seen as the claws of the Scorpion</note> begin to rise,
happy is the man born under the equal weight of the **Balance** <note>Libra</note>.
As a judge, he will stand over the very limits of life, and by necessity
he will impose a yoke upon the lands and demand laws.
Cities and kingdoms will tremble before him, governed by his nod;
he alone shall remain, and after his life on earth, a life in heaven.
When the **Scorpion** <note>Scorpius</note> raises the lights of its tail's end,
if anyone is born while those stars are in favor,
he will expand the lands with cities and, with yoked oxen,
girded for the task, he will mark out the walls with a curved plow.
Or, he will level established cities and return the fields to the wild,
or restore towns and the exchange of grain to the houses.
So great will be both his virtue and the power <note>original: "potas", likely an abbreviation for "potestas"</note> accompanying that virtue.
Likewise, when the **Archer** <note>Sagittarius</note> rises with his first garments,
he will grant hearts renowned in war and great triumphs;
he would lead the conspicuous victor to the citadels.
But let him <note>the person born under this sign</note> avoid the sea <note>original: "aquas"</note> and walls already built.
For Fortune, too indulgent in favorable circumstances,
harbors envy in her face and rages most harshly against his brow.
Dreadful in war, such a victor—at **Trebia**, **Cannae**, and the **Lake** <note>Refers to Hannibal’s famous victories over Rome at the Trebia River, Cannae, and Lake Trasimene</note>—
weighed these things in his mind's eye before his eventual flight.
The last part in the sharpest point of the **Goat-Horn's** <note>Capricorn</note> tail
dictates military service upon the sea and the tending of ships;
harsh duties and the peril of a life that is never at rest <note>original: "inertis", here suggesting a life that cannot remain still or idle</note>.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of the zodiac signs from the previous page, finishing the descriptions of Aquarius and Pisces before moving into the geographical distribution of planetary and stellar influences—a field known as chorography.</meta>

And if you should wish for someone who is active, chaste, and upright,
such a person will be born for you when the first part of **Aquarius** <note>The Water-Bearer</note> emerges.
From here, so that the soul <note>original: "anus", likely a scribal error for "animus" (soul/mind) or "annus" (the year)</note> does not proceed to the first of the **Fish** <note>Pisces</note>,
a hateful talkativeness is granted, and a tongue like poison,
with malignant words always changing for new ears,
to carry accusations through the people with a double-tongued mouth.
No trust will reside in those born then, but rather the highest lust—
it is said that the soul <note>again "anus", likely "animus"</note> passes through the midst of burning fires.
For certainly **Cytherea** <note>An epithet for the goddess Venus, referring to her birth near the island of Cythera</note> marked herself in the form of a fish
when, submerged, she fled to the Babylonian waters <note>Refers to the Euphrates river</note>
to escape the snake-footed **Typhon** <note>A monstrous giant from Greek mythology who challenged the gods</note> who bears winged serpents;
she placed her own fires <note>The fires of passion/love</note> into the scaly fish.
This [dual nature] had belonged to the **Twins** <note>Gemini</note> alone, but with the rising of the **Fish**,
there was a sweet brother or sister, or the mother of the two. <note>Refers to the two fish of the constellation Pisces, often identified as Venus and her son Cupid/Eros</note>

## On the parts of the earth distributed to the signs.

Come now, learn of the stars that rule the divided lands,
but the total shape of things must be considered first.
The circle of the heaven is described in four parts:
the rising day <note>The East/Orient</note>, the setting day itself <note>The West/Occident</note>, and the midday heats <note>The South</note>,
and you, **Helice** <note>The constellation Ursa Major (the Great Bear), used to signify the North</note>, in your entirety. From these same parts
the winds break out and carry wars with them through the void.
Rough **Boreas** <note>The North Wind</note> rushes down from the axis; **Eurus** <note>The East Wind</note> flees from the sunrise;
**Auster** <note>The South Wind</note> loves the midday sun, and **Zephyrus** <note>The West Wind</note> the setting sun.
Between these four, in the middle parts, two breezes
breathe out similar blasts, though with changed names.

<summary>This page describes the astrological characteristics of those born under Aquarius and Pisces, including the myth of Venus's transformation into a fish, and introduces the division of the earth and winds according to the four cardinal directions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion of the zodiac signs (Aquarius and Pisces) into a poetic "tour" of the known world's geography. This segment describes how the Great Ocean flows into the Mediterranean and winds around the coasts of Africa, Europe, and into the Black Sea, setting the stage for how these regions are assigned to different astrological signs.</meta>

The Earth itself floats, encircled by a crown of the sea,
which girds the world in its center with liquid embraces.
Into its bays the sea receives waters which, admitted from the dark West <note>original: "vespere ab atro" - literally "from the dark evening," referring to the Atlantic entrance at the Strait of Gibraltar</note>,
wash on the right the **Numidians** and burning **Libya**,
and the citadels of once-great **Carthage**,
pulling back the winding shores into the shallow **Syrtis** <note>The Syrtis Major and Minor were notorious, shallow gulfs off the coast of Libya feared by ancient sailors for their sandbanks</note>.
It comes out again with its forward currents aimed toward the **Nile**.
To the left of the strait, the **Spanish** tribes give way to the sea,
and you, **Gaul**, clinging to neighboring lands,
and the cities of **Italy** curving into the waves on the right,
as far as your dogs, **Scylla**, and greedy **Charybdis** <note>The personified whirlpool and sea-monster traditionally located in the Strait of Messina between Italy and Sicily</note>.
Where the sea first pours itself through this gateway,
it flows out into the **Ionian** and wanders through the open waves;
and first pouring out to the left, it circles all of
**Italy** and the sea accompanied by the name of **Hadria** <note>The Adriatic Sea</note>.
It drinks the floods of the **Eridanus** <note>The poetic name for the River Po in Northern Italy</note> and cuts its path across the water.
It washes **Illyricum** and **Epirus** and famous **Corinth**,
and flies around the wide shores of the **Peloponnese**.
Again it flows back into the **Ionian** and, with a vast retreat,
passes the borders of **Thessaly** and the **Achaean** fields.
Here you find the sea and the strait of the **drowned girl** <note>The Hellespont (Dardanelles), named after Helle, a mythological princess who fell from the Golden Ram into the sea here</note>;
the reluctant jaws of the **Propontis** <note>The Sea of Marmara</note> are said to be
joined by a narrow passage to the **Euxine Sea** <note>The Black Sea</note> and the waters of **Maeotis** <note>The Sea of Azov</note>,
which remains joined at the back and feeds the sea.
Now where the sailor looks back at himself in the narrow straits...

<summary>This page provides a poetic survey of the Mediterranean basin, tracing the coastline from the Strait of Gibraltar past North Africa, Italy, and Greece, and ending at the Black Sea. It serves as a geographical foundation for the author's system of astrological chorography.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic survey of the world's geography according to the Roman poet Manilius. Having previously described the Mediterranean and the lands of Asia and Egypt, the text now moves toward the Caspian Sea, the Indian Ocean (the Red Sea and Persian Gulf), and finally the continent of Africa, focusing on the historical might of Carthage and the natural dangers of the desert.</meta>

The sea is a neighbor, lest it should conquer the whole earth with its waters.
For where it drinks in the North Wind <note>original: "boream"</note> and the summer sun gleaming,
the wave is <gloss>3</gloss> passable in a <gloss>1</gloss> long, <gloss>2</gloss> narrow sea,
and reaches [its end] and then finally pours out in open fields,
making the **Caspian** waters similar to a small sea.

hold the title of the sea, though shut off from those [other] waters,
which the place itself rules and pours out wide into the world.
This [sea] reaches <gloss>1</gloss> far <gloss>2</gloss> into the <gloss>3</gloss> soft **Arabians** and the land
bearing delights: <gloss>1</gloss> various <gloss>2</gloss> roots and <gloss>3</gloss> new colors <note>A reference to the exotic spices and dyes for which Arabia was famous in antiquity</note>.
It sends them out and pours them forth where the twin shores meet the sea,
and the sea there has the name of the land—that land in the middle of the two.
Once it was the kingdom of **Carthage**, a power under arms,
in which when **Hannibal** crushed the **Alban** heights <note>A poetic reference to the hills of Rome, signifying Hannibal's invasion of Italy</note>,
he made the **Trebia** and **Cannae** into eternal tombs <note>The Trebia (218 BCE) and Cannae (216 BCE) were two of Rome's most devastating military defeats at the hands of Carthage</note>,
and he overwhelmed them and poured **Libya** <note>The Roman term for the continent of Africa</note> into Italian cities.
Here the land has granted various plagues: dreadful monsters and wild beasts
destined for future wars; for it is infested with
horrendous snakes, their <gloss>3</gloss> limbs <gloss>1</gloss> irritated <gloss>2</gloss> with poison,
and the offspring of death, the living crimes of the earth.
And it has vast elephants and savage lions;
it brings forth horrid wars to be borne as its own punishment,
and plays at the portentous births of **Cyclopes** <note>In ancient lore, the edges of the world were often populated with legendary giants or monstrous races</note>,
and dread haunts the narrow passes of the sterile sands.

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic geography of the ancient world. Having previously described the southern and eastern reaches near the Red Sea and the lands of the Carthaginians, the poet now moves northward through the riches of Asia and the mountains of the East before crossing into Europe to catalog the famous city-states of Greece and the rising power of Rome.</meta>

Until [the sea] sets its laws for the settlers of **Egypt**. 
From there come the peoples of **Asia** and a land wealthy above all others, where golden rivers flow and sparkle with gems, and fruit-bearing, scented forests breathe out healing medicines <note>The poet refers to the exotic spices and resins like frankincense and myrrh found in the East</note>. **India** is a lesser southern world, as is the land of the **Parthians**.

There is another world formed by the walls of the **Taurus** <note>The Taurus Mountains in modern-day Turkey</note> rising into the sky, with so many nations divided by name around it. There are the waters that separate the **Scythian** cities, the **Maeotic** lake <note>The Sea of Azov</note>, the rough waters of the **Euxine** sea <note>The Black Sea</note>, and the **Hellespont** <note>The Dardanelles</note> beyond the furthest reach of the **Propontis** <note>The Sea of Marmara</note>. 
Here, nature has placed the boundary of powerful **Asia**.

What remains, **Europe** holds. She first received **Jove** <note>Jupiter, who in myth transformed into a bull to carry the princess Europa across the sea</note> as he swam through the waves; she bore the weight of the bull, aiding him with her beauty. He bestowed the girl's name upon the waves and consecrated this monument to himself as a title of his love <note>The poet explains that the continent of Europe is named after the maiden Europa as a tribute to Jupiter's affection for her</note>.

This land is greatest in its men and most fertile in learned cities: **Athens**, with its shores flourishing into a kingdom; **Sparta** with its strength; and **Thebes**, once divided under a single king. That land of **Greece**, the first home of the **Trojan War**; **Thessaly** and powerful **Epirus**, and the neighboring shores of **Illyria** and **Thrace**, which claimed **Mars** <note>The Roman god of war</note> as its inhabitant. And **Germany**, astonished by its own offspring; **Gaul** <note>Specifically Gallia Narbonensis, the Roman province in southern France</note>; **Spain**, greatest in wars; and at the summit, **Italy**, where **Rome** is the supreme ruler.

<summary>This page concludes the description of Asia’s boundaries and begins a survey of Europe, detailing the mythological origin of the continent's name and listing the prominent regions of Greece and the West, culminating in the praise of Rome.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic geography of Marcus Manilius. The previous page ended while describing the great cities of Greece, specifically mentioning Athens and likely Sparta. This page transitions from the physical description of Europe to the astrological concept of "chorography"—the idea that different regions of the world are governed by specific zodiac signs, just as different parts of the human body are.</meta>

**I** mposed upon the lands, she <note>Europe</note> is herself joined to the heavens;
**H** ere at the ends of the world, the sea must be summoned,
**W** hich God divides into parts and individual stars,
**A** nd to each he gave kingdoms of protection throughout the world,
**A** nd added specific nations and high cities,
**I** n which the stars might exert their preeminent powers.
**A** nd just as the human figure is mapped out by the signs—
**A** lthough a common protection extends through the whole
**B** ody, yet specific powers exist in each divided part—
**S** o **Aries** clings to the head, and **Taurus** to the neck;
**T** he arms are reckoned under **Gemini**, and the breast under **Cancer**;
**T** he shoulders call for you, **Leo** <note>original: "nemee", referring to the Nemean Lion</note>, and the flanks for you, **Virgo**;
**L** ibra cherishes the buttocks, and **Scorpio** reigns in the groin;
**T** he Archer <note>Sagittarius</note> loves the thighs, and **Capricorn** the knees;
**T** he Youth <note>Aquarius, the water-bearer</note> defends the shins, and **Pisces** the feet.
**I** n this same way, one star or another claims various lands for itself.
**F** or this reason, the race of men is arranged into various laws 
**A** nd various physical forms; nations are formed with their own
**C** oloring, and their legal rights are associated through their limbs,
**W** hich nature marks with the same material under a private pact.
**G** olden-haired **Germany** rises up through her vast offspring,
**W** ho are stained with a ruddiness too much like their neighbors;
**A** harsher climate contracts the sturdy limbs of the **Spaniards**;
**A** warlike part of the world shapes the faces of the **Romans**,
**W** hich, mixing a certain sturdiness, tempers their limbs well.

<meta>This page continues the astrological geography (chorography) of Marcus Manilius from Book 4 of the *Astronomica*.
