Through her ingenious peoples, refined **Greece**
presents many gymnasia <note>ancient centers for both athletic and intellectual training</note> and strong wrestling schools;
and curly hair twisted at the temples betrays the **Syrians**.
The **Ethiopians** mark the world with their dark features,
and **India** produces sun-baked nations, a gift of people drenched in heat.
Between these, the climate regenerates and creates a middle type with a moderate character.
The broader land of **Egypt**, swimming in the **Nile**,
gently darkens bodies in its irrigated fields.
The **Carthaginian** <note>original: "Poenus"</note> in sandy lands, and the **African** with dust,
parches those caught in the heat; and **Mauritania**’s very name
and appearance holds its title, derived from its own color <note>original: "Mauritania... color"; Manilius refers to the etymology of "Mauri" (Moors) from the Greek "mauros," meaning dark or black.</note>.

Add to this the sounds of voices, and include just as many languages;
and the customs and rituals of places, paired by their lot.
Add the specific types of crops sown from the seed,
and the grain <note>original: "Cererem", referring to Ceres, the goddess of agriculture</note> returning with varying harvests across the globe—
for not all lands bring back legumes with equal strength,
nor do you, **Bacchus** <note>god of wine</note>, gift all lands with an equal bounty,
but rather you pour forth different grapes on different hills.
Nor does cinnamon grow everywhere in all fields.
Consider the diverse appearances of cattle and the unique traits of wild beasts,
and the elephants enclosed in their massive bodies by the earth <note>original: "duplici corpore", likely referring to the elephant's enormous size or the "double" nature of its trunk and body</note>.
As many parts as there are of the globe, under just as many parts of the sky,
the stars shine, mapped out by divine nets <note>referring to the coordinate systems or "grids" used by ancient astronomers to map the heavens</note>.
They drench the nations associated with them in their own sea—
The **Ram** <note>original: "Laniger", the wool-bearer, i.e., Aries</note>, having been allotted the stars in the center of the world...

<meta>This page continues Marcus Manilius's "astrological geography" from Book 4 of the *Astronomica*. is **Rhodes**, the guest-house of the prince <note>A reference to the future Emperor Tiberius, who lived in self-imposed exile on Rhodes</note> who would rule the world;
and you, Rhodes, are truly the home of the **Sun**, to whom you are entirely sacred <gloss>3</gloss>,
since they received their light under a great **Caesar** of the world.
Included are the cities of **Ionia** and the **Doric** countrysides,
the ancient **Arcadians**, and **Caria**, celebrated in fame <gloss>3</gloss>.
What sign should I say rather rules **Italy**, if you are selecting a star? <gloss>3</gloss>

<meta>This page continues Marcus Manilius's astrological geography from Book 4 of the *Astronomica*. And because he <note>Libra, the Scales</note> rules all things, since he knows the weights of things,
He designates the totals and separates the unequal from the equal;
Under him the seasons hang, and where night and day yield to each other.
**Libra** holds **Hesperia** <note>original: "Hesperiam"; a poetic name for Italy, the "Land of the West"</note> as his own, where **Rome** was founded,
And he retains the distinctions of the world and its imperial affairs <note>original: "impium," likely a scribal error for "imperium" (empire)</note>;

Where **Remus**, born with his brother <note>Romulus</note>, founded this city;
She <note>Rome</note> curbs the hanging world with her own nods of command.
Below, the sign <note>rules</note> the citadels of conquered **Carthage**,

Tears up the **Tyrrhenian** <note>Etruscan/Western Italian</note> citadels, yet he looks back toward the waves of **Italy**.
He holds **Sardinia** and the lands spread across the sea.
The **Gnosian** land <note>Crete</note>, surrounded by the **Cretan** sea,

He himself comes as a double sign; hence **Crete** claims swift arrows,
And she imitates the stretched bows of the constellation.
The island of **Trinacria** <note>Sicily, the "three-pointed" island</note>, aided by her floating sister <note>possibly a reference to the nearby islands or the myth of Arethusa</note>,
Follows **Crete**, founded under the same sign;
She is closest to **Italy** and divided by only a shallow depth,
She follows the laws of the earlier shore; she was not snatched away by the star.
You, **Capricorn**, rule whatever lies under the setting sun,
Whatever is exposed, and whatever touches the icy **Helice** <note>The Great Bear/Ursa Major, signifying the North</note> from that direction:
The **Spanish** peoples, or whatever rich **Gaul** brings forth,
And you, **Germany**, a mother worthy of wild beasts.

He <note>Capricorn, the Sea-Goat</note> claims a dual sign for both the land and the sea,
Holding the earth and the ocean with unceasing tides.
But the youth <note>Aquarius, the Water-Bearer</note>, formed more softly with a naked bow <note>original: "arcu"; likely a scribal error for "urceo" (urn), the traditional vessel of Aquarius</note>,

Took possession in the waves when **Venus** fled from **Typhon** <note>In Greek myth, Venus and Cupid escaped the monster Typhon by transforming into fish in the Euphrates river</note>.
A vast and rare land surrounded by great riverbanks,
The **Parthians**, and the nations conquered by the **Parthians** through the ages:
**Bactria**, and **Aria** <note>original: "herios"</note>, **Babylon**, **Susa**, and the **Persians** <note>original: "panos"</note>,
And shores <note>original: "nora," likely "ora"</note> scarcely to be embraced by countless forms,
And the **Tigris**, and the radiant shores of the **Red Sea**.
Thus the earth remains divided through all the ages,
From which the laws of influence are to be drawn into their own parts.
For they preserve the same relationships that exist among the signs,
And they come together or clash in hatred;
Now opposite on the pole, and now shared in a **trine** <note>term: trine - an astrological aspect where signs are 120 degrees apart, indicating harmony</note>,
Whatever other cause governs various effects.
Here lands correspond to lands, spheres to spheres,
Shores to shores, and kingdoms to opposing kingdoms.
Thus there will be a place to be avoided and one to be sought by each person;
Thus is loyalty to be hoped for, and thus are dangers to be feared,
As the race of mortals <note>original: "genus interrum," likely "genus in terris"</note> descends from on high.

<meta>This page continues the fourth book of Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*. While the previous page focused on the geographical distribution of the zodiac signs across the known world, this passage transitions into a philosophical and scientific reflection on the cyclical nature of the cosmos. It moves from the static division of lands to the dynamic, ever-changing nature of the Earth and the heavens, using both mythology and early astronomy to explain these shifts.</meta>

Do you not see <note>original: "H omine," a scribal error or misreading of "Nonne"</note> how things, worn down through certain years,
Sometimes cease, numbed by a sterile motion?
Clearly, in the immense passage of time, nothing remains equal,
Nothing holds its flowers <note>original: "flores"; often "honores" (glories/beauty) in other manuscripts</note> perpetually or keeps a single color.
Instead, all things change as they fluctuate in the world for a long time:
The fields, once fertile, survive on their own crops,
Then, exhausted, they deny continuous births by their creating;
In turn, lands which had been sterile for seeds
Later supply new harvests, with no one demanding tribute <note>A metaphor for the earth producing crops freely without the "tax" of intensive labor</note>.
The earth is shaken, held together by its various structures;
The ground is withdrawn from under our feet, and the world floats within itself.
The Ocean arrives, and thirsting for the sea, it breaks it apart,
Nor can it contain itself. Thus once did it sweep away the cities
Of the human race, when the lone heir remained—
**Deucalion** <note>In Greek myth, Deucalion was the son of Prometheus who survived a great flood sent by Zeus to end the Bronze Age</note>, who possessed the world on a single rock.
So too, when **Phaethon** <note>The son of the Sun-god Helios who attempted to drive the solar chariot, lost control, and nearly scorched the earth</note> first attempted the reins:
The nations burned, and heaven feared the fires;
The burning stars fled the new flames,
And Nature <note>original: "nã," abbreviation for "natura"</note> feared being buried in a single tomb.
To such an extent do all things change over a long time,
And then return into themselves again; thus in a fixed time
The signs <note>The constellations of the zodiac</note> also lose their strength and regain what was taken.
The cause is clear why the **Moon** fails among her stars,
Bereft of her brother <note>The Sun, often called Phoebus or Apollo</note> and plunged into the shadows of night,
When the middle orb <note>The Earth</note> intercepts the rays of **Phoebus**.

<summary>This page describes the instability and cyclical transformation of the physical world, citing the myths of Deucalion's flood and Phaethon's fire as evidence of past cataclysms. It concludes by explaining that even the stars and the moon follow these cycles, specifically describing a lunar eclipse as the Earth blocking the Sun's light.</summary>
<keywords>cosmic cycles, Deucalion, Phaethon, lunar eclipse, Phoebus, fertility, geological change, Manilius</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*, specifically transitioning from the discussion of the physical world’s instability to the astronomical phenomena of eclipses. On the previous page, the poet described cataclysms like the flood of Deucalion and the fire of Phaethon. Here, he examines how the zodiac signs respond to the "failure" (eclipse) of the moon and sun, and begins a philosophical inquiry into whether human reason can truly grasp the divine order of Fate.</meta>

This shadow draws toward the assembly where the **Delian moon** <note>original: "delia luna"; an epithet for the goddess Diana/the Moon, who was born on the island of Delos</note> shines.
These signs likewise languish along with their own star <note>The "star" here refers to the Moon itself</note>,
Weakened at the same time and deprived of their usual vigor;
And they mourn **Phoebe** <note>original: "phœben"; another name for the Moon</note> as if she were being carried out to her funeral.
Nature itself teaches the names and causes of the **ecliptic signs** <term>eclyptica signa: zodiac signs in which the Sun or Moon can be eclipsed because they lie on the ecliptic path</term>.
The ancients spoke of them; likewise, two signs suffer at once,
Not those neighboring in position, but those which shine in opposition.
Just as the moon then fails in its orb only
When it does not see the Sun <note>original: "phœbum"; Phoebus Apollo, the Sun</note> running with the stars opposite.
Nor do all things languish with an equal heart,
But sometimes the whole year is drawn out, infected by the omen;
Now the weary stars remain for a shorter time, nor do they stay longer,
And the **lunar times** <note>original: "phœbeia t̅pa"</note> depart by their own occurrence.
And when whatever space has been appointed is completed,
And they have fulfilled their labors in their assigned station,
Two signs shine across the sky in opposition.
Then the neighboring signs, clinging to those very signs, falter,
Which first come upon the lands and leave the lands.
Not so that it fights as an adversary to the starry orb,
But because the universe drives its courses, it too inclines,
And it denies the rights and great gifts that have been lost.
Nor does it return the crimes of the **Scythians** <note>A nomadic people of antiquity often used as a symbol for distant or "barbaric" lands</note>; the place suffers all the defects.

## That the reason of the Fates can be perceived:

*But what does it profit us to scrutinize the world with such subtle reasoning,*
*If each person's own mind resists?* <note>original: "si mens sua cuiq̅ repugnat"; Manilius is questioning the limits of human intellect against the complexities of the universe</note>

<summary>This page describes the astronomical mechanics and astrological impact of lunar eclipses, specifically how signs in opposition are affected. It concludes by shifting into a philosophical defense of studying the 'Reason of Fate' despite the difficulty of the subject.</summary>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical conclusion of Marcus Manilius's fourth book of the *Astronomica*. Following the discussion of eclipses and the signs of the zodiac, the poet now shifts to a profound Stoic defense of human reason. He argues that because the universe is a rational, divine organism, the human mind—which contains a spark of that same divinity—is capable of understanding the heavens. This passage transitions from the mechanics of the sky to the spiritual relationship between man (the microcosm) and the universe (the macrocosm).</meta>

### The Divinity of the Human Mind

Fear takes away hope and prohibits us from the very threshold of heaven.
For whatever **Nature** <note>original: "natura"</note> hides in her vast retreat,
Escaping both mortal sight and our own hearts,
This can still be of benefit, since all things are ruled by **Fates** <term>fatis: the Stoic concept of an unbreakable, divine chain of cause and effect</term>.
Since Fate can be moved by no reason or plea,
What does it help to be carried away by one's own self-reproaches,
And to defraud oneself of the goods which not even God himself envies?
Why should we lay aside the eyes of the mind which Nature gave?
Why not look upon the transparent sky and the gifts of heaven,
And let the world descend deep into its own inner substance?
To understand how such a great mass is composed from its own seeds,
And how the space of heaven is sustained by its own nourishment,
To follow the furthest harbor and go beneath the tracts
Of the hanging earth, and to live in the whole world?
How great a part of **Reason** <note>original: "rationis"</note> remains to be learned from the night?
Now nothing lies hidden; for we have seen through the whole universe,
And we possess the captured world and our own parent;
Being ourselves a conspicuous part of the world, we, the created, approach the stars.

Is it a matter of doubt that a god dwells within our breast?
And that our souls return to heaven and come from heaven?
And just as the world is constructed from every element—
Of **ether** <term>ætheris: the "fifth element," a divine, fiery substance that makes up the stars and the upper atmosphere</term> and highest fire, of earth and sea—
And a swift spirit in the whole governs with justice,
So too in us there are earthly bodies of fate,
And life-giving souls of blood, and a **Mind** <note>original: "animum"; here referring to the higher rational faculty as opposed to the "anima" or biological soul</note> which governs all things.

<summary>Manilius argues that humans are a "microcosm" containing a spark of the divine, which grants us the unique ability to study and understand the celestial laws of Fate. He concludes that the human mind is essentially a god dwelling within a mortal body, mirroring the way the divine spirit governs the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Stoicism, Microcosm, Fate, Divine Reason, Nature, Human Soul, Astronomy, Ether</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical conclusion of Marcus Manilius's fourth book of the *Astronomica*. The poet is expanding on the Stoic doctrine that humans can understand the universe because we are made of the same divine substance as the stars.</meta>

### The Microcosm: Man as a Reflection of the Universe

God <note>original: "Dispensatque," implying the divine management of the universe</note> governs mankind: what wonder is it that we can know the world,
If humans can possess the world itself within them?
Each person is a miniature example of God, an image in small proportions.
Could such a nature be born of anything but heaven?
Other living creatures lie prostrate and face the ground,
Either submerged in the depths of the earth, or suspended in the air.
A single stillness comes to them all; their senses spread through their limbs,
But because they lack **Reason** <term>consilium: the ability to deliberate and use logic</term>, their tongues are silent.
Man alone has a vision of things and the power of speech;
A capable intellect leads him into various arts.
This "offspring" <note>Man, as the child of the universe</note> who rules all has gone out into the world
And tamed the earth for harvests; he has captured the wild beasts,
And imposed a path upon the sea. He alone stands upon the citadel of the world,
Erect of head; as a victor, he directs his gaze toward the stars.
With eyes like stars, he is the first to look upon the heavens <note>original: "olympus"</note>
And seeks out God <note>original: "iouem," referring to Jupiter or the supreme divine mind</note>. Not content with the surface of the gods,
Or just the visible world, he searches for the divine even in its depths.
Following his own kindred nature, he seeks himself among the stars.

Why should we demand less faith in this science than we give to birds,
Whose flight we watch, or to the trembling organs <term>fibrae: the entrails of sacrificed animals used by Roman priests to predict the future</term> beneath a breast?
Is it not a greater thing to derive reason from the sacred signs of the sky,
Than to attend to the death of cattle or the songs of birds?
And for this reason, God does not begrudge the sight of heaven to the world;
He reveals his own face and his own body to us.
By always revolving, he impresses and offers himself to our sight.

<summary>Manilius argues that humans are a "microcosm" (a small world) containing a spark of the divine, which grants us the unique ability to study the stars and understand God’s plan through astrology.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, Stoicism, Reason, divination, human nature, divinity, astrology</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page concludes Book IV and begins Book V of Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*. It completes the Stoic argument from the previous page: that the smallness of the human body does not limit the vastness of the human mind, which is capable of encompassing the entire universe because it is guided by Reason.</meta>

### The Power of Reason and the Sight of the Soul

So that the world might be well known and teach through the act of seeing, <margin>4</margin>
Observe its laws and the manner in which it moves. <margin>1</margin>
The universe itself calls our minds to the stars; <margin>2</margin>
It allows this because it does not wish its laws to remain hidden. <margin>A</margin>
Who thinks it a sin to be born into that which it is holy to see? <margin>3</margin>
Do not despise your own powers because of your small body:
What is powerful is often immense. Thus, small weights of gold <margin>1</margin>
Exceed in price numerous heaps of bronze. <margin>8</margin>
So the diamond <note>original: "adamas," referring to the hardest known gemstone, likely a diamond or white sapphire</note>, a mere speck of stone, is more precious than gold. <margin>p</margin>
So the tiny **pupil** of the eye surveys the entire heaven; <margin>1</margin>
And what the eyes themselves are is tiny, though they perceive the greatest of things. <margin>8</margin>
So the seat of the mind, located beneath the slender heart, <margin>8</margin>
Reigns throughout the whole body from a narrow boundary.
Do not seek the quantity of matter, but look at its powers, <margin>3</margin>
Which **Reason** <term>ratio: the divine logic that governs the universe and the human mind</term> possesses, not weight. Does Reason not conquer all? <margin>2</margin>
Do not hesitate to believe that the visions of men are divine. <margin>f</margin>
Man himself now makes gods and sends divinity to the stars, <margin>1</margin>
And the heavens grow greater under an **august prince** <note>A reference to the Roman Emperor—likely Tiberius, though using the title "Augustus"—suggesting that the deification of the imperial family literally adds new divinity to the sky</note>. <margin>1</margin>

<margin>M</margin> ->MARCUS MANILIUS, ASTRONOMICA, BOOK FOUR<- <margin>A</margin>
->HERE ENDS THE FOURTH BOOK: HERE BEGINS THE FIFTH BOOK BY THE SAME AUTHOR<-

<image-desc>A large decorative blue initial H begins the first word of the new book.</image-desc>
<margin>h</margin> Here is another end to the work, the signs having been related, <margin>A</margin>
Through which the five divine powers of the wandering stars <note>The five planets known to the ancients: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> move, <margin>A</margin>
Along with **Phoebus** <note>The Sun</note> in his four-horse chariot and **Delia** <note>The Moon, often associated with the goddess Diana who was born on the island of Delos</note> in her two-horse chariot.
I would have built my work no further, having returned from the heavens, <margin>f</margin>
And by a descent run down through the middle of the signs. <margin>1</margin>

<summary>This page concludes Book IV with a philosophical defense of human intellect, arguing that the mind's small physical size is irrelevant to its divine power to understand the cosmos. It then transitions into the opening of Book V, which focuses on the planets and the sun and moon.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, Reason (Ratio), human divinity, Augustus, Phoebus, Delia, planets, Stoic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the end of Book IV, where the author established the divine capacity of the human mind. It serves as the opening (proem) to Book V of the *Astronomica*, transitioning from the logic of the universe to a poetic catalog of the extra-zodiacal constellations—the stars that lie outside the central path of the Sun and planets.</meta>

### The Celestial Path and the Catalog of Stars

Of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, and the Sun beneath them;
After Venus, and you, wandering Moon, who follow the son of Maia <note>original: "Maia natum," referring to Mercury, the son of the nymph Maia</note>.
The universe commands me to hasten my path; around all things 
The stars fly, running their course through the whole sky,
Once they have been commanded to climb their ethereal chariots
And have reached the highest summit, following their own tracks.
From here, Orion calls—a most massive part of the great heaven—
And the ship of heroes <note>the *Argo Navis*, the ship of Jason and the Argonauts</note> which now sails among the stars,
And the Rivers winding their wandering bends far and wide,
And the double-natured sea monster <note>original: "coetum," likely a variation of *cetum*, referring to Cetus the Whale</note> with scales and a terrible mouth,
And the watchful guardian of the Hesperides and their rich gold <note>the constellation Draco, the dragon who guarded the golden apples</note>,
And the Dog <note>Sirius, the Dog Star</note> carrying fires into the whole world.
There is the Altar of the gods, where heaven pays its vows,
Now the Snake that glides between the twin Bears,
And the Charioteer <term>Heniochus: the Greek name for the constellation Auriga</term> mindful of his car, and Bootes of his wagon,
And the celestial gift of Ariadne’s Crown,
And the victor Perseus with the hated scythe that slew Medusa.
And Cepheus the father, with his wife, dooms Andromeda <note>original: "necat," literally "kills." This refers to the mythological moment where her parents sacrifice her to the sea monster to appease the gods, though some manuscripts suggest "dedicates" or "binds" instead.</note>,
And where the starry Dolphin flies from the waters, and the swift Arrow,
And the hull of the ship <note>the constellation Puppis</note> standing with its deck protected,
And all the other things gliding here and there through the whole sky.
All these I must sing of in their own right:
What power they hold at their rising, what when they sink into the waves,
And what each pair of divided stars <term>bifidis astris: constellations that are split into parts or appear as pairs, such as the Gemini or perhaps the stars that rise alongside the Zodiac</term> brings back,
As I govern <note>or "describe"</note> the specific powers and times for the stars.

<summary>In this opening to Book V, the poet Manilius transitions from the planets to a grand catalog of the constellations lying outside the Zodiac, setting the stage for a detailed explanation of their astrological influences.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, constellations, Orion, Perseus, Andromeda, Argo Navis, Mercury, planetary order, celestial myth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of extra-zodiacal constellations in Book V of the *Astronomica*. The previous page ended with the description of Andromeda’s peril; this page shifts to the constellation Argo Navis (the ship of the Argonauts) and the character of those born under its influence, specifically their mastery of the sea and the historical naval battles that the stars have ordained.</meta>

### The Celestial Ship and the Masters of the Deep

The creator of great Olympus <note>original: "fabricator olympi," referring to the divine architect of the universe</note> once established 
This distinguished man and victor of the sea, to whom—having left his own land—
<margin>itself</margin>The stars gave a name and a burden; nor was he free from that famous fleece
Of Colchis, and the magic arts of the Colchian <note>Colchis was a region on the Black Sea, the home of Medea</note>
Medea, whom he commanded, and who spread her poisons throughout the world.
And hither the neighboring stern <note>the constellation Argo Navis is often represented only by its stern</note> is led through the stars 
By the region of the right side, just as the *Argo* itself might sail.
But when the stern first rises in its own fires,
When the Horn-bearer <note>original: "corniger," referring to the zodiac sign Aries, the Ram</note> has raised his face in four degrees,
Whoever is born on earth at that rising
Will be a ruler of a ship, clinging unmoving to the tiller;
He will exchange lands for the sea and, as a seeker of the winds,
He will wish to sail across the whole deep with his fortune,
To see other months and another Phasis <note>a river in Colchis associated with the Argonauts' journey</note> with his fleets,
And to surpass even Tiphys <term>Tiphys: the legendary pilot of the *Argo*, the first navigator in Greek myth</term>, who trembled with caution.
Take away these births of men, and you would take away
The Trojan war from such a star, and the fleet released
By blood <note>a reference to the sacrifice of Iphigenia to appease the winds</note>; nor would the land be driven by the waves.
Xerxes would not have looked upon the stars with envy, nor made the sea be covered <note>Xerxes famously "punished" the sea and bridged the Hellespont with ships</note>.
True Salamis <note>the site of the Greek naval victory over Persia</note> would not have submerged Athens at Syracuse,
Nor would the Punic <note>Carthaginian</note> prows have floated upon every sea,
Nor would the world have hung suspended between the two Actian bays <note>referring to the Battle of Actium between Octavian and Mark Antony</note>,
And the very fortune of heaven would not have swam upon the deep.
Under these leaders, fleets are guided through the blind sea,
And the earth itself is joined together through its use.

<summary>This passage describes the constellation Argo Navis and its astrological influence, asserting that those born under it are destined to be master navigators and that the great naval conflicts of history were dictated by these stars.</summary>
<keywords>Argo Navis, Jason, Medea, Golden Fleece, navigation, Aries, Tiphys, Xerxes, Battle of Salamis, Battle of Actium, astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of extra-zodiacal constellations (stars outside the zodiac belt) and their astrological influences on human character. The previous page concluded the description of the Argo Navis and the seafaring nature of those born under it. This page moves to the mighty constellation Orion and the Charioteer, Heniochus.</meta>

### Orion and the Charioteer

The world is summoned by kings sent upon the winds.
But rising from the highest part of the left side,
**M**ighty Orion <note>original: "Orion," one of the most recognizable constellations, depicted as a giant hunter</note> embraces the vast heavens.
When he is shining, he draws day across the lands and the sky;
**N**ight, counterfeiting the day, draws in her black wings.
He will fashion clever minds and swift bodies,
**A**nd a mind agile in duty and care through all things.
You will see them hurrying with a heart that never tires.
**H**e will be like a gateway and will be known throughout the whole world,
**F**litting across thresholds and carrying a single word to all people;
**A** common friend, bringing the morning greeting.

**B**ut when the Ram <term>Aries: the first sign of the zodiac, represented by the golden ram</term> lifts itself from the earth,
**A**fter five degrees have passed, it first raises its heights from the waves.
**H**eniochus <term>Heniochus: the Greek name for the constellation Auriga, the Charioteer</term> pulls his wheels up from the bottom of the slope,
**W**here the icy North Wind <note>original: "Boreas," the personification of the cold northern wind</note> is like sharp blasts.
He will grant his own devotion, retaining in the sky
**T**hose arts which he, as a driver, loved previously upon the earth:
To stand in a light chariot, managing four mouths 
**R**eined by foamy bits <note>original: "lupatis," a jagged horse-bit designed to look like wolf teeth</note> and to turn the horses'
**P**owerful strength, and to bind them in a twisted circle.
But when the gates have flown open from the loosened hinge <note>a reference to the *carceres*, the starting stalls of a Roman circus</note>,
**T**o drive the wild steeds and to lean forward to pass those who fly ahead,
And scarcely to touch the top of the field with light wheels,
**S**urpassing the winds with their feet; and holding the first place 
In the ranks, to match the spiteful turns of the course.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influence of the constellations Orion and Auriga (Heniochus), noting that Orion produces industrious messengers and socialites, while Auriga produces skilled chariot racers and horsemen.</summary>
<keywords>Orion, Auriga, Heniochus, Aries, astrology, chariot racing, constellations, Roman social life, Boreas</keywords>

### The Arts of the Charioteer and Circus

**A**nd to block an opponent with a delay, practicing the maneuvers of the whole track,
**O**r now to pass through the midst of the crowd in right-hand circles;
**E**ntrusting himself to the open field, he rushes past the sharp curve of the goal-post <note>original: "meta," the conical turning post at each end of a Roman circus track</note>
**A**nd hangs his doubtful hope upon the final turn of fate.
**U**nder no other star does the vaulter <term>desultor: a circus performer who leaps from one moving horse to another</term> 
**L**earn to plant his feet so firmly upon the backs of four-footed beasts;
**H**e runs across these horses, playing upon the backs of the "flyers" as they gallop.
**O**r, carried by a single horse, now he will wield weapons in mock combat;
**N**ow he pursues prizes throughout the length of the circus track—
**W**hatever skill is shaped or mastered by such a dedicated study.
**F**rom here comes Salmoneus <note>original: "Salmoneus," a mythical king of Elis who reached such heights of arrogance that he tried to impersonate Jupiter</note>, who imitated heaven in his circuit;
**W**ith man-made mountains built and four-horse chariots driven across a bronze bridge,
**H**e seemed to have replicated the very sound of the universe, and even
**F**ancied he had driven Jupiter himself from the earth by faking the lightning bolt.
**B**ut he felt the immense fires of the true heaven upon his own head;
**I**n death, he finally learned the difference between himself and the almighty Jupiter.
**Y**ou might believe Bellerophon <note>original: "Bellerophontem," the hero who tamed the winged horse Pegasus and attempted to fly to Olympus</note> was born from this star,
**H**e who set a path through the world, flying among the constellations;
**F**or him, the sky was a field, while the lands and the sea 
**W**ere far below his feet, and the ground bore no tracks of his passing.
**N**ow you must sing of the rising form of the Charioteer <term>Heniochus: the Greek name for the constellation Auriga</term> as he ascends;
**W**hen the Ram <note>the zodiac sign Aries</note> has doubled ten degrees of its rising (reaching the twentieth degree),
**T**hey begin to bring forth the trembling fear of the sky. 
**O**nly then do these stars finally turn their backs upon the earth,
**W**here the favorable North Wind <note>original: "boreas"</note> breathes—though do not trust his severity...

<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions from the previous page, shifting focus from the grand spectacles of the circus and the arrogance of Salmoneus to the subtler, more deceptive influences of the "Kids" (Haedi) and the "Hyades" constellations. It contrasts outward appearance with inner character and details the professions of shepherds and political agitators.</meta>

### The Influence of the Kids and the Hyades

**D**o not believe that men shaped by the facade of this sign <note>The "sign" refers to the Kids, part of the constellation Auriga</note> are Catos <note>original: "Catones," referring to Cato the Elder or Younger, symbols of stern Roman morality</note>,
**O**r a Brutus, or a Tarquin, or capable of the legendary deeds of Horatius.
**T**he burden of this sign is the Kids: such weighty virtues do not suit
**T**hese butting youngsters; their loins are restless, and lewdness marks
**T**heir hearts. They sweat for agile luxuries and expend their vigor
**I**n varied loves, leading their youth through a maze of desire.
<unclear>A</unclear> face is never a guarantee of virtue; instead, lust often
<unclear>D</unclear>rives them, and a shameful pleasure is bought at the price of death.
**A**nd to have fallen is the least of their evils; it is being conquered by their own crimes that is worse.

**T**hey also grant a love for the care of flocks to those born under them,
**P**roducing the shepherd, upon whose neck the pan-pipe <term>fistula: a traditional shepherd's pipe made of reeds of different lengths</term>
**H**angs, drawing forth melodies through alternating stops.

**B**ut when the twenty-seventh degree of the Wool-bearer <note>original: "Lanigeri," the constellation Aries (the Ram)</note> increases,
**T**he Hyades <note>A cluster of stars in the constellation Taurus, often associated by the ancients with rain and storms</note> rise. At this time, for those being born,
<unclear>N</unclear>o rest is pleasing, and leisure is never fruitful;
**B**ut they hurry, seeking out crowds, business, and tumults.
**S**edition and shouting delight them; they long for the Rostra <note>the speaker's platform in the Roman Forum</note>
**L**ike the Gracchi <note>Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus, famous Roman populist reformers known for their fiery oratory and controversial political moves</note> and desire the Sacred Mountains.
**T**hey prefer the wars of peace to peace itself; anxiety provides their nourishment.
**T**hey drive their filthy flocks through the dirty countryside
**A**nd trust in [the stars]... they have also produced Boötes <note>The "Herdsman" constellation</note>.
**T**he Hyades generate these characters when their stars are rising,
**W**hen the final part of the Wool-bearer’s orbit is excluded,
**W**hich shows the whole world to the lands and rescues it from the waves;
**T**he Olenian star <note>original: "Olene sidus," referring to Capella, the "Goat Star" named after the goat that nursed the infant Jupiter</note> is raised, along with the moving Kids.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influences of the Kids and Hyades, warning that those born under them may appear virtuous but are often prone to lust or political restlessness. It also links these stars to the humble life of shepherds and the tumultuous life of Roman demagogues.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Hyades, Kids, Aries, shepherds, pan-pipes, the Gracchi, Boötes, Capella, Roman politics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astrological influences of the stars associated with the constellation Taurus and Auriga. It specifically moves from the general influence of the Kids (Haedi) to the nursing of Jupiter by the celestial Goat, and then details the rising of the Pleiades, focusing on the social and personal characteristics—often associated with luxury and vanity—that these stars bestow upon those born under them.</meta>

**A**nd the part that is starred by the cold pole on the right side
**P**erformed its duty for the nurturing of great Jupiter <note>original: "magni mat² iouis"; This refers to the myth of the goat Amaltheia who nursed the infant Jupiter on Mount Ida</note>, the Thunderer.
**I**t provided the foundations and filled his gaping breast
**W**ith its own milk, giving him strength worthy of his thunderbolts.
**F**rom here, timid minds and trembling hearts are created,
**S**uspended in anxiety and vulnerable to trivial causes.
**T**o these people, there is a natural desire to see the unknown;
**J**ust as goats <note>original: "capellæ"</note> rush through new thickets and the night,
**T**hey always rejoice as they stretch further in their grazing.

**T**aurus rises headlong into the scorched dawns,
**A**nd at the sixth part <note>The 6th degree of the sign</note> of its burgeoning light,
**I**t leads the Pleiades <note>The "Seven Sisters," a cluster of stars in the shoulder of Taurus</note>, who, by their breathing,
**B**ring to light the followers of Bacchus <note>The god of wine</note> and Venus <note>The goddess of love</note>;
**A**cross feasts and tables, they bring forward petulant hearts,
**S**eeking sweet laughter through biting wit <term>sale mordaci: literally "biting salt," a Roman metaphor for sharp, stinging humor or sarcasm</term>.
**T**o these, care for their own face and the beauty of their brow
**W**ill always be a concern: to set their hair in twisted curls,
**O**r to bring it back with waves and shape it again with a thick comb,
**A**nd to change their head's appearance with added hair <note>A reference to wigs or hairpieces, which were common in high-society Rome</note>,
**T**o polish their rough limbs with hollow pumice stones <note>Used by ancient Romans for hair removal and skin smoothing</term>,
**A**nd to loathe their own manhood, preferring smooth and sterile limbs.
**F**eminine garments [they wear] and [fine] coverings for their feet.
**B**ut appearances and artificial steps please their soft manners;
**T**hey are ashamed of nature, and within their blind breast gapes
**A**mbition, and they call their vice by the name of virtue.

<summary>This page describes the astrological effects of the constellation Auriga (the Goat) and the Pleiades, shifting from the mythological origins of Jupiter's strength to a critique of the vanity, wit, and perceived effeminacy of those born under these stars.</summary>

**A**lways to love is not enough; they will also want to seem loved. <note>The poet concludes a section on those who seek social validation and romantic attention.</note>

**N**ature scarcely denies wings and the pathways of a bird;
**S**uch great vigor will be throughout their limbs, echoing the winds.
**T**hat man will depart as victor from the stadium <term>stadium: the ancient Greek running track, approximately 185 meters</term> before he has even been sent from the starting line.
**H**e will elude the stiff boxing-gloves <term>caestus: Roman boxing gloves made of leather strips, often weighted with metal to cause lethal injury</term> with swift movement;
**N**ow lightly dodging the blows sent his way, now parrying them with his palms.

**A**nd match his hands with his feet, and play with leaps,

**H**e is able to pour out a crowd of balls [into the air],
**A**nd wandering across his whole body, to position his feet <margin>at palmas: or rather his palms</margin>

**A**nd command them, as if they were trained, to fly around him. <note>This passage vividly describes a juggler or an exceptionally skilled player of *pila*, a Roman ball game where players used both hands and feet to keep balls in motion.</note>
**D**iligence watches over his sleep, and industry overcomes his cares.
**H**e spends his sweet leisure through various games.

**T**he claws rise <note>original: "ungulæ"; referring to the stars representing the feet or claws of the Crab</note>, by whose breathing those born

**A**nd returning the gifts to your mother through your death, <note>In myth, Meleager’s life was tied to a piece of wood (a brand). His mother, Althaea, burned it in revenge for him killing her brothers, thus killing her son.</note>
**W**hose life was buried bit by bit even before his death;
**A**nd he who attempted to bear the labors of Atlas,
**A**nd the girl fighting on the Calydonian rock. <note>This refers to Atalanta, the famous huntress who was the first to wound the Calydonian Boar.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the athletic and physical skills granted by constellations rising alongside the zodiac signs. It concludes the section on the Hare (Lepus) and transitions into the influence of Procyon (the Lesser Dog) as it rises during the sign of Cancer.</meta>

**V**ictory over men and the ability to be seen as a victor <note>The poet is concluding the description of the athlete or performer from the previous page.</note>
**V**irginity manifests <note>Likely a reference to the purity or discipline of the athlete.</note>, with the first wound permitted.
**Q**uickly, just as Actaeon <note>In Greek mythology, Actaeon was a hunter who was transformed into a stag by the goddess Diana and torn apart by his own hounds.</note> was destined to be changed in the woods
**E**nding as a new kind of prey for his own dogs; so too are the beasts led.
**R**estricting the fields with nets, they close them in with the fear of death;
**M**aking deceitful pits and tenacious snares,
**C**atching running wild beasts, they bind them with shackles and fetters,
**A**nd kill their prey either with dogs or with steel, and carry them home.
**S**ome have a passion for the sea, to have captured the beasts of the deep,
**D**iverse forms sent forth from the blind abyss,
**S**trewing the bodies of sea-monsters upon the sands of the shore.

**A**nd straining the wandering rivers with nets drawn through them.
**A**nd such a man does not hesitate to follow prey where no tracks exist <note>A reference to the trackless nature of water.</note>,
**L**uxury on land will loathe the "pure" world <note>Manilius suggests that simple land-based food becomes boring to the wealthy, who then seek exotic sea-creatures.</note>,
**V**enturing even into the ocean to feed the gluttony of the belly.

**S**eventh degree of Cancer raises itself from the waves toward the stars,
**V**enery <term>venatus: the art or practice of hunting</term> is not granted directly to those born then, but rather the tools of the trade.
**V**ocation is given to nourish sagacious puppies for the hunt,
**A**nd to instill the character of the breed into the young offspring from their birth,
**R**eticulated nets and various hunting spears fixed with sharp points,
**L**ithe shafts to be woven together with knots,
**A**nd whatever else the care of hunting is accustomed to require;
**I**n their own workshops, they will manufacture these goods for sale and profit.

**C**orda their hearts are moved, and their tongue, barking as it speaks, hurries along.
**M**orbibus in frequent fits of passion, it even leaves the mark of teeth in the voice;
**A**rdescit it burns with vices, and one vice feeds the cares of another;
**B**acchus <gloss>Bacchus</gloss> <note>The god of wine.</note> also <gloss>and</gloss> kindles savage anger <gloss>in the flames</gloss> amidst the heat of wine <gloss>flames</gloss>.
**H**ec this man fears the woods, the crags, and the vast lions,
**A**ut or the tusks of the foaming boar and the natural weapons of wild beasts;
**E**ffunditque; and it pours out its flames through the strength granted to it.

<summary>This page describes the rising of the Dog Star (Sirius) during the heat of midsummer, detailing its destructive physical effects on the environment and its astrological influence on the human character, producing violent and impulsive temperaments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the astrological influence of the rising stars. Nor should you wonder at such skills under such a star.
You see how even the star itself hunts among the heavens <note>original: "uenet[ur] in astris." This refers to the constellation Canis Major, the Great Dog, depicted as a hunter.</note>,
It pursues the Hare <note>The constellation Lepus, located at the feet of Orion and pursued by the Great Dog.</note> which has run ahead, seeking to catch it in its course.
When the final part of the Great Lion's <note>The constellation Leo, specifically the last degrees of the sign.</note> mouth is raised,
The Cup <note>The constellation Crater, which in mythology is the vessel of Apollo or Bacchus.</note> also rises, studded with golden stars.
From this source, whoever follows its path draws their nature and character:
[They will love] well-watered fields, rivers, and lakes,
And you, Bacchus <note>The god of wine, viticulture, and ecstasy.</note>, it joins your "wedding" vines to the elm trees <note>The Roman practice of "marrying" vines (*nubentem iungit*) involved training grapevines to grow up elm trees for support and better sun exposure.</note>.
It arranges them on hillsides, where the leaves seem to mimic dances.
By its own strength, it leads the vine as it spreads its "arms" [branches],
And it always trusts itself to you, who were restored from your mother <note>A reference to the myth of Bacchus; his mother Semele died while pregnant, and he was sewn into the thigh of his father, Jupiter, to complete his gestation.</note>.
It joins the grain to stalks and grafts the crop into the grapes.
There is no other form of cultivation so varied throughout the world;
Each person harvests wine according to their region.
He drinks the vintages sent to him and is sustained by the fruits.
He will delight in pure wine and drown his mind in his cups.
Nor does he trust his hopes only to the earth and empty prayers,
But also to the grain market <note>original: "Annonæ." The Annona was the Roman grain supply and its presiding goddess.</note> and the profits that follow—
Especially those nourished by moisture, provided the water does not fail.
Such is the character the Cup will mold: a lover of liquid.
Now Erigone <note>Another name for the constellation Virgo, often associated with Justice (Astraea) or the daughter of Icarius.</note> approaches, and when five parts [degrees] are carried up,
Rising from the waves of the sea,
The once-dear monuments of the Crown of Ariadne <note>The constellation Corona Borealis, the crown given by Bacchus to the Cretan princess Ariadne.</note>.
From here, the gentle Girl <note>Virgo.</note> will bestow the gifts of her soft arts,
For they shine there, and the rising one is the Girl herself.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the influence of the zodiacal constellations on human character and professions. He cultivates a garden shimmering with budding flowers,
Pale violets and purple hyacinths,
Lilies and poppies that mimic the brilliance of Tyrian dyes <note>original: "tyrias... luces." Tyrian purple was the most expensive and prestigious dye in the ancient world, harvested from Mediterranean sea snails.</note>,
And the flower of the spring rose, red as blood.
He will sow the hillside, blue with leaves and green with grass,
And paint the meadows with the vibrant forms of spring.
Or he will weave together various flowers, arranging them in wreaths,
Fashioning by hand likenesses of nature, pressing one against another;
He will steep them and blend them with the fragrances of Arabian forests <note>A reference to the exotic perfumes and resins, such as frankincense and myrrh, traditionally imported from southern Arabia.</note>,
And he will produce ointments that offer medicinal scents.
Thus, through the "adultery" of dyes <note>original: "adulterio fuco[rum]." Manilius uses the word "adultery" metaphorically to describe the artificial blending or "corrupting" of natural elements to create more potent, manufactured scents and colors.</note>, their charm becomes greater—
The arts of elegance, refinement, and graceful living,
The "theft" of life's hardships and the presence of pleasure.
These are the gifts the Virgin <note>The constellation Virgo, often identified with Erigone or Astraea, the goddess of justice.</note> and the flowers of the year’s crown demand.

But when, rising through its tenth degree, the bristling
Spica <note>The brightest star in Virgo; its name literally means "Ear of Grain" in Latin.</note> brings the ripening ears of grain to the fields,
It inspires a passion for the land and for tilling the soil:
To return seeds as "interest" <note>original: "foenus." Manilius uses financial metaphors, comparing the growth of crops to the accumulation of interest on a loan.</note> to the furrowed earth,
To pursue a profit with a greater fortune of gathered
Fruits, and to seek out granaries for the boundless harvest.
If only this were the only "metal" <note>original: "metallu[m]." Manilius contrasts the "golden" grain with mined metals like gold or silver, suggesting that if grain were the only wealth men sought, the world would be free of the greed associated with mining.</note> mortals knew,
No hunger and no famines would plague the world;
The wealth of the nations would be rich and well-fed.
And if by chance the labor of the countryside slowed the development of other arts,
Still, without this labor, there is no Ceres <note>The goddess of agriculture; here used as a metonym for the grain itself.</note> and no use for the seed.

To place in the furrows the grain that will grow,
To guide the heavy rollers and submerge the spelt <note>original: "farra." An ancient species of wheat (emmer) that was a staple food in early Rome and used in religious sacrifices.</note>,
To parch the grain at the hearth and prepare the food of men,
Transforming one type into many different shapes.
And just as he is skilled in arranging grain through art,
A similar order is composed for the fruits themselves:
Providing storerooms and granaries for their respective seeds.
The stars will also make one who carves coffered ceilings for sacred temples,
Constructing a new heaven within the vaults of the Thunderer <note>original: "tonantis." A common epithet for Jupiter, the king of the gods, whose temples featured magnificent high ceilings.</note>.
<margin>+</margin> This style of decoration was once reserved only for the gods,
But now it is a part of luxury; dining rooms <note>original: "trichnia" (triclinia). The formal dining room of a Roman house, named for the three couches arranged around a table.</note> compete with temples,
And we live surrounded by gold in our gilded halls.
But when the Arrow <note>The constellation Sagitta.</note> rises at the eighth degree,
It will grant the power of the Claws <note>original: "Chelaz" (Chelas). This refers to the constellation Libra, which in older astronomical traditions was seen as the "Claws" of the neighboring Scorpion.</note> and the ability to hurl the javelin with strong arms,
To let fly the reed-shaft... <unclear>elebas</unclear>... and to snatch the bird

With his quiver, he waged the war of Troy;
A great enemy in arms, he settled there as an exile.
<unclear>Qunnq</unclear> he suffers... and born from such...

<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions of the constellations' influences on human lives. Having previously discussed the rising of the Arrow (Sagitta) and its gifts of archery and precision, the text now illustrates this with the legend of Alcon. It then transitions to the rising of the constellation of the Kids (Haedi) and the Lyre, shifting the focus from physical skill to the complexities of urban law, commerce, and the divine power of music.</meta>

<margin>+</margin> An unhappy father, watching his son as he drank in sleep and breath... <note>This refers to the story of the archer Alcon, whose son was attacked by a serpent while sleeping. Alcon had to shoot the snake without harming the child.</note>
He had the courage to take aim and strike with his launched dart.
The power of being a father overcame the danger;
And he gave birth to the youth a second time, raising him from both sleep and death.
Then once more the son, who had been snatched from fate through his dreams...
But when the shameless Kid <note>original: "edus" (haedus). Referring to the constellation Haedi, often associated by the Romans with stormy weather and a restless, wandering temperament.</note> among the secret stars,
Like a wanderer, seeks the tracks of his brothers,
After the flock has been separated by a long distance,
It pours forth skillful minds and restless hearts,
And shapes their effectiveness through various cares.
Nor are those public duties <note>original: "munist̃a" (ministeria). Official services or administrative roles performed for the state.</note> confined to the home;
Magistrates proceed and public laws are brought forth.
No spear <note>original: "hasta." In Roman law, a spear was stuck into the ground to signal a legitimate court session or a public auction.</note> which could be moved by the care of any finger was lacking,
Nor was there a bidder <note>original: "sector." A term for those who bought confiscated goods at public auctions to resell them for profit.</note> for property, seeking to profit from a penalty.
The guilty debtor who defrauded his former debt of money...
Better known in the city, and no less playful in his loves,
He places himself in the various [theaters] and the forum... <note>The text here describes a man of the city, involved in legal disputes, public auctions, and urban social life.</note>
He goes into the glades, made softer by his art.
To him, as the Lyre rises, the tortoise-shell <note>original: "testudinis." Legend says the first lyre was created by Mercury using a tortoise shell as the sounding board.</note> tells its story through its strains,
A beauty preferred even over the heir of the Thunderer <note>original: "tonantis." Jupiter. This refers to the high esteem in which music and the constellation Lyra are held.</note>.
And as once the Oeagrian Orpheus <note>Oeagrus was the king of Thrace and the father of the mythical musician Orpheus.</note>, bringing forth the sound,
Added senses to the rocks and gave ears to the woods,
Bringing tears to Dis <note>original: "diti." Dis Pater, the god of the Underworld. Orpheus famously descended to the land of the dead to retrieve his wife, Eurydice.</note> and finally an end to death.
To him will come the voices of a resounding gift,
The talkative flute <note>original: "tibia." An ancient wind instrument, often consisting of two pipes played simultaneously.</note> with its melodies and divided form.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the astrological influences of constellations. & whatever speaks by the hand or is moved by the breath,
He will provide sweet songs amidst banquets,
And he will soothe Bacchus <note>In Roman mythology, Bacchus is the god of wine; here used to mean the wine itself at a feast.</note> with sound and hold the nights spellbound.
With a secret song, modulating his voice into a murmur,
He will always sing, as if for his own ears alone.

At a distance from the Lyre, when the twenty-sixth part

They are almost gods themselves, and those who are able to see things to come.

Such a person will either drive with a goad, or join together

And they <note>Meaning the stars or the person born under them.</note> take away or change the movements of the silent animals, which had never been heard.
This is the work of his art: not to wait for the groaning of the patient,
And to realize the body is sick before the animal itself has felt it for long. <note>Manilius is describing the skill of a veterinarian or a master of animals who can diagnose a creature that cannot speak its pain.</note>

<meta>This page continues the astrological catalog from the previous page, moving from the influence of the Centaur to the Archer (Sagittarius) and the Swan (Cygnus). It maintains the theme of how stellar positions at birth determine a person's future career and character, shifting here from state administration to the specialized world of avian arts and luxury trade.</meta>

Next follows the **Archer** <note>original: "arcitenens." This refers to the constellation Sagittarius.</note>, whose fifth part shows the shining **Arcturus** <note>Arcturus is the brightest star in the northern constellation of Boötes; ancient astronomers often linked its rising to specific degrees of the zodiac.</note> emerging from the sea. At this time, bold **Fortune** herself commits her own wealth; she creates those who handle royal riches and sacred treasuries, and those who act as rulers under a king or as high-ranking ministers.

When the Archer has drawn himself entirely from the waves, at the thirtieth degree of the wild beast, the feathered **Swan** <note>original: "holor" (olor). This refers to the constellation Cygnus.</note> flies into the sky with its shining wings. As it rises, trailing light and leaving its mother <note>This likely refers to the myth of Leda, where the swan is born from or leaves the "mother" egg/starry origin.</note> behind, it calls for a devotion and a focus on the "aerial people" and the winged race dedicated to the heavens.

A thousand arts flow from this: either to declare war upon the [bird] world and seize them in mid-flight, or to watch them with a keen cry while they sit and feed, ensuring that they do not flee under the moonlight. These people will also hold the guardianship of the people or the management of households; as overseers, they will confine their duties within foreign boundaries.

And now, luxury has come from afar for its own sake: we are fed from **Numidian** shores <note>Numidia was a region in North Africa, famous for supplying the "Numidian bird" or guinea fowl to Roman tables.</note>, and the **Phasian** bird <note>original: "Phasidos." This refers to the pheasant, which the Greeks and Romans believed originated from the banks of the river Phasis in Colchis (modern Georgia).</note> is summoned to the market—the same place from which the Golden Fleece was once carried across the new sea. Such a person will even teach human languages and senses to the birds of the air, leading them into a new kind of commerce; he will teach them words that were denied to them by the law of nature. He worships the progeny of a god <note>A reference to Jupiter, who famously transformed into a swan.</note> and the voice that is held under his power.

<summary>This page describes the astrological influences of Sagittarius and Cygnus, linking them to careers in royal administration, the hunting and training of exotic birds, and the Roman trade in luxury delicacies.</summary>
<keywords>Sagittarius, Cygnus, Arcturus, Royal Treasury, Fowling, Exotic Birds, Numidia, Pheasants, Bird Training, Astrology</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological influences of the stars rising alongside the zodiac. It transitions from the final "aerial" arts of the Swan (Cygnus) to the dangerous "earthly" arts of the Serpent-Bearer (Ophiuchus) and finally the "underwater" labors of the Southern Fish (Piscis Austrinus).</meta>

He will not turn over the whole world in his mind or murmur only to himself in secret. Nor should those pass you by who delight in feeding the birds of **Venus** <note>original: "aues veneris." These are pigeons or doves, which were sacred to the goddess of love and frequently kept as pets or messengers in Rome.</note> under a high roof, returning them to their enclosures, or those who recall them with specific signals throughout the city, carrying birds in cages that are ready to obey every command. The entire livelihood and wealth of such people consists of just a small amount of birdseed.

As the golden **Swan** <note>original: "holor" (olor). This refers to the constellation Cygnus.</note> takes its place in the heavens, it bestows similar skills. But the one who holds [the serpent], surrounded by the great coil of the **Dragon** <note>This refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer, who is often depicted in ancient astronomy as entangled with a serpent near the constellation Draco.</note>, when he enters the region of your figure, **Capricorn**, he does not make the limbs of those born under him hostile to [venomous creatures]. Instead, they receive the slithering serpents into their very laps and the folds of their flowing robes; they will even join kisses with terrifying venoms and remain unharmed <note>The poet is describing snake-charmers or healers who were thought to possess a mystical immunity to snake bites.</note>.

But when the **Fish** <note>original: "piscis." This is the Southern Fish (Piscis Austrinus), distinct from the zodiac sign of Pisces.</note> brings himself forth from his own sea and enters the heavens in foreign territories, whoever begins his life at such a time will spend his years wandering the shores and riverbanks. He will catch the fish as it hangs in the hidden depths of the sea. He will also drag out shells <note>A reference to the luxury trade of pearl diving or the collection of murex shells used for purple dye.</note> that are submerged and hiding within their own "houses" and fortifications; for such a person, there is no risk left undared. He seeks out bodies cast into the deep from recent shipwrecks, diving into the abyss so that the lost cargo itself is sought as prey. Yet the reward for such great labor is always meager: though these shells are valued as equal to entire fortunes, and the swift South Wind is braved, the diver himself is rarely rich. The land is adorned by the spoils of the deep, and by such a fate, he practices his trade along the shores.

<summary>This page describes the professional influences of the constellations Cygnus, Ophiuchus, and the Southern Fish, ranging from bird-training and snake-charming to the dangerous life of pearl divers and shipwreck scavengers.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Cygnus, Ophiuchus, Capricorn, Southern Fish, snake-charming, pearl diving, shipwrecks, Roman luxury, bird training</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of astrological influences from the Southern Fish (Piscis Austrinus) and transitions into the rising of the Dolphin (Delphinus). It moves from the commercial and legal professions associated with the previous stars to the specialized physical skills of swimmers and divers influenced by the Dolphin.</meta>

Or he buys the labors of foreigners for a price and barters for them, and he establishes various goods under the image of maritime merchandise. When these stars rise in the great world, an investigator of crimes shall come, a punisher of the guilty, who will probe into the acts committed by using his own proofs. <margin>shouting out</margin> <note>original: "latrantia." This marginal note likely refers to the "barking" or loud accusations made in a court of law.</note> He will drag into the light things that were hidden by silent deceit. He is also a ruthless torturer and a master of punishment; he is one to whom truth has shown favor or who exposes guilt. He brings it forth and removes disputes from the depths of his heart. <note>Manilius here describes the character of a judge or a forensic investigator, a "seeker of crimes" (quaesitor scelerum) whose career is determined by the stars rising at his birth.</note>

When the sea-blue **Dolphin** <note>original: "delphinus." This constellation was associated by the ancients with the story of Arion or the rescue of shipwrecked sailors.</note> raises itself from the ocean into the stars and emerges with its scales mimicked by the stars, it is ambiguous in nature, having been created for both the land and the sea. For just as the dolphin itself glides through the air swifter than wings, now cleaving the surface of the sea and now the deep waters of the abyss, and gathers strength in its knees to give shape to the waves, so from this constellation comes whoever shall be tossed in the waves. 

Such a person, carrying his alternating arms in long, slow strokes, will make the waters resound with his splashing; now, as his hands are plunged into the sea, he will draw down his palms like a secret oar. He will enter the water upright and swim everywhere, feigning shallow ground as he turns the surface of the sea into a level plain. <note>The poet is describing the art of treading water or floating so effortlessly that the deep sea appears to be solid ground.</note> Or, carrying his limbs motionless upon his back or side, he will not burden the waters but will lie atop the crest of the waves, hanging over the entire sea without the need for an oar. It is a joy to seek the sea while upon the sea.

<summary>This page describes the astrological transition from the legal and mercantile professions to the physical mastery of swimming and diving, governed by the rising of the constellation Delphinus.</summary>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the constellation Delphinus (the Dolphin), which governs those who live in and around water, as well as acrobats who mimic the dolphin's agility. It then transitions to the rising of Cepheus, which bestows a grave, stern character suited for traditional education and moral guardianship.</meta>

Those who submerge their bodies in the waves and attempt to visit the ethereal nymphs <note>original: "æquoreas nymphas." In Roman mythology, these were the spirits of the sea.</note> in their own caves; they bring back spoils from the sea and things snatched from the deep shipwrecks, greedily scouring the bottommost sands. 

A part of this skill is pursued with a different focus, and the influence is divided between two types [the diver and the acrobat], yet it rises from a single source. To music <note>original: "numeros"</note>, and even though these skills are related by art, their bodies leap forth, tossed from a strong springboard <term>petauro: a high-flying seesaw or springboard used by Roman acrobats</term>. They set in motion alternating movements: one man, having been carried down by his weight, throws another, and that one is suspended by his fall. Their limbs, shot through flames and burning hoops, rise up as if they were placed in liquid waves above the ground. They mimic the swimming motions of dolphins through the empty air, and as if clothed in feathers, they fly and play in the sky. 

But even if these specific skills are lacking, the natural disposition will remain; for nature will provide vigor and swift races, and limbs that fly across the field.

But Cepheus <note>In mythology, the King of Ethiopia and father of Andromeda, placed in the stars near the North Pole.</note>, passing through the region of the moist **Water-Bearer** [Aquarius], will not give a hidden character; he creates an enduring expression and composes the face and features with the weight of the mind. Such people will be fed by cares and will always revolve the examples of the ancients in their minds. They will always praise the words of old **Cato** <note>Cato the Elder, a Roman statesman famous for his strict traditional morality and resistance to Greek luxury.</note>. They will adopt the sternness of a guardian or a harsh rigor; they will be the ones to guide tender years, ensuring the master follows the law of the *toga praetexta* <term>toga praetexta: a purple-bordered toga worn by Roman boys until they reached adulthood, and also by high-ranking magistrates.</term>

<summary>This page describes the astrological influence of the Dolphin on divers and acrobats, followed by the influence of Cepheus on stern moralists and educators who value ancient Roman traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Delphinus, Dolphin, diving, acrobatics, Cepheus, Cato, education, toga praetexta, astrology, Roman values</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological profile of those born under the constellation Cepheus. While the previous page established a character of gravity and moral guardianship, this section explores how that same sternness translates into the dramatic arts—both the gore of tragedy and the wit of comedy.</meta>

Whatever such a man does, he will believe in it, stunned by the very image of justice. Indeed, they will provide words for the tragic buskin <term>cothurno: the high-soled boot worn by ancient tragic actors to increase their height and presence</term>, and the writer's very pen will be blood-stained upon the page. No less will they dare to commemorate the mourning of the dark tomb, the horror of crimes, and the chaos of the world—showing a father vomiting up his own children <note>original: "Rutantemq[ue] prẽm natos." A reference to the myth of Thyestes, who was served his own sons at a banquet by his brother Atreus.</note>, the sun turning back its course, and a blind day lacking its light. They will delight in the wars of Thebes <note>Refers to the tragic saga of Oedipus and the battle between his sons.</note>, those battles of the womb where a parent is confused with a brother. 

They will describe Medea seeking her children, her brother, and her father; here, the flames sent through a poisoned gift <note>original: "uestis flammas." Medea killed her rival, Creusa, by sending her a golden robe that burst into flames.</note>, and there, her flight through the air and rivers conquered by fire. They will draw a thousand other visions of things into their poetry. Perhaps the very gestures of the stage will be recorded in their works.

But if someone chooses a milder path in their zeal for writing, they will compose comic spectacles for joyful festivals: men full of laughter, girls snatched away in love, old men who are deluded, and slaves who are agile in every situation. Among these is Menander, who extended his life into every age, a man more learned than his own world, flourishing in the flower of language; he showed life through his depiction of life and hallowed its course. 

And even if the strength of his resources should deny him such grand inventions, he will still be suited to perform the works of foreign poets—now with his voice, now with silent gestures and a face that speaks through expressions. By guiding his own movements, he will go alone through all the different characters and represent an entire crowd in a single person <note>This describes the Roman "pantomimus," a virtuoso solo dancer who played every role in a story through gesture and mask changes.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the dramatic professions influenced by Cepheus, ranging from the composition of gruesome tragedies (like those of Medea and the House of Atreus) to the "milder" path of New Comedy exemplified by Menander, and finally the skill of the solo pantomime actor.</summary>
<keywords>Cepheus, tragedy, cothurno, Medea, Thyestes, Oedipus, comedy, Menander, pantomime, Roman theater</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of astrological influences from the previous page, shifting from the playwright's craft to the physical performance of the actor, then moving into the celestial influence of the Eagle (Aquila) and Cassiopeia.</meta>

Or he will act out the parts of great heroes and perform in dramas of the Roman state <term>scenas togatas: "toga plays," or Roman comedies and dramas set in Roman life, as opposed to Greek settings</term>. He will channel every face of Fortune through the movements of his limbs. He will match the rhythm of the choruses with his gestures, and by his skill, he will force the audience to see Troy as if it were present, or Priam falling before their very eyes.

This character is brought forth by the constellation of the Eagle, which rises to the left of the dewy youth <note>original: "rorantis iuuenis." This refers to Ganymede, the cupbearer of the gods, who is represented by the constellation Aquarius.</note> whom the eagle itself once carried up from the earth. Borne on foreign wings, the bird circles its prey; it retrieves the hurled thunderbolts and serves as a winged soldier in the heavens.

It marks the twelfth degree of the watery Water-Bearer. One born on earth at the time of this rising will rise toward spoils, factions, slaughter, and plunder. When he has finished the massacre of men, he will turn to the slaughter of wild beasts. This nature will not distinguish peace from war, nor a citizen from an enemy.

He is a law unto himself, carried wherever his will leads him. His strength is headlong, and his pride is to refuse to yield in anything. Yet, if a noble impulse happens to guide his daring, his virtue will achieve its full potential; he will bring wars to a close and be able to adorn his life with great triumphs.

And because his nature does not involve handling birds but rather providing arms—retrieving the fires sent forth and returning the thunderbolts—he will serve as a high minister to a great king or general through the trials of war, providing immense service through his own strength.

But when Cassiopeia, after twenty degrees have passed, rises on the right side of the youth from the sea <note>likely referring to Perseus, the hero who rescued Cassiopeia’s daughter Andromeda from the sea monster</note>, she will produce artisans who work with gold in a thousand different shapes.

and they wept for your misfortunes with a pitiful song

From here, vast coils swell in immense twists,
Its back consumes the sea, and the sound of the shallows echoes on every side.

<meta>This page continues the dramatic climax of the Perseus and Andromeda myth. And it <note>original: "ipi" — referring to the sea monster, Cetus.</note> even feared the mountains and the hero rushing from the rock.

Lifting the ocean to the stars with bloody waves.

At last, the beast sank down with its limbs pierced through,
Filled with seawater, yet it floated back to the surface of the waves,
And covered the great sea with its vast body.

<meta>This page concludes the rescue of Andromeda by Perseus and shifts from the mythological narrative to the astrological influences of the constellations Andromeda and Pegasus (the Horse). It follows the previous page's description of the aerial battle with the sea monster.</meta>

Then, the terrible beast—a sight the maiden’s eyes should never have had to behold—**Perseus** overwhelmed, drenching its massive body in the liquid marble of the sea. Greater now than before, he flies from the waves toward the high cliffs and releases the girl from the rock where she hung, still clinging to her chains. Betrothed by this very battle, she is to be wed, with her husband's victory serving as her dowry. 

For this, he gave **Andromeda** a place in the heavens and consecrated her among the stars as a reward for such a great war which he himself brought to an end. He relieved the ocean of a monster no less heavy than the **Gorgon** <note>original: "Gorgone" — referring to Medusa, whom Perseus had previously slain.</note> herself.

Whoever is born at the time when **Andromeda** rises from the sea will emerge as a harsh master of punishment and a warden of a cruel prison. While he stands by, proud in his office, mothers lie prostrate on the wretched threshold in grief; through the nights, fathers long for the final kisses of their children, wishing to draw the departing souls into their own very marrow. He comes in the likeness of an executioner leading the condemned to their death, with funeral pyres lit and the axe often laid ready. Punishment will be his revenue <note>original: "uectigal" — Manilius suggests that for such a person, the administration of justice and punishment is their primary "tax" or trade.</note>; he is a man who could look upon that very girl hanging from the rocks without pity. He is a lord of the bound and a partner to the chain’s burden, though he also preserves innocent bodies who have not yet earned their punishment.

When the twenty-first degree has risen from the sea-monsters <term>Pistibus</term> <note>original: "pistibus" — referring to the constellation Cetus or "The Whale," from which the degree is measured.</note>, and the marked light shines upon the lands and the world, another **Horse** <note>Pegasus, the winged horse born from the blood of Medusa.</note> shall be born and fly through the heavens. At such a time, he will grant swiftness to those being born, giving them limbs that remain ever-watchful for every possible task.

<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions from Marcus Manilius's *Astronomica*, moving from the character of those born under Andromeda to the influences of the Horse (Pegasus), the constellations of the Fishes, and the great Sea Monster (Cetus). This man will gather the horse into tight circles, and proud upon its back, he will wage steep wars as a leader mingled among his soldiers. Another, born of this star's study, will be more inclined to break his word and would rather be seen to lie; he will take his course across the open field. Who has ever flown back more swiftly from the edge of the world? As a messenger, he has pierced the farthest reaches of the earth three times over.

Yet, he will also heal wounds with simple juices <note>original: "sucis" — referring to medicinal plant extracts or saps.</note>, knowing the medical arts for the limbs of four-footed beasts. And if by chance his mind rises toward any of the arts, he will give his study to steep precipices and conquer danger through his genius. Though his attempts may seem thin and his steps without limit, he will place sure footsteps through the very edge of death <note>original: "per extinctos... finem" — Manilius is describing the precarious life of a tightrope walker or acrobat.</note>; he hangs as if in the mid-point of the heavens' heights, powerful as the **Apennines**, suspending the gaze of the people upon himself.

On the left, beneath the furthest parts, rise the stars of the **Sea Monster** <note>original: "Ceti"</note>, which follows **Andromeda** through the sea and the sky with its jaws <note>original: "Pistibus"</note>. This sign draws those born under it into the slaughters and wounds of the deep. They ensnare the profound waters by extending a scaly flock <note>original: "Squamigeri gregis" — a poetic way of describing a massive catch of fish.</note> within their nets, arming the very ocean with their pursuing bonds.
