--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition in Marcus Manilius’s *Astronomica*, Book V, where he moves from specific constellations to a grander cosmological theory. Having previously discussed the varying brightness of stars (magnitudes), he now introduces a sophisticated political metaphor, comparing the organization of the heavens to the social hierarchy of the Roman Republic—divided into the ruling class, the knights, and the common people.</meta>

An order takes its proper place, the knights and the common people follow;
You may see the inactive mass and the nameless crowd.
In this way, certain things correspond to the great cosmos <note>original: "mundo"</note>,
Which nature created when she established the sphere in the heavens.
There are stars of the foremost rank <note>original: "procerum vires," literally "the powers of the leaders," referring here to first-magnitude stars.</note>, and there are those closest to the first;
There are constellations and there are ranks, and all are joined to the one before.
The greatest is that which is carried by its own strength at the highest peak;
To which, if nature had given powers equal to their number <note>Manilius argues that if the sheer quantity of stars all had the power/brightness of the greatest ones, the universe would be destroyed by the heat.</note>,
The ether itself would be unable to bear its own flames,
And the whole world would burn with a blazing heaven <note>original: "olympo" — a poetic term for the sky or the residence of the gods.</note>.

<summary>Manilius uses a Roman social metaphor (Senators, Knights, and Plebeians) to explain the hierarchy of stellar magnitudes and argues that the universe's stability depends on the varying intensity of starlight.</summary>
<keywords>celestial hierarchy, stellar magnitude, Roman social order, cosmology, Marcus Manilius, nature of stars, ether</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a transition in the manuscript from the astronomical poetry of Marcus Manilius, seen on previous pages, to the medical teachings of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Serenus was a 2nd–3rd century Roman scholar whose work, the *Liber Medicinalis*, provides a series of folk remedies and ancient medical treatments organized anatomically, starting here with the head.</meta>

# THE BEGINNING OF QUINTUS SERENUS’S BOOK ON DISEASES FROM HEAD TO FOOT, WRITTEN IN VERSE.

<image-desc>A large, historiated initial 'M' at the beginning of the text, rendered in gold leaf with interlaced vines in red and green inside the letter's loops. It is set against a square blue background decorated with white filigree patterns and gold dots. A decorative blue floral spray with gold highlights extends vertically along the left side of the initial.</image-desc>

The sequence of the limbs is drawn out in a fixed course
so that a corresponding order of medicine may flow forth.
At the start, let us speak from the high citadel <note>The "high citadel" is a poetic metaphor for the human head.</note> of the body.
Apollo <note>original: "Phoebe"</note>, support this health-bringing song we compose
and accompany your invention <note>Medicine was traditionally considered an invention of the god Apollo.</note> with ready favor.
And you, powerful one <note>This refers to Asclepius, the god of healing and son of Apollo.</note>, who knows how to restore lives
and recall the buried dead to the light of the sky,
who dwells in Aigai, in Pergamon, and in Epidaurus <note>These were major centers of the cult of Asclepius in the ancient world.</note>,
who once, hidden under the skin of a gentle serpent,
sought the Tarpeian peaks and the famous temples <note>Asclepius was famously brought to Rome in 293 BCE in the form of a sacred snake to end a plague; he was enshrined on the Tiber Island near the Capitoline (Tarpeian) Hill.</note>,
driving away foul diseases with your present divinity:
come here, and whatever you have often told me
and confirmed, set it all out on these tender papyri.

## For curing the head and for pain.

If you pour balsams into both ears,
then you can powerfully restore the head’s vigorous strength.
<margin>Vervain</margin>
Or you may usefully bind the grasses that agree with seven nodes <note>original: "septenis nodis." This refers to Verbena (Vervain), which was believed to have magical and medicinal properties based on the number of its joints or nodes.</note>,
or a garland made from the cornel tree.
Or you shall place powerful pennyroyal <note>original: "Poleum"</note> above one ear;
or, having gently boiled that herb in a mixture of vinegar,
you shall draw it in through a cautious nostril; or you may apply
linen cloths smeared with sticky glue to the forehead; or crushed sprigs of mint.

A sponge attached with warm liquids flows out like rain.
Or ivy boiled in old oil <note>original: "oleo uetusto"</note>
also flows. And it is helpful to treat the forehead with small snails
if the violence of the sun has harmed the brain.
Often celandine <term>celandine: (Chelidonium majus), a herb traditionally used in folk medicine for its yellowish sap</term> produced from an acidic liquid
has healed it. And poppies, friends to sleep, are beneficial
if they are first ground after being soaked in slow-burning oil.

### On healing migraine

Furthermore, if you are tested by a sharp head ailment <note>original: "capitis morbo... acuto"</note>,
rue <note>original: "A uia," likely referring to the herb *Abia* or *Ruta* (rue)</note> divided and covered in wool, 
inserted through the ear, is beneficial; and balsam produced in the same way.
Or, you may smear ground rue (or three grains of pepper
added to three others); and such care will give you health.

### For dandruff of the head

It is an imperceptible disease and a harmful condition
when it scours the head with immense itching,
producing a bounty of meal <note>original: "copia farris"; the poet compares dandruff flakes to ground flour or bran.</note> as if from grinding stones.
The boiled root of mallow <note>original: "maluae radix"</note> can relieve this.
Or you may grind both fennel <note>original: "fenuculū"</note> and native sulfur <term>native sulfur: (sulfura viva), naturally occurring sulfur, often used in ancient dermatology</term>, 
then wash the head with all these mixed together.
Or for new dandruff, you will mix harsh vinegar,
and you may purge the varied hairs with such a liquid <note>original: "sanguine," literally "blood," but used here poetically for the medicinal mixture.</note>.
Blood taken from a slow tortoise <note>original: "testudine"</note> is also beneficial,
as is the sap released from the cedar tree.
Meanwhile, the mealy cloud <note>A poetic description of a flurry of dandruff flakes.</note> will cease to gather.

And the heavy rain of frequent itching will cease.

# On dyeing the hair

For those who are ashamed of their advanced age, whom sacred old age offends, they desire to hide their own gray hairs and to imitate black hair with a deceptive dye <note>original: "fuco simulare doloso"</note>. For these people, cypress crushed in sharp vinegar is beneficial, or the leaves of the mastic tree <term>mastic tree: (*Pistacia lentiscus*), an evergreen shrub used in antiquity for resin, breath freshener, and hair dye</term>, and the bitter berries of the elderberry <term>elderberry: (*Sambucus nigra*), often used to create dark blue or black dyes</term>. Earthworms also, when mixed with olive oil, restore the hair to its living bloom. Furthermore, one can drive away snowy-white hair with resin, easily combined with wax and birdlime <term>birdlime: (viscum), a sticky substance usually made from mistletoe or holly bark, here used as a binding agent for hair treatments</term>. To achieve the reddish hope of turning black hair golden <note>literally "yellowing" (flauescere)</note>, the author Tullius <note>likely referring to Marcus Tullius Cicero, though here cited as a medical authority</note> stated that an ointment of ash is the remedy. If a pregnant woman eats the parts of a goat <note>original: "forcas," likely a corruption of "fecis" (liver or offal); ancient folklore suggested maternal diet could influence the physical traits of the child</note>, they say the offspring’s eyes are formed with a dark color.

# On warding off lice and against an abundance of vermin

Nature has produced certain things from our own bodies that are harmful to those very bodies; for, unwilling to allow our sleep to be broken, she intends for us to pay watchful care to our senses and warnings. If anyone does not fear the fate of the tragic poet Pherecydes <note>Pherecydes of Syros, a 6th-century BC thinker who was famously (and perhaps mythically) said to have died from a lice infestation</note>, who, flowing with excessive sweat, breathed out his spirit in a foul state, and whom [the lice] carried off in a wretched and shameful death. Unhappy Sulla <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla, the Roman dictator, whose death was also attributed to phthiriasis (lice disease) as a form of divine or natural retribution</note> also collapsed, seized by such a wasting illness, and saw himself conquered by a foul swarm. Therefore, it is often proper to consume stinging mustard...

Or with natron <term>natron: a naturally occurring mixture of sodium carbonate decahydrate used as a cleaning agent in antiquity</term> and salt mixed together and loosened by an acidic liquid, so that the body may be touched by them all at once; or to bring forth a treatment with the tears of ivy <note>original: "lacrymis hederae," referring to the sap or resinous exudate of the ivy plant</note> or the juice of cedar. And whenever sulfur is medicated with mixed vinegar, or the wave of the sea is used, it serves to draw down the wicked nits from the head, and whatever else the thick forest of hair protects.

# To ward off itching, pimples, and the mange

The sweat of those people and an abundance of noble food <note>Ancient medical theory often attributed skin diseases to "excessive" or "rich" humors caused by overindulgence in food</note> often irritate limbs seized by a heavy mange <note>original: "scabie," a general term for various itchy skin conditions including scabies and eczema</note>. Therefore, it is beneficial to apply mud to the limbs after they have been rubbed; the dried moisture from the body of a donkey achieves the same result. Nor should one be ashamed to handle the dung which a heifer has poured out. Mix the ash of a pig with water; thus, the trembling body is moved by the pressure of the hand. It will also be useful to use dust mixed with oil, which the hardworking ant <note>original: "patiens labori," emphasizing the ant's industrious nature</note> has gathered from deep within its sweet hiding places; it is fitting to surrender the bodies to this, smeared only upon the warm limbs. 

For burning pimples, you will mix lupin meal <term>lupin meal: flour made from lupin beans (*Lupinus*), frequently used in antiquity as a skin detergent and medicinal base</term> with treated papyrus in liquid, or with dry Bacchus <note>a poetic reference to wine</note>; and thereafter you shall soak the limbs in a warm bath. But the liquid of vinegar relieves salty itching, as does the "sweat of the dry sea" <note>referring to salt or sea foam left on the shore</note> and small snails, by whose contact the bitter desire to scratch is suppressed.

# For curing frenzy and the head

From a defect of the brain, a furious frenzy <note>original: "frenesis," a term for delirium or inflammation of the brain accompanied by fever</note> is stirred up.

And gnashing madness brings back lost strength, whether a burning fever consumes the tossed limbs, or the taste of undiluted wine or a cold breeze brings it about. It is fitting to appropriately bind the temples of the suffering person with a medicinal crown made from the warm lungs of livestock <note>original: "e-calidis pecudum pulmonibus," a common ancient practice of using the residual animal heat and moisture from freshly slaughtered organs as a poultice to "draw out" the heat of a fever</note>. Remember to fumigate with unwashed wool <note>original: "ilotis lanis," wool still containing 'suint' or natural lanolin, which was believed to have medicinal properties when burned</note>. Since a present pain is not always curable, care is more beneficial when it meets coming diseases <note>This reflects the ancient medical adage that prevention or early intervention is superior to treating an established malady</note>.

**On hair loss and removing spots from the head**

The hair, driven out by a lurking disease, falls away if it has been thinned by a rare illness, or if the force of poison has been drunk, or if the powerful salamander—subject to no flames—has devoured the outstanding honor <note>original: "eximium honorem," a poetic way of referring to hair</note> of the head with its touch <note>Ancient naturalists like Pliny the Elder believed that the salamander was so cold it could extinguish fire and that its toxic secretions caused hair to fall out upon contact</note>. Sometimes spots vary for a short while before time brings new wounds scattered in circles. To treat these things, add the ashes of a viper’s skin; do not fail to join oak-galls <note>original: "roboreas pilas," small round growths on oak trees caused by wasps, used for their astringent properties</note> with bear fat, or soothe the evils with the blood of a tortoise.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Often the force of cold invades the path of the body and grips the limbs, so that it barely recedes even when a remedy is sought. If you boil a frog in oil, discard the flesh; warm the limbs with the ointment. In a similar way, it will be prepared <gloss>it shall be cooked</gloss>. <margin>+</margin> By applying seed to the crown of the head, you conquer the chills you have caught, and deer marrow can soften the stiffness. 

You shall swallow boiled radish seed with honey. Or, dilute bear's gall in warmed water; it will be beneficial as a drink. Or the burnt shell of an oyster <note>original: "cassis ostrea testis," referring to the helmet-like hard shell of the mollusk</note> will provide ash which, taken in place of salt in your food, will strike away the cold poison by warming the crown of the head. 

Some swallow liquid honeyed wine mixed with mustard, swirling it in the palate, both holding it and moving it about. Others chew garlic <note>original: "alia," likely a variant of "allia" (garlic), which was frequently used in ancient medicine to "heat" the body</note>, or pour a liquid of warm oil into the hidden passages of the brain and nose. They believe the flavor of lettuce water is also helpful. This method of treatment is both pleasant and useful.

->For those suffering from Elephantiasis<-

Elephantiasis <term>elephantiasis: in the Roman world, this referred to a severe, disfiguring skin disease (often leprosy) characterized by skin thickening and nodules, named for its resemblance to an elephant's hide</term> is a disease dreaded for its somber name, not only defiling the face with unspeakable pustules <note>original: "papillis," here referring to the characteristic nodules or lumps on the skin</note> but also quickly killing with a headlong, fatal poison. 

To combat this, the juice from the bark of a cedar will be favorable, as well as the ash of a weasel and the blood shed from it. Some have said that drinking the parrot-fish <term>scarus: (parrot-fish) a Mediterranean fish whose internal organs were highly valued in Roman medicine and cuisine</term> is beneficial; the leaf of wild mint <term>mentastrum: (Mentha longifolia) a pungent wild mint used for its cooling and antiseptic properties</term> is healthy when taken as a drink or applied topically.

### For curing ear pain and other defects

When cruel pain deeply wounds the tender ears,
Instill the juice which the ash <note>original: "fraxinea"</note> or the linden tree <note>original: "tilia"</note> pours out in the flames, 
Or the "dew" from the groin of a pig <note>original: "obsteno ex inguine rores," likely referring to animal fats or secretions</note>.
Or use the juice which the white poplar has given from its leaf.
Often let warm, sharp vinegar soothe the pain,
And with soda <term>nitro: natron or sodium carbonate, used in antiquity as a cleansing agent</term> added, the remedy for health comes forth.
The squeezed liquid of wild mint <term>mentastri: Mentha suaveolens, a wild mint species believed to have analgesic properties</term> is said to be beneficial.
Oil brought into the cavity of the ear has been very helpful.
If you gather red worms from an aged tree,
Warm them in oil and pour them into the strained ear.
If the sense of hearing dies within the hidden ear,
Cook earthworms in the fat of a hoarse goose,
And you will be able to dissolve the old disease.
They say ox gall <note>original: "fella bouis"</note> is also beneficial for the deaf, 
If you join it with the heavy-smelling substance wiped from a goat.
But if a living creature <note>meaning an insect or small pest</note> has entered the unwary ears,
A little mouse gall mixed with vinegar will be useful.
Or if harmful liquid has shaken the ear, goose fat is suitable;
Let it be applied with the juice of onions;
Though it is heavy for the eyes, it then increases the sense in the ears.
Use and learn the divine care of the son of Phoebus <note>original: "phoebigenæ," referring to Aesculapius, the god of medicine and son of Apollo/Phoebus</note>,
And let it be for your ears affected by these connected pains.

### For pain in the eyes

It is the highest blessing to possess the keen sight of men, 
Which Nature, looking forward, placed in the highest citadel <note>meaning the head or the brow</note> 
As pious guardians and defenders. 
She arranged them so that they would suffer no blows from above,

These can also help with long-standing diseases, 
As can the gall of a rooster softened with plain water.

## On the pain of teeth, their decay, and foul breath

It is not easy to endure the sharp pain of the teeth,
Wherefore it is more right to know the medical cure beforehand.
When you have boiled violets with wine <note>original: "baccho," the god of wine, used here as a poetic term for the liquid</note>, hold it in the mouth;
Just as chewing the juice of bitter wild olives is beneficial.
From here, I show that the [pain] flees and the wounds of the tongue yield.
For the one in pain, pepper with wine and warm nitre <note>original: "metrum," likely a transcription for "nitrum," a mineral salt or soda used for cleansing</note>;
Often with the juice of celandine and the milk of a goat,
The teeth are healed, or they are assisted by bull's gall.
Or acidic liquids are swished in the closed mouth.
The bramble <term>rubus: likely the blackberry or raspberry bush, used in ancient medicine for its astringent properties to tighten the gums</term> is suitable for chewing for the gums and good for the lips.
Mastic <term>lentiscus: the mastic tree, which produces a resin still used today in some cultures as a natural chewing gum and breath freshener</term> and myrtle improve the odor of the mouth.

And indeed, the name [dentifrice] was taken from "rubbing the teeth" <note>original: "dentifricium," from "dens" (tooth) and "frico" (to rub)</note>;
It is made from the ash of a stag's horn or a sow's hoof.
Also roasted ash from eggs, but not without wine.
Or the extinguished embers of a toasted mouse or an owl.
You may think these things are ostentatious, yet they are healthy for everyone.
But if you chance to complain of decayed teeth,
Burn the dung of a mouse and add it to the wide cavities.
Extinguish the ash of a stag's tooth with vinegar;
It is suitable, and place it fitly into the hollow parts.
Powder from earthworms with toasted bodies is also beneficial.
You will quickly heal a burnt mouth with dog's milk.
And often wash the gums with cold water <note>original: "lympha," a poetic term for pure water</note>,
So that you may enjoy the vigor of firm teeth.
If indeed a terrible wound creeps toward the inner parts <note>likely referring to a deep-seated ulcer or gangrene</note>,
Mix salt with an equal weight of frankincense;
Treat the place with this, and you will experience a wondrous cure.
Or take the ball [of wool] that clings to sheep-stalks <note>original: "caulis... ouinis," likely referring to bits of wool caught on brambles or thistle stalks in a pasture</note>;
When dried and crushed, this will yield soft meal;
And by rubbing this, the foul ulcer will be soothed.

---

->To heal the throat and <note>blank space in manuscript</note><-

The tender throat grows rough when the force of dark cold
Enters; and when the air, stirred by winds,
Turns, and a heavy breath infects the very fluids;
Or when a mad shouting with a broken sound happens
To scrape the smooth surface, as if it were a garden vegetable <note>The poet compares the rasping of a sore throat to the rough texture of garden greens or the act of scraping them</note>.

**Vocabulary:** teeth, tooth-powder, throat, earthworms, cure, ash, horn, gums, frankincense, garden-grown, vegetable.

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical instructions of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, transitioning from general throat irritation to specific remedies for the loss of voice, inflammation of the uvula, and the beginning of a section on neck pain.</meta>

For when the voice is worn out and exhausted by the conducting of business <note>original: "causis agendis," referring to legal or public oratory</note>,
The voice falls silent even while the master lives,
And the tongue of the eloquent man should not yet die out.
Therefore, seek with all zeal to restrain the pain.
Mix "honey of the air" <term>mellis aërii: ancient writers believed honey fell from the sky onto plants as a divine dew before being gathered by bees</term> with a great deal of water <note>original: "lymphis," a poetic term for water</note>;
Add bran shaken out from snowy-white flour;
When these are boiled together, let them be worked around in the closed mouth.
Furthermore, it is beneficial to anoint the throat from the outside
With bear's grease, bull's tallow, and softened wax,
All of which shall be leveled in equal scales beforehand <note>measured in equal parts by weight</note>.
Learn also a wondrous cure from a humble medicine:
Join wild poppy to thickened honey;
Chew this together once boiled and you shall swallow what is chewed.
Let five roots of "hairy" leeks <note>likely referring to the fibrous root ends of the leek</note> be cooked;
From this, let the water—not boiling—be rolled in the sounding throat <note>gargled</note>.
However, let this powerful drop descend into the stomach.
But if weakness has brought down the afflicted uvula <term>uuam: literally "the grape," referring to the uvula hanging at the back of the throat</term>,
Then remember to lie down for a few hours first;
Either the powder of toasted dill will relieve it,
Or ash from a snail and cabbage roasted in flames.
But quinsy <term>anguina: a severe, suffocating throat inflammation, likely peritonsillar abscess</term> demands vinegar mixed with salt,
Which it is important to shake within the mouth with the palate closed.

## On pain of the neck

But if the nape and the hardened neck shall be stiff,
I shall tell of wonders: the twin backs of the knees <note>original: "poples," the ham or back of the knee; ancient medicine often used "sympathetic" treatments where a remedy was applied to a body part opposite the ailment</note> shall be soothed with ointment.

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, moving from general throat ailments to specific treatments for neck pain, coughs, and the vital role of the stomach.</meta>

From here, a medicine will follow through both long nerves <note>original: "neruos," referring both to nerves and tendons or muscles of the neck</note>.
You shall soothe a stiff neck with the fat of a goose.
Lentils cooked in strong vinegar also help greatly,
Or goat dung mixed with onion, or swine’s marrow.
With this medicine fostering the unmoving nerves,
You shall remove whatever is harmful by touching it with the right hand
Where a cricket <note>original: "grillus"</note> has been crushed by its pressure. <note>This refers to a folk belief that the hand which crushes a cricket gains the power to heal certain ailments by touch.</note>

### For warm rheum <term>reuma: a "flow" of humors, often referring to inflammation or catarrh</term> and a cough

You will see the harmful poisons of fig trees <note>likely referring to the irritating sap</note> rage deep within;
You shall relieve them with a cooked lettuce leaf.
It will also be beneficial to often take moist cabbage stalks.
Sometimes the throat is shaken by a fierce cough—
Then you take boiled garlic smoothed with honey,
And the sweet seeds of a radish ground thrice and mixed with honey.
Or, if you pluck the seed from the foliage of the ash tree,
Swallow the inner parts; it is forbidden to let them touch the tooth. <note>original: "vetitur contingere dente," meaning the seeds should be swallowed whole rather than chewed.</note>
Grind a soft egg tamed by boiling water,
Or powerful horehound <term>Marubium: a bitter herb in the mint family long used for respiratory issues</term>; take these as the best things for the throat,
And you shall restrain a heavy cough with an easy medicine.

### On pain of the stomach

Those who contend that the stomach is the king of the whole body
Seem to rely on true reason.
For its strong condition strengthens all the limbs,
And all things are shattered by its pain.
Indeed, unless care helps, it can even damage the brain...

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Having established the stomach as the "king" of the body on the previous page, the text now details specific herbal and dietary remedies for stomach pain, indigestion, and the restoration of the senses.</meta>

It is said that from there <note>the stomach</note> it even carries away all the senses. 
Take black lettuce seed that a wooden pestle <note>original: "pila"</note> has ground, mixed with honey, on an empty stomach.
If you take three spoonfuls in one taste, it rouses you; 
Or ground radish seed and honey-wine <term>mulsum: a popular Roman beverage made of wine and honey</term> will help.
Or let two parts of wormwood <term>absinthium: a very bitter herb used since antiquity to treat digestive spirits and parasites</term> and a third part of rue <term>ruta: a bitter medicinal herb</term> be drunk in boiled water, along with moist pods <note>likely carob pods, often used in ancient medicine to soothe the gut</note>.
Fennel <term>marathrum: also known as foeniculum</term> seeds ground thrice with the milk of a pregnant goat, 
And boiled pennyroyal <term>poleum: an aromatic herb of the mint family</term> will kindly provide a cure.
Vinegar benefits the stomach both as a drink and as a warm compress <note>original: "fotu," a medicinal wash or fomentation</note>.
Or take snails warmed in water or nearly cooked; 
Place them to roast over hot coals and drench them in wine and fish sauce <term>garum: a pungent, fermented fish sauce that was a staple seasoning in the Roman world</term>; but it is more beneficial if they are sea snails.
In this way, little turnips will serve well for poorly digested food.

### For the stomach and digestion

Drink a mixture of pepper and the warm flower of rosemary <term>ros marinus: literally 'dew of the sea'</term>,
Or add ground cumin <note>original: "terre cuminum"</note> to salts, 
Which you shall touch to cooked feasts, and thus help the food you have consumed.
Or, as you recline on your elbow <note>original: "cubitum ponens," referring to the traditional Roman dining posture</note>, take a draught of sharp vinegar.
If you take the stomach of a diver bird <note>original: "mergus," likely a cormorant; ancient medicine often used the organs of animals known for strong digestion to treat human ailments</note> with ground salt added, 
You will receive a divine gift in your vitals.
This will benefit the stomach and help digest the food you have eaten. 
Scatter five grains of foreign pepper over toasted bread, 
Mixed with pepper powder and clear honey, 
Which you should take in the morning and in the evening.

<summary>This page provides various recipes for digestive aids, ranging from herbal mixtures (lettuce, wormwood, fennel) to animal-based remedies like sea snails and the stomach of a diver bird. It emphasizes the importance of pepper, honey, and vinegar in maintaining a healthy "king" of the body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous page’s advice on digestion and stomach ailments, the author now addresses issues related to bile, respiratory distress (specifically chronic coughing), and the treatment of nursing breasts.</meta>

# For Bile, Vomiting, and Chronic Coughing

If yellow bile increases and the skin becomes harmful <note>a reference to jaundice, where the skin turns yellow due to bile imbalance</note>, take nine grains of white pepper and an equal weight of parsley seeds <note>original: "apii grana," likely mis-transcribed as "pmole"</note>. Dilute these in a small cup <term>cyathus: a Roman liquid measure, approximately 0.045 liters</term>, then chew and drink. Take seven grains again later, and then five more.

Furthermore, if the small elderberry <term>sabucus: the elder tree, Sambucus nigra, long used in folk medicine for its purging properties</term> is in leaf, mix its boiled stems with salt and olive oil. Take this, and by such aid, you will purge the horrid bile.

Seawater is also beneficial when you mix it in the correct weight with sweet water and liquid honey. The snowy milk from the udder of a she-ass <note>donkey milk was highly prized in antiquity for both health and skincare</note> is helpful if you infuse it with warm wine, honey, and pepper.

A drink made of narcissus <note>the narcissus bulb was famously used as an emetic to induce vomiting in ancient medicine</note> will move the stomach to vomit. 

But if a chronic, wicked cough <term>ptyſis: from the Greek 'phthisis,' referring here to chronic spitting or a wasting lung disease like tuberculosis</term> persists, it will be useful to swallow live snails. A ball of old pig fat will also be beneficial, providing a healthy medicine for the passages <note>original: "capulas," likely referring to the bronchial tubes or vessels</note>.

# For Healing the Breasts

Breasts that flow heavily with fertile juice <note>mother's milk</note> are often harmful when they leak too much. Behold, therefore, they must be restrained with a strong binding when you see the breasts beginning to swell. 

For these ailments, mouse dung <note>original: "murinis fimus"</note> mixed with rainwater or a settled liquid is recommended. If the pain deep within constricts the breasts while they are closed... <note>the text breaks off here</note>

<summary>This page details Roman treatments for jaundice, respiratory wasting, and excessive lactation, utilizing a mix of herbal seeds, donkey milk, and unusual animal products like live snails and mouse dung.</summary>
<keywords>bile, jaundice, elderberry, donkey milk, emetics, phthisis, snails, lactation, Roman pharmacology</keywords>

Combine earthworms with the swollen clusters <term>corymbos: the berry clusters of the ivy plant</term> of the earth. It is a matter of great care to provide relief for nipples that have become constricted or painful. Remember to bind both breasts with ivy leaves, and immediately commit the plucked wreaths to smoke. Or, prepare goose fat with warm milk, or the wash of an egg produced by the chattering partridge.

Furthermore, poppies boiled in rainwater may cause a shivering sensation for many days before the pain is eventually released. You may also wish to take the heavenly gifts of the son of Phoebus <note>original: "phoebigenae." This refers to Aesculapius, the god of healing and son of Apollo (Phoebus)</note> when the breasts are torn with foul pains. You have ... and the herb named *prassion* <term>prassion: likely white horehound, a bitter herb used since antiquity for various ailments</term> and that which in common speech is called "the insane herb." <margin>Henbane</margin> <note>original: "Iusquiamus," from the Latin *hyoscyamus*. It was called "insane" because of its potent hallucinogenic and delirious effects if ingested in high doses.</note> Know that the Greeks call it *hyoscyamos* in their own tongue. These, along with the root of the reed and the dock plant, will provide miraculous cures (believe in Christ <note>original: "xpto," a Christogram for "Christo." This reflects a medieval scribe adding a Christian blessing to a classical medical text</note>).

### On the vomiting of food and blood

If the stomach, not yet in harmony, spits out its food, and the ailment grows stronger and more frequent by its recurrence, we should withdraw from dice-play <note>original: "alea." This suggests avoiding the excitement or agitation of gambling to settle the stomach</note> and draw in frequent scents. 

But if bright red blood is brought up from the chest, a draught of mint and boiled radish <term>rafanus: the radish, which ancient doctors believed could treat internal bleeding</term> will be of help. Often a stone is ground up, known by the name *specularis* <term>lapis specularis: a transparent or translucent mineral like mica or selenite, used in ancient medicine as a styptic to stop bleeding</term>; when drunk mixed with water, it stays the flow of blood. Sometimes the bark taken from the cork tree <term>subere: the cork oak</term> is burned, and the ash, when drunk in a warm liquid, is beneficial.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions from the previous leaf. From this point, the peach tree will provide a drink from its inner kernel,
a remedy which has been sufficiently proven to me through a chance discovery.

## For ailments of the spleen

When the spleen binds the loins with a swollen band
And shows the limbs to be stiffened by the fullness of the spleen,
Cups made from the soft wood of ivy remain useful;
From these, the sick person shall draw whatever liquids they are accustomed to,
Or they shall drink the broth of green, boiled vegetables.
A river leech will be beneficial once its blood has been drained <note>This likely refers to applying a leech to the skin over the spleen or, in some ancient traditions, using the remains of the leech in a preparation</note>.
Whether powerful tamarisk <term>myrica: the tamarisk shrub, often used in antiquity for its astringent properties, particularly for spleen disorders</term> or the sea-flower <note>original: "flos marinus"; likely referring to rosemary (ros marinus), which was believed to grow best near the sea</note> with bread,
Or the juice of ivy, whether taken as a drink or applied externally, it will help.
Also, find an alder tree that you see has been untouched by iron.
From this, the bark is stripped away with iron, but let it be boiled 
Until a third part of the water has been reduced.
After drinking a small amount of this, all the driven-out pain will depart.
Some record that the spleen can be worn away <note>meaning reduced in size or "consumed"</note>
If one always takes water on an empty stomach.
Likewise, dried herbs are overcome by pouring on boiling vinegar,
And once ground, it is smeared on, or the spleen of a young goat is applied.
They say that horehound <term>marubium: white horehound (Marrubium vulgare), a bitter herb used for various medicinal purposes</term> drunk in wine is helpful;
Furthermore, pepper and catmint <term>nepeta: cataria or catmint, often used in ancient medicine as a sedative or for digestive issues</term> with its flower and dill,
And also parsley <note>original: "apium"; can refer to celery or parsley</note> and a mushroom are joined together;
It is healthy to drink the boiled juices of these.
Mountain germander <term>poleum: Teucrium polium, a Mediterranean herb prized for its medicinal qualities</term> and southernwood <term>abrotanum: a species of sagebrush with a citrus-like scent, used to treat internal parasites and liver/spleen ailments</term> boiled with clear mastic <note>a resin obtained from the mastic tree (Pistacia lentiscus)</note>,
And a kind of savory <term>tymbra: a genus of aromatic plants similar to thyme or savory</term> which they call "headed" <note>original: "cephalotes"; referring to Thymbra capitata, characterized by its head-like flower clusters</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the treatment of the spleen, moving from physical remedies like ivy wood and tamarisk to the psychological symptoms associated with the organ, specifically the "Sardonic" laughter. It then transitions into a new section focused on the precordia (the diaphragm and the area around the heart).</meta>

God has promised that the spleen can be healed;
A dark bird's clapping <note>original: "pulla plausus ave"; this may refer to a specific omen or a metaphorical description of the spleen's movement or sound</note> is less suited to a great man.
A swollen spleen causes harm, yet it adds an inept laughter,
So that it seems to me to be the first among **Sardinian herbs** <note>original: "sardois herbis"; a reference to the plant from Sardinia that was said to cause "Sardonic laughter"—a facial convulsion resembling a grin that preceded death</note>,
Which mingle vain joys with miserable fates.
It is said that when standing upright, the spleen takes away easy laughs <term>cachinnos: immoderate or mocking laughter</term>
And presents a stern forehead to your face.

### To heal the precordia <term>precordia: the region of the body in front of the heart, including the diaphragm and the upper abdomen; often considered the seat of the soul and emotions in ancient medicine</term>

If the listless precordia swell with a harsh disease,
One pint of **mead** <note>honey-water</note> is mixed with **spelt**,
Along with crushed **flax** <note>linseed</note> and the pod of the **legume**.
Bind these together once they are boiled and while they are still warm;
Or use the **quinces** <note>original: "poma cydon"; literally "Cydonian apples"</note> which the Cretan shores have sent.
Grind them when cooked so that you may soften the hard tumor.
Furthermore, **basil** <note>original: "ozima"</note> or bitter bulbs are smeared on;
It will also be beneficial to immerse the body in **sea waters**.
Place a suckling **puppy** upon the limbs;
It is agreed that every evil is said to pass into it <note>This describes the practice of "transference," where a disease is believed to leave the human body and enter an animal</note>,
To which, once it has died, the duty of burial is owed.
Such great evils follow human contact
That a spouse joined to another might draw the sickness from their partner.
As for what remains, you shall join the **bile of a dragon** <note>original: "fel draconi"; likely referring to the plant dracunculus or a specific sea-fish like the weaver-fish, rather than the mythical beast</note> with such a vessel;
Let it be given from ten things which are taken and called for:
**Mastic** <note>a resin from the mastic tree</note> shall be added, along with a thousand grains of crushed **spelt**.
With such aids, the precordia will settle safely.

<summary>This page concludes the treatment for the spleen, noting its traditional link to "Sardonic laughter," and begins a series of remedies for the precordia using poultices of mead, quince, and a ritual transference of disease to a puppy.</summary>

### For pain in the loins and to heal the kidneys.

<margin>Palladius</margin>

**C** when a deep-seated injury settles within the **loins**,
**I** n surging waters, a glowing stone from the fire is placed <note>This refers to the practice of "quenching" a hot stone in water or wine to create a medicinal steam or a mineral-infused drink</note>.
**H** aving drunk this, one can overcome the wretched pain;
**A** lternatively, mix **nitre** <note>a naturally occurring mineral salt, often potassium nitrate</note>, **sulfur**, and **vinegar** with **honey**;
**S** oaked in these falling drops, **greasy wool** <note>original: "succida lana"; unwashed wool containing natural lanolin, which was believed to help the skin absorb medicinal oils</note> is used as a compress.
**P** lacing this frequently upon the suffering limbs will be beneficial.
**A** nd take the head of an **asparagus** with old **wine**,
**S** imilarly, you might apply it; this method is certainly effective.
**A** lternatively, apply **sulfur** mixed with **fat** to the loins.
**S** ometimes you will add **celandine** with the juice of **sorrel** <note>original: "lapaery", likely a corruption of "lapathi"</note>,
**T** ogether with medicinal **earths** <note>original: "tarra", likely "terra"; specific clays were used for their cooling or drawing properties</note> that benefit when sweating over flickering flames.
**F** urthermore, it is said that the juice of the **almond** nut should be drunk;
**P** ounded and warm, it is swallowed after being added to water.
**A** lternatively, boil three **snails** in wine to be cooked,
**C** rushing them along with their shells; then add **pepper**—
**T** hree times five grains <note>fifteen grains of pepper</note>—and you will be helped by that drink.
**F** ine **bran**, when it has been heated with a little water,
**P** oured over with **oil** and mixed with suitable juices,
**Q** uite helpfully should be added to the burning limbs that can barely endure the pain.
**P** rofit comes also from **veal** <unclear>veal</unclear> **marrow**; for it is said
**H** ealing comes to those who have drunk a liquid cooked from **stags**,
**A** s well as a mixture of tender **alyssum** <note>original: "hlysagon"; an herb traditionally used to treat madness or rabies, but here used for physical pain</note> with water.
**C** rushed **dill** combined with **mastic** <note>a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used for its anti-inflammatory properties</note> also provides relief.
**Q** uite clearly, these things are composed according to the name <note>original: "ex note clarum est"; a reference to the acrostic structure of the poem, where the first letters of each line likely spell out a mnemonic or the name of a remedy</note>.

<meta>This page continues the medical prescriptions attributed to Serenus Sammonicus. To these, join the place; the gods have proven these things to me so,

->**For pain in the belly.**<-

If an insane pain rages through the entire belly,
Boil strong **rue** <note>original: "rutam"; a bitter herb used since antiquity for digestive issues, though toxic in high doses</note> in water with three parts [of liquid]
And **celery** <note>original: "apium"; often referring to wild celery or smallage</note> until a double portion of the water is gone;
The drink will be a healer, with the palate not refusing the taste.
Furthermore, the shell of a sterile, snowy-white **egg** is burned,
Which, after it has been reduced to a dark ash,
Is drunk from hot waters; and having been drunk, it heals.
A new branch of **tamarisk** <note>original: "myricae"; a shrub associated with cleansing and astringent properties</note> should not be smeared on the belly,
Let it be carried in these vessels, provided it is untouched by **iron** <note>The avoidance of iron in harvesting or preparing herbs is a common theme in ancient "sympathetic" medicine, believed to preserve the plant's magical or natural potency</note>.
But it is beneficial to sprinkle the middle of the belly with **earth**
Which the pressed tracks of **wheels** have marked <note>This folk remedy suggests using the fine, compressed dust from a roadway, perhaps for its mineral content or symbolic connection to "movement"</note>.
Dust snatched from under a smooth-moving **hinge** will also help;
And it will be suitable to drink **cumin** ground in five [parts] of water,
As records have already proven to us.
And **mint**, swallowed in this manner, will provide medicinal flavors.
But you will restore those suffering from **colic** <note>original: "Ciliacos"; referring to those with "celiac" or general intestinal distress</note> with healthful bread,
Which you will make from soaked grain or soft eggs,
Whose shell has first dissolved in sharp **vinegar**.
It is also useful to soften smooth **bulbs** by grinding them,
And with these, to drink the harsh stream of **Lyaeus** <note>original: "lenei fluentum"; Lyaeus is an epithet for Bacchus, meaning the "loosener," here signifying wine</note>.

->**For dropsy.**<- <note>original: "hydropysum"; a condition known today as edema, the swelling caused by excess fluid trapped in the body's tissues</note>

**Dropsy** grows from the defect of a corrupted **liver** and a bitter **spleen**,
Or when the marrow is dried out by fever...

<meta>This page continues the medical advice of Serenus Sammonicus. The preceding text discussed various remedies for abdominal pain, ending with a mention of mint. This page completes the treatment for dropsy (the "watery illness") and transitions into remedies for constipation.</meta>

And the greedy throat has drawn in the cold liquid,
When the water swells within as the wine increases,
Separating the wretched skin from its own flesh <note>This vividly describes the swelling of the skin associated with edema or dropsy, where fluid accumulates between the tissue and the skin</note>.
A decoction of the tender root of **elderberry** <note>original: "sabuci" for *sambuci*; elderberry was commonly used as a diuretic</note> will be suitable,
Doubling the cups of warm pure wine <note>original: "meri"; the OCR reads "heri", but the context of "cyathos potando" suggests *merum*, undiluted wine</note> to be drunk.
The seed of the **ash tree** <note>original: "frassineum semen"</note> should be drunk with the dew of Bacchus <note>A poetic term for wine</note>.
Furthermore, the limbs are treated with an applied **pitch-plaster** <note>term: <term>dropace</term> (from Greek *dropax*); a medical plaster made of pitch and oil used to stimulate the skin or remove hair</note>
Or a simple remedy softens the spine with warm wine <note>original: "lyacho"; a reference to Lyaeus, an epithet for Bacchus/Dionysus as the loosener of care</note>,
Which, suddenly snatched away, pulls out winds and impurities;
Also, let the sufferer roll his body in warm sands.
And light **catnip** <note>original: "nepite" for *nepeta*</note> applied will help the palate and the taste.
Often, harmful things are driven out by "forest wine" <note>likely a reference to a wild herbal infusion or fermented drink</note>.
The "watery error" <note>original: "lymphaticus error"; refers to the imbalance of fluids in dropsy</note> is well purged with **hellebore** <note>original: "helleboro"; a potent and toxic plant used in antiquity as a violent purgative to "reset" the humors</note>,
Or in its place, the seed of the rocky **broom** <note>original: "genesta"</note>
Should be mixed with mead and taken in drafts;
And he who mixes very strong **vinegar** with parched **salt**,
And refreshes the pale limbs with the juice of **oil**,
With which ointment he breaks the strength of the watery illness.

->**To loosen the belly**<-

Often, because of the time between meals or by a fault of the body itself,
The bowels will be delayed, restricted by hardened pores.
Such delay will be overcome by the herb **mercurialis** <note>Mercury herb, often used as a laxative in medieval and ancient medicine</note>,
The water of which they shall drink boiled with a very little **honey**.
**Plums** <note>original: "pruna"</note> are also suitable, which famous **Damascus** sends <note>Damson plums (from "Damascene") have been celebrated since Roman times for their digestive benefits</note>.
Moreover, many medical juices may be composed...

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous discussion on treating dropsy, the author now transitions from remedies for fluid retention to those for constipation (the "obstructions" of the belly), emphasizing accessible, low-cost ingredients before moving into the opposite affliction: dysentery and diarrhea.</meta>

Though precious things may be absent, yet when you come to buy, 
You will be deceived in vain, for you will pour out immense sums of money <note>The author frequently warns against expensive, exotic medicines, advocating for simple, local cures instead</note>. 
Come now, and recognize health even in humble things. 
Either, therefore, you place greasy wool beneath the straight belly <note>original: "lana supponis"; wool soaked in oil, wine, or vinegar was a common topical treatment in Roman medicine to soothe the abdomen</note>, 
Or you eat remedies cooked in pans with tender **beans** <note>original: "fabucis," a diminutive or variation of *faba* (bean)</note>, 
Or you take salted honey-wine with **goat's milk**. 
Yet believe that the drink of **mare's milk** is better; 
Taken thus, it will conquer the stubborn diseases of the donkey <note>original: "asinae... morbos"; this likely refers to ailments traditionally treated with donkey milk, which was highly prized in antiquity for its medicinal properties</note>. 
They say the "tear" <note>Referring to the sap or resin</note> of the **wild tamarisk** <note>original: "fafici" is likely a corruption of *tamarici*</note> is beneficial. 
But it is much better where we learn from Horace <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace, *Satires* 2.4.27-28, which discusses the laxative properties of shellfish</note>: 
The **mussel** and the cheap **shellfish** will drive away obstructions. 
But first, the **snails** must be fed on nettles or bran; 
This cleaner taste moves the bowels without harm. 
Often, earthworms <note>original: "umbri" likely referring to *lumbrici* (earthworms), which were used in various ancient medicinal preparations</note> are piled up with opposing honey; 
These purge the body if they are mixed in equal weight.

## For excessive bowel movements and dysentery.

SO MANY various misfortunes weary the generations of mortals, 
That bodies are liable to ailments in their separate members. 
For often the force of the belly flows with too much speed; 
**Boiling cabbage** with mixed wine curbs it. 
Or the fruit of the **cherry**, preserved now for a long time, 
Or the juice of the **Aminean vine** <note>term: <term>Aminean vine</term>; a high-quality grape variety famous in the Roman world, praised by Virgil and Pliny for its superior wine</note> with bread heals it. 
**Honey** well mixed with warm **vinegar** is drunk; 
A cheap thing of easy making, yet a useful drink 
If the purgatives given by the physician's hand should cause harm <note>The author suggests this honey-vinegar mixture as a corrective if a previous laxative treatment was too aggressive</note>.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions of Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous section on relieving constipation with simple foods like mussels and snails, the text now pivots to treatments for the opposite condition—chronic diarrhea and dysentery—before moving into the treatment of intestinal parasites (worms).</meta>

Warm water mixed with a dusting of **pepper** <note>original: "piperis"; black pepper was a valuable spice used both for flavor and as a warming medicinal stimulant</note> is a remedy.
But if the internal parts run with a long-lasting flux, 
And this terrible evil has been spreading for a long time,
The roasted **shell of an egg** <note>original: "cortex... ovi"; powdered eggshells provided calcium and acted as an astringent to "bind" the bowels</note> shall be drunk with wine.
They believe that the shavings of snowy **ivory** <note>original: "elephantae"; shavings from elephant tusks were a rare and highly prized astringent in ancient pharmacy</note> are beneficial.
Bark boiled from a **pear tree** shall be cooked;
Mixed thus with black **wine** <note>original: "nigro... Lyaeo"; Lyaeus is a poetic name for Bacchus, the god of wine. "Black wine" refers to a dark, heavy red wine</note> under the ethereal sky,
This drink is able to restrain the immense flow.
Or, for the exit of the humor, you may mix **cedar** with **mastic** <note>original: "mastice"; a resin from the mastic tree (Pistacia lentiscus), used for its aromatic and medicinal properties</note>,
And softened **pitch** and the **grease** scraped from ships,
Which, when applied, will soothe the weak limbs with the medicine.
It will also be useful for those whom the decay of the teeth fatigues
To have cooked **chestnuts** and aged **service-berries** <note>original: "sorba"; the fruit of the Sorbus domestica tree, known for being extremely astringent when overripe</note>,
As well as **celery** and the stems which the sharp **bramble** sends forth;
A potion that, when consumed, will halt the fluid belly.

->For expelling roundworms and tapeworms<-

What does Nature not provide for the use of mortals? 
When rightfully the **tapeworm** <note>original: "tinea"; though it usually means a moth or maggot, in medical texts it refers to flatworms or tapeworms</note> and the gluttonous **roundworm** <note>original: "lumbricus"</note> 
Are created together as internal enemies—
A kind that tears at the vitals with a constant bite,
And often, climbing up, it sticks in the filled throat
And, panting, shuts off the blocked ways of life.
Therefore, a draft of the ash of a **hartshorn** <note>original: "cornu cervini"; the antlers of a stag were burned to create a medicinal ash rich in minerals</note> will be beneficial,
Or crushed **catmint** <note>original: "nepitae"</note> with wine or goat's milk.
Nor is a juice of a little vinegar drunk in vain here.

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Serenus Sammonicus. Following the treatment for flux and tooth decay on the previous page, the author here concludes remedies for the stomach and mind before moving into specific treatments for "colic"—a term used in antiquity for severe abdominal pain—and "calculus," or bladder stones.</meta>

Moreover, **peach leaf** <note>original: "psica frondis"; likely a contraction of *persica*. Peach leaves were used in traditional medicine for their sedative and diuretic properties</note> taken with **wine** <note>original: "baccho"; the poet uses the name of Bacchus, the god of wine, to represent the drink itself</note> is beneficial.
**Democritus** <note>the famous Greek "laughing philosopher" (c. 460–370 BCE), often cited in ancient medical texts for his works on nature and health</note> records that this drink is suitable for the mind.
Choose **southernwood** <note>original: "abrotanum"; a woody herb related to wormwood, used to treat parasites and digestive issues</note> and also common **black cumin** <note>original: "melantum"; Nigella sativa, highly prized in the ancient world as a "cure-all" for lung and digestive ailments</note>.
**Hollyhock** <note>original: "alea", likely "alcea"; the marsh-mallow plant, known for its soothing, mucilaginous properties</note> often heals, or the power of **coriander**.
Indeed, even **horehound** <note>original: "marubium"; a bitter herb used then, as now, for respiratory and digestive health</note> boiled and drunk will be of help.
Or let powerful **germander** <note>original: "poleum"; Teucrium polium, a traditional medicinal herb of the Mediterranean</note> and wild **dill** be joined.
This mixture is beneficial when softened with warm water.

### For soothing colic pain.

When **colic** <note>original: "colus"; refers to "colicus dolor," a generic term for agonizing intestinal or abdominal cramping</note>, that hateful kind of disease, seizes the internal parts,
Eat the **crested lark** <note>original: "galeritam"; the Alaudidae bird. Pliny the Elder also mentions that eating this bird or wearing its heart could cure colic</note>, a bird which they call by that name.
Or drink the soaked **rennet** <note>original: "coagula"; the curdled milk found in the stomach of a nursing animal, used as a digestive aid</note> of a fearful **hare**.
Take **catmint** <note>original: "nepitas"; Nepeta cataria, used for its calming effects on the digestive system</note> crushed with **celery** and join them with **mastic** <note>a resin from the mastic tree</note>,
And also add both kinds of **dill**;
The patient shall swallow the boiled liquids of these.

### For a stone in the bladder.

If for anyone the slow fluid of the **bladder** shall linger <note>referring to urinary retention caused by kidney or bladder stones</note>,
This delay shall be broken, conquered by **aged wine**.
It is beneficial to drink the berries from small **elder trees** <note>original: "sabucis"; elderberries were frequently used as diuretics</note>.
Or the juice of **ivy**, or crushed pungent **mustard** <note>original: "lachrymosi... sinapi"; literally "tearful mustard," referring to its sharp, eye-watering heat</note>,
As well as **resins** produced from the **terebinth tree** <note>original: "therebinto"; the Pistacia terebinthus, the source of "Chian turpentine," used to treat infections and stones</note>;
You shall roll these into small spheres in the manner of herbs,
Which, when soft and mixed with warm medicine, are meant to be drunk.
Sometimes also, when the urine flows with too much fluid <note>this refers to the opposite condition: incontinence or bedwetting</note>,
And it drenches the bedding and enters the dark shadows;
Then the ash of **catmint** will be beneficial when drunk in a potion.

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Serenus Sammonicus, a 2nd-century Roman author. The text transitions from the treatment of internal "colic" pain to the specific ailment of bladder stones (calculi). It then shifts to a new section regarding fertility and the difficulties of conceiving children, blending herbalism with folk magic typical of the era.</meta>

Then it will be fitting to drink the **hare's brain** <note>original: "leporis cerebrum"</note> with **wine**
If pain burns within the internal cavity of the **bladder**.
The root and leaf of the **cypress** <note>original: "cupressi"; used in antiquity as an astringent and for urinary issues</note>, when drunk, will give aid,
Or powerful **germander** <note>original: "poleium"; Teucrium polium, an herb believed to have strong diuretic properties</note> tamed by boiling water.
But if the thick humor congeals into **stones** <note>original: "lapides"; referring to bladder or kidney stones (calculi)</note>
Which hold back the flow <note>of urine</note> and weary one with groans of pain,
They believe that **saxifrage** <note>original: "saxifragam"; literally "the stone-breaker," a plant named for its supposed ability to dissolve stones</note> or **hart’s-tongue fern** <note>original: "frongiceũ", likely *phygitidem* or similar; a fern used for liver and urinary health</note> gives succor.
Moreover, let the seed of **wild myrtle** be drunk with **wine** <note>original: "lyacho"; an epithet for Bacchus, the god of wine</note>
And mixed with oil, unless vinegar is lacking.
And together they take the fruit preserved in **sorrel** <note>original: "rumino"; the plant *Rumex*, known for its acidic leaves</note>.
Or the fierce, sharp smoke of **wood-pigeons’ heads**,
Sprinkled with bittersweet juices, ground and dissolved.
Nor will the foul liquid of a **goat** <note>original: "capre humor"; likely referring to goat urine, which was frequently prescribed in ancient texts to break down stones</note> be drunk in vain;
This overcomes the disease and releases the weakened stones.

---

### For conception and childbirth.

If the duties of a sterile marriage languish without fruit,
And the hope for **offspring** <note>original: "sobolis"</note> has been vain for many years,
Let it be determined whether the matter arises from a **female fault** <note>original: "femineo uitio"; ancient medical theory often attempted to diagnose which partner was the cause of infertility</note> or not.
The fourth part of a **great lizard** <note>original: "magni lacerti"; animal parts were often used in "sympathetic magic" or early pharmacology for fertility</note> will be able to show this.
But yet, the condition is overcome by a powerful medicine:
Either the woman consumes the **heart of a hare**,
Or, when a sheep in the stables chews its gathered herbs,
She <note>the woman</note> draws the hanging **foam** from its soft mouth—
And remembers to drink it mixed with **Falernian wine** <note>one of the most famous and highest-quality wines of the Roman world</note>.
Just as **mercury-herb** <note>original: "mercurialis"; *Mercurialis annua*, an herb historically associated with the god Mercury and used to influence the sex of a child or aid conception</note> is commonly taken with other herbs.

<meta>This page continues the medical poem of Serenus Sammonicus, a 2nd-century Roman scholar. Having previously discussed urinary ailments and the initial stages of conception, the author now focuses on the health of the pregnant mother and the risks of premature labor. The text then transitions to a new chapter on "hemostatics"—the ancient methods used to stop excessive bleeding or hemorrhages.</meta>

If at night both spouses hasten to the marriage bed,
And pregnancy now promises a certain **offspring** <note>original: "foetum"</note>,
So that the **woman in labor** <note>original: "puerpera"</note>, once accustomed to the task, may thrive with an easy birth,
We prescribe that **dittany** <note>original: "dictamus"; a medicinal herb from Crete famous in antiquity for its ability to ease childbirth and expel arrows</note> be drunk and edible **snails** <note>original: "cocleæ"</note> be eaten.
But if [the child] lingers in the womb for less than eight months,
And breaks into the chambers, loosening the weak bonds of the womb,
Then the juice of cleaned **pennyroyal** <note>original: "pulei"; *Mentha pulegium*, an aromatic herb used in ancient folk medicine to stimulate or regulate the uterus</note> is suitable;
Various cases have often proven this to be a true help.
Furthermore, place five eggs of an **ostrich** <note>original: "struti"</note> or a **black vulture** <note>original: "vulturis atri"</note> beneath her,
So she may feel the least pain as the birth presses near.
These are mixed with **rue** <note>original: "ruta"</note> and fragile **dill** <note>original: "aneto"</note>,
Which, when diluted with wine and drained, provide healing.

### For the excessive flow of blood of any kind

If a moderate amount of blood flows from the full body,
It is helpful; but it is excessive when life itself is poured out.
**Burdock** <note>original: "lappa"</note>, filled with much varied blood, [gives aid];
The long-eared **bedbug** <note>original: "cimex"</note> finishes [the flow], though it confuses with its odor.
**Wool** <note>original: "lana"</note>, if not yet washed, is moistened with **rose oil**;
This is placed in the nostrils and closes the thick ears.
<margin>It is beneficial</margin>
It is useful to bind the forehead with crushed **snails**,
Or with the brain of a **rooster** <note>original: "galli"</note> and the blood of a snowy **dove** <note>original: "columbæ"</note>;
And if the blood is not suppressed, even the blood itself must be drunk. <note>A form of "sympathetic medicine" where the ailment is treated with a substance similar to the problem.</note>
Furthermore, it is proper to measure out Egyptian **papyrus** <note>original: "papyris"</note>;
Remove what remains, and bind the genitals with another cloth;
It is also helpful to bind the female **breasts** <note>original: "mamillas"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions of Serenus Sammonicus, following the discussion on stopping excessive hemorrhages. If menstrual blood <note>original: "menstruus... sanguis"</note> flows forth with an immense force,
Unwashed wool <note>original: "sucida lana"; wool still containing its natural oils or lanolin, believed to have healing properties</note>, inserted below, restrains the harmful courses.
It also brings forth dead bodies <note>refers to a miscarried or stillborn fetus</note> from the womb.
But wherever an immoderate force of blood flows,
The bark of a cork tree <note>original: "subernus cortex"</note> is drunk in warm water,
Having first been ground very finely with diligent effort.
A woman may also be purged by a warm drink of **pennyroyal** <note>original: "pulei"; an aromatic herb often used to regulate the reproductive system</note>.
But if by chance blood lingers in the closed belly,
They drink either **catmint** <note>original: "nepeta"</note> or **rue** <note>original: "ruta"</note> for a **mole** <note>original: "moles"; a term used in ancient medicine for a non-viable mass or growth in the uterus</note>;
Or if the bladder pours out crimson fluids,
Ground **horehound** <note>original: "marubui"; a bitter herb of the mint family</note> is poured out with water and raisin wine;
These draughts will be able to clear away the reddish color.
The herb **dittany** <note>original: "dictami"</note> draws out the remains of a birth.
And if a rain of female blood should hasten,
And what runs down in vain from the stroke of a single mass,
Which voice binds something right to the heart <note>original: "praecordia"; literally the area around the heart or diaphragm</note>.
These things together ought to stop the incautious blood,
So that the stone of the road may now cease its loosened orbits <note>This likely refers to an amulet or a specific rhythmic incantation used to "bind" or stop the flow, a common practice in Roman folk medicine</note>.

**For hair loss: so that hairs do not grow back once they have been plucked.**

Noble playfulness shudders at hairy bristles,
And rejoices in being stripped of the "flower" of hairs.
But sometimes there is seen to be a good reason for plucking,
When a clear concern for health relieves the body.
For the hostile weapons of hairs provoke the eyes <note>refers to trichiasis, a condition where eyelashes grow inward and irritate the eyeball</note>—
And that which was lacking to protect them strongly...

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions of Serenus Sammonicus, finishing the previous chapter on the permanent removal of unwanted hair and beginning a new chapter on ailments of the genitals and groin.</meta>

...from there the hostile crop <note>original: "seges"; here used metaphorically for unwanted hair</note> rages in its own circles.
Therefore, touch the place of the plucked hair with blood,
Which a bird <note>likely a bat, described here by its "trembling skins" rather than feathers</note> gives with trembling skins and wings,
Or with the blood which you would pluck from a nearby black dog.
Moreover, whatever bristles <note>original: "setas"; coarse hairs</note> you wish [to remove],
And to block them from reviving forever with a remedy,
Apply the discharge <note>original: "saniem"; can mean blood, lymph, or secretion</note> of a frog to the plucked skin.
But use the one which is small in size and talkative with a hoarse complaint;
Not that one which stands out raw in stagnant pools.
It is taken alive and boiled in a healthy pot;
Anoint the limbs with this mixed with acidic liquids,
And it forbids the plucked forest <note>metaphor for thick hair</note> to grow again.

->**For the private parts**<-

One must also speak of what the medicine for the private parts should be.
A sluggish penis is cleansed with ancient **wine** <note>original: "Iaccho"; a poetic name for Bacchus or wine</note>
And it is smeared on top with the bile of a fertile she-goat.
In the early morning, it is beneficial to chew leaves of **myrtle** <note>original: "myrthi"</note>.
He who suffers in the armpits <note>likely referring to sores or foul odors</note> should spit into the wound itself.
Washed with the dregs of wine, the genitals are relieved,
And indeed, water restrains swollen testicles.
Or bulbs <note>original: "bulbi"; likely referring to muscari or wild onions</note> in honeyed wine and wax mixed with **cypress** <note>original: "cupresso"</note>,
Or a bean boiled with the warm streams of **wine** <note>original: "bachi"</note>,
Is said to check an immense swelling of a **rupture** <note>term: <term>Ramex</term> refers to a hernia or a varicose vein in the scrotum</note>.
Spelt soaked in a little water with a branch of cypress—
Whence also this leaf is remembered as wonderful in a drink.

<meta>This page continues the medical treatments for the groin and genitals from the previous page, transitioning into remedies for sciatica, joint pain, and the beginning of a section on boils.</meta>

But if a hard swelling exists in the glands of your groin,
It will be beneficial to apply small snails crushed with **honey**.
If new wounds seize the sensitive <note>original: "obscenos," referring to the private parts</note> areas of the penis,
They are dreaded, but are cured by chewing the foliage of **brambles** <note>original: "ruborum"; likely blackberry or raspberry leaves</note>.
But if a fistula <note term="fistula">a chronic, abnormal tunnel-like sore that develops in the body</note> follows an old disease,
The ash of a **weasel**, once applied, will purge the ulcer,
Along with the blood which a nearby cow has carried in her anus <note>A bizarre folk remedy characteristic of the era's belief in sympathetic or animal-based magic</note>.
The herb **celandine** <note>original: "celidonia"; a yellow-flowering plant in the poppy family</note> is said to heal when mixed with honey,
As is the herb called "thousand-leaved" <note>original: "mille vocata"; referring to yarrow or milfoil</note> with tallow and its own leaves.

### For diseases of sciatica and the joints.

Often a hidden disease rages with the toll of life
And delays one's steps with a harsh exhaustion.
The **white poplar** will provide medicinal drinks from its bark,
As will the **broom** <note>original: "genesta"; a shrub with slender green branches</note> from its tender, slender foliage.
It will be fitting to take gathered stalks soaked in an acidic liquid,
And a red-colored <note>original: "rubentem"; possibly referring to a red lead mixture or simply a red-tinted potion</note> drink to lead the way,
Or to swallow snails brought from **Bithynia** <note>A region in modern-day Turkey, famous in antiquity for specific medicinal ingredients</note>.
But if such pain has stuck through all the joints as an enemy,
You will bind together berries from a **fig tree** with honey,
Or you will take sea water along with the gifts of Bacchus <note>original: "baccheia dona"; a poetic term for wine</note>;
But spare yourself from too much wine.
Ennius himself, the father <note>referring to Quintus Ennius, the "Father of Roman Poetry," who famously suffered from gout</note>, while draining cups into water,
Is said to have earned such pains by this very vice.

### On healing a boil.

When excessive blood is mixed with pure blackness...

<keywords>groin swelling, snails, honey, fistula, celandine, yarrow, sciatica, joint pain, white poplar, broom plant, Bithynia, Bacchus, Ennius, gout, boils</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on joint pain and sciatica, moving into the treatment of inflammatory skin conditions, specifically pustules and the dangerous "carbuncle" (anthrax or severe boils).</meta>

If it burns and raises sharp **pimples** <note>original: "papulas"</note> high,
You should mix **resin** and crushed **barley** grown among rocks;
It is proper to apply these scattered in warm water.
**Henbane** <note>original: "hyocyamon"; a toxic plant in the nightshade family used historically as a sedative and analgesic</note> also helps, joined with **wax** and **tallow**.
A hectic fever <note>original: "Hecterē"; likely referring to a persistent, wasting fever</note> is no less able to be helped by this medicine.
Furthermore, take the **white dung** which you have gathered from a **rooster**,
And apply it to the aching part, sitting in acidic liquids <note>original: "imbribus ex acidis"; likely referring to vinegar</note>.
Also, press out the soft **earwax** of cattle;
With this ointment, you will give medicine to the cherished <note>original: "fotis"; can also mean "warmed" or "treated"</note> nipples.

### For curing a carbuncle

The **carbuncle** <note term="carbuncle">In antiquity, this referred to a "burning" tumor or a malignant skin ulcer, often associated with anthrax, which could be fatal.</note> is more horrific because it weighs upon the body,
It burns and, when enclosed, breaks the vital structures.
The ancients once drove this away with various cures:
For the one hundred and third sheet of **Titus**'s linen book <note>A reference to the *Liber Medicinalis* of Serenus Sammonicus, citing an earlier source named Titus.</note>
Teaches that such a pain should be cut out with a **red-hot iron**,
Or driven out by a drink of **turnip seeds**.
It infects the person, and it is said that life can hardly be
Prolonged for **seven days**, so great is the violence of the disease.
Then, you shall anoint it with an acidic liquid and **coriander seed**,
And likewise compose a mixture of **pigeon dung** and **papyrus**.
From this, smear the hardened parts and the enclosed poisons.
Furthermore, let them bring the pods <note>original: "adopta"; refers to the husks or shells</note> of the **lupine** stem.
Some dissolve **lime** in strong **vinegar**,
And apply the "smoking snow" <note>The chemical reaction between lime and vinegar creates heat and effervescence, resembling "smoking snow" or white foam.</note> to the white pimples.
There is also that which a **chicken** produces from its dung for the body.

**Marshmallow** <note>original: "Alcea"</note> and **pepper**, mixed in small amounts, are smeared on; and that plant related to **Pythagoras** <note>original: "Pythagorae cognata"; likely referring to mustard, which the philosopher Pythagoras was said to have held in high medicinal regard</note>, seasoned with a little **cumin**.
<margin>+</margin> A moist leaven of fine flour, pressed on, will also be beneficial.

### *For dispelling a swelling, a wart, and a scrofulous tumor.*
<note term="struma">A scrofulous tumor (struma) refers to a swelling of the lymph nodes, usually in the neck, often associated with tuberculosis in a historical context.</note>

There are other hard **tumors** that cause distress, which are no less horrific, but not as deadly. Whatever the **abscess** <note>original: "Vomica"</note> may be, there is a similar remedy: we will mix **Siphnian resin** <note>original: "Simphoe resina"; resin originating from the Greek island of Siphnos, valued for its drawing properties</note> with crushed **barley**, honeyed water, and the **droppings of a dove**. When these are well-boiled, they soothe the weary limbs. Let the soft **bean** be joined with fragrant **coriander**; when applied, it helps by softening and opening the tumor.

Or use a root of **marsh-mallow** <note>original: "salgamo... palustri"</note> boiled, which should be pounded with enough seasoning mixed in. **Mistletoe** <note>original: "viscum"</note> gathered from a tree and chewed will also help. Combine grass, **henbane**, wax, and old tallow, and smear the aching spots with the mixture.

Try this, and let the powerful medicine enter the body: pour an egg into an earthenware vessel, then fill the shell with the juice of **horehound** <note>original: "Marrubij"</note>, and then with liquid honey. Let all these combined be taken as a beneficial warm drink; they will drive away the illness, both purging and relieving it.

->For the sacred fire<-
<note term="sacred fire">Historically known as *ignis sacer*, this term most often referred to **erysipelas**, a bacterial skin infection characterized by a bright red, "burning" rash, or sometimes to shingles or ergotism.</note>

There is also a kind of disease which is called **fire**, by which the weary limbs are scorched with great heat.

Or you may melt ox tallow <note>original: "sevum bovis"</note> over a gentle fire <note>original: "murino igne"; literally 'mouse-fire,' a term for a very low, slow heat</note> and, once softened, smear it on; and if you provide eggs with the dregs of vegetables <note>original: "oleris faecibus"</note> without being cooked, you will soothe the scorched limbs in this way.
Earthworms mixed with vinegar are applied; or fresh rue <note>original: "ruta"</note> in a like manner, but you should add oil.
Eggs with crushed beets are also beneficial when smeared on.
Marshmallow <note>original: "Alcea"</note> <note>The transcription reads 'Alea,' likely a scribal error for 'Alcea' or 'Allia' (garlic)</note> provides ashes to be rubbed with oil and garum <note term="garum">A fermented fish sauce used frequently in antiquity both as a condiment and a medical solvent</note>; 
with this ointment, the glowing fire <note>a reference to the "sacred fire" or skin infection</note> will lay aside its fury.
Often the white part of an egg serves as celandine <note>original: "celidonie"</note>; 
and you should take these things crushed in a small, simple drink; 
I pray you add water and a flow of Falernian wine <note>a prestigious and strong wine from Italy, often used in medicine for its potency</note>.

->**For Gout.**<-
---

There are certain medicines worthy of raging gout <note term="podagra">Gout, specifically when it affects the joints of the foot, causing intense pain and swelling</note>, of which the God of Epidaurus <note>Aesculapius, the Greco-Roman god of healing</note> himself said there are thirty types; 
he claimed to bring rest to the disease and at least soothe the grim pain.
Therefore, come, take the leaf and the bark of the willow <note>original: "salicem"</note> and grind them with wine; in this way, smear the contracted tendons. 
Or when the first origin of the evil is noticed, endure hot cauteries <note>the medical practice of burning the skin to draw out "humors" or seal tissue</note> on the soles of the feet, and do not be afraid. 
Keep the [calcined remains] of a goat <note>original: "hurci" for "hirci"</note> from the chest; 
apply them, and thus you will repress the beginnings of the cruel plague. 
But if the corrupt humor <note>in medieval medicine, diseases were caused by an imbalance of the four bodily fluids or 'humors'</note> has settled deeper, 
crushed cypress <note>original: "cupressus"</note> shall be smeared there with bitter wine; 
and bread added to the mixture will restrain the growths.

Likewise, small elder <note>original: "sabucus"</note> is used, smeared with goat tallow,
or the juice of celandine <note>original: "celidonie"</note> mixed with salt and vinegar.
There are some for whom dried swallow's blood <note>original: "hirundinis sanguis"</note> is applied;
let it be lawful for me to report things not just heard, but read.
A certain man, driven mad by this disease <note>referring to the pain of gout</note> during the time of harvest,
