and the heavy pain vanished by this accidental medicinal chance.

**For wounds made by iron, or to the breasts, or for bruises.**

We have opposed medical arts to the flaws of nature;
it is likewise right to resist the "spears of fortune" <note>a poetic way of describing accidental injuries</note>.
<margin>ground ivy</margin>
If by chance a stone has injured tender limbs,
calf fat <note>original: "adipe vituli"</note> and crushed ground ivy <note>original: "cameroson"; likely from the Greek 'chamaikissos'</note> are used correctly.
Nor should one be ashamed to produce the dung of a muddy sow <note>Animal excrement was a common, if unpleasant, ingredient in ancient and medieval "dirt pharmacy" for drawing out heat or infection</note>,
if the body is cruelly and severely torn.
Incense <note>original: "thura"</note>, resin, ash, wax, and eggs dissolved together,
with oil added, compress dark welts <note>original: "vibices"</note>.
If indeed unrestrained blood flows from a wound,
purple cloth scorched with common lichen <note>original: "villi pluta fuco"; 'fucus' refers to a reddish sea-lichen used as a dye</note> [is applied];
by this, one can hold back the flowing wave of the dark stream.
Truly, also, the burnt powder from a Tyrian fleece <note>Wool dyed with expensive Tyrian purple was sometimes used in high-status medical recipes</note> is said to check the blood
when a cut has been made.
Furthermore, fennel <note>original: "feniculum"</note> ash and the trimmings of a leek
bind the excessive "rivers" of an opened wound,
as does the dung of a sea-calf <note>original: "fimus marini"; likely referring to the seal or 'vitulus marinus'</note> when it is burned with eggshells;
it restrains the fluid courses with a wonderful medicine.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions from the previous leaf (103v), transitioning from the treatment of bleeding and bruises into a broader section on general accidental wounds, animal bites (including humans and apes), and the onset of a section on snake venom.</meta>

### For wounds caused by any accidents

Since the heavy burdens of the human condition bring 
So many and so countless chances of injury,
That I cannot assign specific cares to every single one;
+ Therefore, by these instructions, we shall heal all accidents,
Yet in such a way that no error mocks the medicine.
<margin>+</margin> Therefore, whatever kind of dark wound occurs,
A crushed bulb <note>original: "bulbus"; likely a squill or sea-onion, often used in poultices</note> given with honey will provide the remedy.
And the herb that receives its name from a "thousand leaves" <note>original: "mille"; referring to Yarrow (*Achillea millefolium*), famous in antiquity for its wound-healing properties</note>,
When applied, is beneficial if mixed with old lard.
But let greasy wool <note>original: "succida lana"; unwashed wool containing natural lanolin, used to retain heat and medicine</note> be bound to it with warm oil.
Or the ash of a burnt thing will fill the mouth of the wound;
Furthermore, an elm leaf or branch closes [the wound],
Or by the juices of ivy, a foul scar is broken down <note>original: "putrefcit"; here meaning the hard, unsightly scar tissue is softened or resolved</note>.

### For the bite of a man or an ape

If either a man or a beast very similar to us <note>monkeys and apes were often categorized together with humans in early medical texts due to their physical similarities</note>
Has given foul wounds with their teeth, the venom is dark within.
It is beneficial to take betony <note>original: "vetonicam"</note> with strong wine <note>original: "baccho"; Bacchus, the god of wine, is used here as a poetic term for the drink itself</note>,
And also the boiled rind of a radish heals
If it is ground and smeared around the bitten limbs.

### For serpent bites and for driving out poisons

Not by a long spear-point, nor by the slaughter of a Thessalian javelin <note>original: "larissa"; a type of spear named for the city of Larissa</note>,
Not by the flash of a sword, nor by a swift arrow's gall <note>original: "felle"; referring to the use of animal bile or "gall" as a poison for arrow tips</note>,
But one can be quickly afflicted by a viper’s strike.
Why should I speak of the swelling? <note>original: "apostema"; a gathering of fluid or an abscess caused by the bite</note> [I speak of] aid and medicinal ointments.

It is said that the head of the snake that has done harm
Is aptly joined to the wounds; it heals the very person it strikes.
Just as the Telephean spear from Larissa <note>original: "larissæa... hasta"; a mythological reference to Achilles (from Larissa) whose spear wounded King Telephus; the wound would only heal when the rust from that same spear was applied to it</note> brings a cure.
It will also be beneficial to drink the stalk of an elder <note>original: "sabuci"; the elderberry plant</note> with wine,
Or to bind on the cooked and crushed inner bark of a radish.
Or if from a towering cypress tree a leaf is taken,
The milky juice of the spurge <note>original: "caprissi"; likely referring to a species of *Euphorbia* known for its white, irritating latex</note> will flow with a little effort.
Or prick the fierce wound with crushed spurge <note>original: "titimallus"; another name for the spurge plant, used to treat skin conditions and bites</note>.
There is a thistle <note>original: "carduus"; specifically the Fuller’s Teasel, used in the textile industry</note> not yet fit for skilled fullers;
From it, the root is drunk in warm water.
Rennet from a deer's offspring <note>original: "ceruino ex foetu... coagula"; the curdled milk from the stomach of a nursing fawn was a common ingredient in ancient antidotes</note> is mixed with wine
And taken, a substance that drives dark poisons from the limbs.
Or the root of giant fennel <note>original: "ferule"</note> is drunk in rain water,
Or light chicken broths with betony or old stews.
If indeed a wild asp has inflicted a horrific wound,
They believe drinking one's own urine is helpful.
This was the opinion of the elder Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), a prolific Roman scholar</note>, and no less—
<margin>PLINY</margin>
As Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), author of *Natural History*</note> records, a draft of vinegar that has been taken assists.
One must also speak of the medicine for a swelling beast,
For precaution can prevent the pain of harsh bites.
If you carry the cut-off part of a slow vulture,
Or if you have smeared all your limbs with arugula <note>original: "erucis"; garden rocket, which was believed to act as a repellent against venomous animals</note>,
Which you first sprinkle with crushed, strong vinegar.
Or if you rest on a skin <note>likely a vulture skin, as mentioned in the previous lines</note> through the night by the light of the Moon,
Or if you carry an amulet <note>original: "amutu"; a misspelling of *amuletum*</note> made from the tooth [of the beast] itself.

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions regarding antidotes and animal bites from the previous page. Moreover, I will explain the toxins that must be driven out by medicine. A dog’s blood <note>original: "sanguis... caninus"; animal blood was frequently used in ancient pharmacology, often believed to possess sympathetic properties to draw out poisons</note> can be beneficial; when drunk easily, it mimics the effects of noble antidotes. If you are cautious, you should also produce wonderful scents; by burning certain things, even the foulest serpent is kept at bay. You might burn storax <note>original: "storacem"; a fragrant resin used since antiquity in medicine and incense</note>, or the wing of a hardy vulture, or catmint <note>original: "nepitam"</note>, or the branch and foliage of a stiff tamarisk <note>original: "myricæ"</note>.

### On the sting of the scorpion and bites

There is very little hope beneath the wound of such a dire pest, for they lie hidden in their slender bodies and deceive us. The scorpion is grave, and the spider is always wickedly catching us unawares in the deep of the night while we sleep. Prostrate Orion <note>In Greek mythology, the giant hunter Orion was killed by a giant scorpion; this served as a common literary warning that even the greatest heroes can be laid low by the smallest creatures</note> has given us proof of this. Great things are often laid low by tiny venoms. 

And if a burning scorpion has inflicted a fierce wound, its head—once crushed in a deserved slaughter—is said to be suitable for the wounds to draw back the poison. Or, the injured areas may be soothed with warm sea-water <note>original: "calidis pelagi nymphis"; literally "warm nymphs of the sea," a poetic classical reference to sea-water baths</note>. Or the wicked plague is dissolved by a drink of undiluted wine. For all bites and lesser stings, cheese made from the milk of a fearful goat will be suitable. Along with this, the herb oregano <note>original: "origanus"</note> should be taken. These two will cure those ailments with wonderful healing. Also, warm vinegar may be applied with a sponge <note>original: "peniculo"</note>, or native sulfur <note>original: "uiuum sulfur"; unrefined sulfur as it occurs in nature, often used for skin ailments and stings</note> may be rubbed on...

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions from the previous page (105 r). It moves from the treatment of snake and scorpion bites to the injuries caused by moles, and then transitions into remedies for sudden internal pains, bone-aches, and recurring fevers such as the quartan fever.</meta>

Sprinkle a little pepper <note>original: "piperis"; black pepper was a luxury import from India, used both as a spice and a potent drug in antiquity</note> on the brain of a rooster; by this, the limbs heal as the pain is driven away. But if the violence of a blind mole <note>original: "muris caeci"; literally "blind mouse," referring here to the mole</note> has caused harm—that creature which marks its track alone, like the ruts of rolling wagons <note>a poetic description of the raised ridges of earth created by a mole's tunneling</note>—then a wonderful cure is provided by a useful powder...

### For sudden pain, fever, and bone-ache

Sometimes a new cause of sudden pain arises, whose origin remains hidden and must be checked with true remedies. It is beneficial to apply celandine <note>original: "celidoniam"; the name derives from the Greek word for "swallow," as folk tradition held that the birds used the plant's sap to heal their young’s eyes</note> mixed with salt; likewise, warm fleeces of wool soaked in sulfur will heal. For bone-ache <note>original: "ostocopo"; a technical term derived from Greek meaning "bone-striking," used to describe deep joint or bone fatigue</note>, it is helpful to be treated with slow honey. 

But if hot fevers lay hold of the body, then let celery juice <note>original: "apij sucus"</note> be warmed with mild oil; smear the limbs, and the fierce heat will be soothed by the warming. Nor should one despise the fat provided by the badger <note>original: "bestia melus"</note>; it is fitting to mix its essence into a liquid and apply it to limbs that are burning with heat. For long-lasting fevers, a new remedy from an old rooster brings aid to trembling and suffering limbs.

### For the pattern of a quartan fever

Do not believe a fever to be light just because its time is delayed; it grants a span of rest only so that it may burn more intensely afterwards. The thick quartan fever <note>original: "quartana"; a form of malaria where the fever peaks every 72 hours—on the fourth day by the Roman method of inclusive counting</note> burns with a lethal heat. To these sufferers, we bring the healing hand and prescribe herbs. Let no one be ashamed of garlic <note>original: "allea"</note> ground up with three bedbugs <note>original: "cimice"; ancient pharmacology often employed insects in remedies, believing their pungent or "sharp" nature could counteract the humors of a fever</note>, and dittany <note>original: "dictamus"; an aromatic plant from Crete famous in the ancient world for its healing properties</note>.

<summary>This page outlines treatments for injuries from moles and various types of fevers and bone pains, utilizing ingredients ranging from pepper and celery juice to more unusual remedies like badger fat and bedbugs.</summary>
<keywords>mole bite, bone-ache, quartan fever, malaria, celandine, badger fat, ancient pharmacology, garlic, dittany</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the pharmaceutical instructions from page 105r, finishing a section on general remedies before moving into specific treatments for recurring fevers (tertian and daily), which were common ailments in the Roman world, often associated with malaria.</meta>

And drink [this] diluted with pure wine <note>original: "mero"; unmixed wine was often used as a solvent for medicines</note> in the middle of the day. Or take the easily obtained fiber of a mouse's liver, so that four scruples <note>original: "scrupula"; a small unit of weight approximately equal to 1.14 grams</note> of harsh wine <note>original: "duri bacchi"; Bacchus is the god of wine, used here as a poetic term for the drink itself</note> may be joined to it. A potion of wormwood <note>original: "absinthii"; known for its extreme bitterness and used to treat digestive issues and fevers</note> is mixed with plain water. Take three fingers' worth of weighed-out dill seed, and just as much fennel <note>original: "maratri"</note>, and let there be no lack of added vinegar. You should drink these aforementioned things healthfully four times in turn. Place a quartan <note>referring to a fever that recurs every four days</note> remedy under the swelling of the flank <note>original: "ilhadis"; referring to the iliac region or the side of the body</note>, or provide the diluted rennet <note>original: "coagula"; the curdled milk from a stomach, used here as a medicinal binder</note> of a fearful hare. Even the liquid of a flayed mouse is a beneficial drink. Some say marvelous things happen when the heat [of fever or passion] comes: for those seeking the games of Venus <note>a poetic reference to sexual intimacy or vigor</note> and the duty to be sought, first boil a little rue <note>original: "rutam"; a bitter herb used in antiquity as a condiment and an anti-aphrodisiac or medicinal stimulant</note> at the crossroads <note>original: "triviis"; locations where three roads meet were often considered to have magical or medicinal significance</note> and smear the limbs with that juice.

### For the pattern of tertian fever
<note>Tertian fever (febris tertiana) is a fever that returns every third day—meaning Day 1 and Day 3—and was a classic symptom of certain strains of malaria.</note>

There is a fever that revives on alternate days, stretching out the intervals of time like the weights of a balanced scale. So that you may be able to prohibit such madness, wrap grains of cumin without their white husks in wax; collect them in a reddish skin <note>likely a piece of red leather or parchment</note> and bind them. Covered by a thin cloak of wool, this will provide healing scents to the doctor at the suspected time [of the fever's onset]. Furthermore, a bedbug <note>original: "cymax," a variant of "cimex"; ancient medical texts frequently suggested ingesting or wearing bedbugs to cure recurring fevers</note> is drunk, crushed in wine; it is horrid to the sight, but provides a suitable effect through the taste.

### For the pattern of daily fever
<note>Daily fever (febris quotidiana) refers to a fever that recurs every single day, often indicating a different stage or type of malarial infection.</note>

<summary>This page outlines traditional Roman treatments for recurring fevers, utilizing a combination of herbs like wormwood and dill alongside more unusual ingredients such as mouse liver and crushed bedbugs. It emphasizes the timing of the fever's return as the primary way to categorize the illness.</summary>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical instructions from the previous leaf, transitioning from folk remedies for daily fevers to the treatment of the more dangerous "semitertian" fever. It notably contains the first historical record of the "Abracadabra" amulet.</meta>

But for him whom the fever does not cease to approach on continuous days,
But rather the disease marks all the individual hours,
He fears a grain of wheat—if it is worthy of belief—
Which, lurking, was found inside a broken piece of bread.
Bones found within the enclosures of houses are also helpful;
It is suitable to place these under the smooth, hanging neck.
I shall remain silent about many other "monsters of words" <note>original: "verborum monstra"; likely referring to strange or nonsensical magical incantations</note> besides these,
For vain superstition and trembling parents believe
That fevers can be driven away by various charms <note>original: "carmine"; can mean a song, a poem, or a ritual incantation</note>.

### For driving away the semitertian fever.

There is a more deadly [fever] which the Greeks call <term>hemitritaeos</term> <note>original: "emitryteum"; a semi-tertian fever characterized by paroxysms that occur in a specific cycle, often associated with a severe form of malaria</note>.
I think our ancestors were not able to name this
In any common words of our own tongue, nor did they wish to.
You write on a piece of parchment that which is called <term>abracadabra</term> <note>This is the earliest known mention of this famous word, used here as a magical formula to cure fever</note>.
Repeat it many times, but subtract from the end,
And let the letters <note>original: "elementa"; literally 'elements', used here for the letters of the alphabet</note> be missing from the shapes more and more,
Which you will always take away one by one, leaving the rest fixed,
Until the writing is reduced into a narrow cone <note>referring to the classic triangular shape where the word is written repeatedly, losing one letter each time until only 'A' remains at the point</note>.
Remember to bind the neck with these [parchments] tied with linen thread.
Some mention that the fat of a lion is beneficial.
However, if you wish to tie coral to the neck,
Do not hesitate to mix true emeralds <note>original: "smaragdos"</note> with it.
Let there be a smooth pearl of precious, snowy color.
Such bonds benefit the neck of the sick person,
And a wondrous power will drive away the lethal diseases.

<summary>This page describes various charms and amulets for treating persistent fevers, most notably providing the first historical record of the 'Abracadabra' magical formula as a medical remedy.</summary>
<keywords>Abracadabra, hemitritaeos, fever remedies, amulets, Roman medicine, Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, charms, emeralds, coral</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical manual attributed to Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the magical and superstitious cures for fevers on the previous page, the author now transitions into practical—if still archaic—treatments for physical injuries such as bone fractures, head trauma, and hernias.</meta>

# For bone fractures and bruises.

<margin>I</margin> It is unspeakable to tell; let bitter things be far from everyone.
<margin>S</margin> If powerful fortune changes the omen into an enemy,
<margin>V</margin> And if it has shattered the bones with an unworthy force and a new crashing sound,
<margin>C</margin> It will be suitable to add the brain of a soft frog <note>The use of frog brains was a common folk remedy in Roman medicine for soothing inflammation.</note> to the broken parts.
<margin>L</margin> Then bind linen cloths over them, and wool that has been spread with ointment,
<margin>S</margin> Frequently sprinkling them with the soot of a lamp wick <note>original: "sanguis olini"; likely a corruption of "sanguis ellychnii", the oily residue or soot from a lamp, used for its drying properties.</note>.
<margin>b</margin> They believe all things heal within fourteen days <note>original: "bisseptem diebus"; literally "twice seven days".</note>
<margin>A</margin> If the dung of a butting goat is then sought out with old wine <note>original: "ueteri baccho"; Bacchus, the god of wine, is used here to mean the liquid itself.</note>.
<margin>H</margin> This work draws out what is trapped within, and sticking to it, heals best.
<margin>S</margin> But if a hostile blow has struck the head,
<margin>E</margin> The web of a spider <note>original: "aragnes"</note> ought to be bound on with oil;
<margin>H</margin> This warms the area and will not leave until it has healed.
<margin>&</margin> If limbs are dislocated and fall away from their proper position,
<margin>V</margin> Burn womanly hair and join it with animal fat <note>original: "seum"; a variant of "sevum", meaning tallow or hard animal fat.</note>;
<margin>C</margin> It will be fitting to bind it now with such a medical application.
<margin>A</margin> Or it will help to have applied ground-up mallows <note>original: "maluas"</note> gathered from nearby.
<margin>A</margin> <insert>+</insert> If a closed wound causes you distress <note>original: "suabit"; likely a variant of "saevit", meaning to rage or cause pain.</note>,
<margin>S</margin> You will gently mix sheep's dung and aged fats,
<margin>P</margin> Which will be able to open the ulcer and heal it once it is opened.

# For groin hernias <note>original: "Herniis inassis"; likely a scribe's error for "inguinis", referring to the groin area.</note>, both painful and contracted.

<margin>C</margin> Hardly anyone would believe that nerves cast down by a wound
<margin>A</margin> Can be called back again to their accustomed vigor.
<margin>S</margin> It is beneficial to apply earthworms ground up,
<margin>Q</margin> Which should be mixed <gloss>and</gloss> with old and fresh grease <term>axungia</term> <note>original: "axungia"; animal fat, specifically hog's lard, used as a base for ointments.</note>.

<summary>This page provides a series of rhythmic medical recipes for physical traumas, recommending materials like frog brains, spider webs, and goat dung for fractures, head wounds, and hernias. It reflects the Roman "encyclopedic" medical tradition where practical first aid was blended with folk substances.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical manual attributed to Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. While the previous page focused on physical injuries like fractures and dislocations, this section moves into internal medicine and neurological complaints, specifically the treatment of stiffened limbs (possibly paralysis or rheumatism) and the management of sleep—both the inability to sleep due to fever and the opposite condition of pathological lethargy.</meta>

<margin>S</margin> If, however, pain burns the sluggish nerves of the eyes, <note>The Latin "oculis" (eyes) here is likely a scribe’s error for "articulis" (joints), which appears in other versions of this text. The context of "sluggish nerves" and "stiff limbs" suggests a treatment for rheumatism or gout rather than an eye ailment.</note>
<margin>V</margin> Apply melted vulture fat and rue,
<margin>A</margin> Or wax; and with such a warm application, you will revive the languid parts.
<margin>C</margin> It is also suitable to bathe them in the warm waves of the sea,
<margin>C</margin> Or dried figs <gloss>or</gloss> and beets joined with viscous honey,
<margin>A</margin> Or grain <note>original: "Ceres," the goddess of agriculture, used here as a metonym for bread or grain.</note> soaked in wine <note>original: "Baccho," the god of wine, used here as a metonym for the liquid itself.</note> with a cypress leaf,
<margin>Q</margin> By which application you are able to restore stiff limbs.
<margin>S</margin> If again the bodies are bent back by the usual disease, <note>This likely refers to spasms or "opisthotonos," where the body arches backward due to tetanus or other neurological conditions.</note>
<margin>C</margin> Eaten dove meat restores the contracted nerves.

### To induce sleep.

Not only does a most foul fever burn the wretched ones,
But it also proceeds to deprive them of desired sleep,
Lest they be able to enjoy the heavenly gifts of calm sleep.
Therefore, a certain letter <note>original: "littera"; this refers to the practice of writing a magical word or character on parchment.</note> which has been painted with various words,
Is burned in warm ash and drunk in a river's water;
It will also be beneficial to place a large pillow under the head.
Likewise, you anoint with the juices of Pallas <note>original: "Palladios sucos"; a poetic reference to olive oil, as the olive tree was sacred to Pallas Athena.</note> along with the flower of roses,
And you mix in moist and ground poppy, <note>Poppy (papaver) was the primary source of opium in antiquity and the standard treatment for insomnia.</note>
With which the smeared forehead will provide easy rest at night.
Furthermore, the taste of mandrake <note>A potent sedative and hallucinogen frequently used in ancient medicine as an anesthetic or sleep aid.</note> adds a deep sleep.
Dilute, moreover, the clusters which the closed eye
Of the uvula <note>original: "uvae"; while this often means "grape," in medical contexts it frequently refers to the uvula or inflammation of the throat.</note> carries within, the shadows of the double hip,
From which they will draw sleep-inducing drinks into the throat.

### For lethargic sleep.

We have said that this is the lot for wretched mortals...

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical manual of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Having previously discussed treatments for stiff limbs and ways to induce sleep, the text now transitions into treating pathological sleep (lethargy) and epilepsy, known in antiquity as the "falling sickness" or "comitial disease."</meta>

And often, diseases that are opposites are among the same evils;
finally, sometimes the limbs are so burdened by sleep
that a deep slumber is joined with bitter death.
Therefore, let the horns of a goat be roasted over a gentle flame,
so that the smell may drive away heavy sleep from the eyes.
Or let the herb called Venus's Lip <note>original: "ueneris labrum"; likely the Wild Teasel (*Dipsacus fullonum*), which traps water in its leaves.</note> be drunk,
which is first crushed and then dissolved in a mixture of wine.
Euphorbia <term>Euphorbia: a plant genus known for its milky, irritating sap, used here as a stimulant to wake the patient.</term> mixed with acidic liquids is suitable.
Let grasses and rue be drawn into both nostrils in the same way.
Some order a dreadful thing: seven crushed bedbugs <note>original: "cimice"; insects were a common, if unpleasant, ingredient in ancient pharmacology.</note>
to be drunk in a measure of water.
They <gloss>1</gloss> consider <gloss>3</gloss> these things <gloss>2</gloss> better <gloss>2</gloss> than dying a sweet death. <note>The numbered glosses reflect the scribe's attempt to clarify the Latin word order: "They consider these things better..."</note>

## For Epilepsy

There is a type of sudden disease which takes its name from the fact
that it causes just votes to be refused to us. <note>Epilepsy was called "morbus comitialis" (the assembly disease). If a person had a seizure during a Roman political assembly (*comitia*), it was considered a bad omen from the gods, and the vote was cancelled.</note>
For often, when limbs fall in dark weakness,
this horrendous blight has broken up the council of the people.
The god himself records that such a downfall often pours forth 
during the phases of the uncertain moon. <note>Ancient medicine closely linked epilepsy to the lunar cycle, hence the term "lunacy."</note>
Black vulture's gall is beneficial with old wine <note>original: "ueteri baccho"; the name of the god Bacchus is used here as a poetic term for wine.</note>
if three spoonfuls suffice for you in a single taste.
The ash of a weasel and a swallow together is even more suitable.
Furthermore, it is useful to consume with fire the shavings <note>The OCR "plumas hoi[m]" likely refers to human hair or skin shavings (*pumas/plumas hominum*) used in folk remedies.</note> which you place 
on the back of a bald head.
Or a stone which a wandering swallow has gathered from its nest. <note>Swallow stones (chelidonii) were small pebbles found in the stomachs of young swallows, highly prized as amulets against falling sickness.</note>

<summary>This page provides ancient remedies for pathological lethargy and epilepsy, highlighting the Roman association of epilepsy with political assemblies and lunar cycles. Treatments include pungent smells like roasted goat horn, herbal mixtures, and symbolic animal products like vulture gall and swallow stones.</summary>
<keywords>lethargy, epilepsy, falling sickness, comitial disease, folk medicine, vulture gall, swallow stone, Venus's lip, Euphorbia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical manual of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous discussion on epilepsy (the "assembly disease"), the author concludes that section and moves into treatments for jaundice and the ailments of childhood, including teething and supernatural "night-hags."</meta>

[The remedy] pulls him from the bond, warms him, and lifts him as he lies; just as it is said that the taste and scent of dill <note>original: "anethi"; *Anethum graveolens*.</note> also drives away sluggishness.

### For Driving Away the Royal Disease

The royal disease <note>original: "Regius morbus"; jaundice. It was called "royal" because it was believed to be cured by the comforts of a palace—rest, wine, and fine food—or perhaps because of the golden color of the skin.</note> is truly marked by its name, since it is softened when it is treated in a high court. For this, red water from honey-wine <note>original: "mulsa"; a mixture of honey and water or wine.</note> is said to be helpful. You shall give crushed Vervain mallow <note>original: "Alcea"; *Malva alcea*.</note> moistened with warm wine. It is also beneficial to fumigate wool with wine and sulfur.

### For the Teeth of Infants and Disturbances of the Night-Hags

<note>original: "syringis"; in this context, it refers to the disturbances or "pipes" (nocturnal cries) caused by supernatural entities or illness.</note>
For the world first poured out moisture into gardens. Moreover, nature causes torment when it arms children with snowy teeth. Therefore, let the teeth of a horse be tied around the child's soft neck—specifically those teeth that were the first to fall out as the foal was growing. Or smear the tender gums with the brain of a pig <note>The OCR reads "porri" (leek), but the context of "cerebrum" (brain) and ancient pediatric medicine suggests "porci" (pig).</note> or that of a hare, which is drunk with goat's milk. Furthermore, if by chance a dark screech-owl <term>striga: a mythical night-witch or bird of prey in Roman folklore believed to suck the blood of infants or poison them with "poisonous breasts."</term> oppresses the boys, pressing her venomous breasts to their innards and lips, Vervain mallow is quickly [applied]... to be bound... he who expressed the famous comedies in Roman dress <note>original: "togatas"; a reference to the Roman playwright Titinius, who often included folk remedies and mentions of the *striga* in his plays.</note> in the ancient manner...

### For Burns from Fire and Frost

The evils of cold and heat are indeed separate, yet the burning of both harms with a similar sore. You may also consider those parts "burnt" which are damaged by frozen snow. Boiling a leafy plant <note>original: "comusa planta"; likely referring to the lily or a similar hairy-leafed herb.</note> in a mortar cures both. But truly, for that which is burnt by flames, the white of an egg...

<summary>This page provides remedies for jaundice (the "royal disease"), infant teething pains, and the mythical "striga" (night-hag), as well as treatments for both heat burns and frostbite.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Liber Medicinalis" of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous page's discussion on the "royal disease" (jaundice) and the protection of infants from supernatural "night-hags," the text concludes a treatment for boils or skin eruptions before transitioning into a famous section on the prevention of poisoning.</meta>

The juice within, [when] applied by the fruit <note>original: "pœna" in OCR, likely "pomis" or "paeonia" (peony). Based on the context of juice and healing, peony or fruit juice is intended.</note>, will be able to heal.
But you should apply dark wool with ashes,
Or mix quicklime with old oil;
You shall crush barley and join it with the whites of eggs;
Let pig fat be added; this is a marvelous form of healing.
Add celandine <note>original: "celidonis"; *Chelidonium majus*, a plant in the poppy family used historically for skin conditions.</note> and thus smear the wounds of the boils <note>original: "furis"; likely a contraction or error for *furunculis* (boils/sores).</note>.
But if it is as stiff as ice,
There is much and easy care to be found in a pleasant medicine.

### For Counteracting Poisons

So that you may be safe from the hostile deception of a stepmother <note>In Roman literature, the "poisoning stepmother" was a common trope representing domestic danger.</note>,
Or whoever, being envious, might pluck at your progress,
The remedy meets the poisons without waiting for them to strike.
Therefore, before meals, let the fruit of the walnut tree <term>iuglandis: the Persian or English walnut, often used in ancient antidotes</term> be eaten.
Cups of amber <note>original: "electri"; amber was believed to change color or crack if poison was poured into it.</note> reveal the presence of venom by their changing appearance.
Boiled waters with deer horn and bark
Are suitable as a drink, or you may take them mixed with olive oil.
The divine power has also often provided the radish <note>original: "raphanum"</note> to be eaten.
But truly, the Mithridatic antidote <note>original: "antidotus mithridatica"; named after King Mithridates VI of Pontus, who famously tried to make himself immune to poisons by taking small doses of them.</note> is spoken of
In many consecrated ways. But when the great victor <note>Pompey the Great, who defeated Mithridates in 63 BCE.</note>
Seized the chests of the King, he found in them a cheap
Formula, and he laughed at the sufficiently common remedies.
Twice ten <note>20</note> leaves of rue <note>original: "ruta"; a bitter herb used as a universal antidote in antiquity.</note> and a small grain of salt,
Two walnuts and two figs <note>original: "ficus"</note> with smooth bodies;
If these are squeezed together into a small space at the break of day,
He would take them, for he was fearful; and his mother had also given him such cups.

<summary>This page concludes a series of topical remedies for skin conditions and introduces legendary preventative measures against poisoning, specifically the "Mithridatic" formula discovered by Pompey the Great.</summary>
<keywords>Mithridates, walnuts, rue, poisoning, antidotes, Roman medicine, Pompey, amber, folk remedies</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Liber Medicinalis" (Medical Book) of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous page's discussion on walnuts and amber cups as preventative measures against poison, the text now details specific dosages, the timing of treatments based on the cycle of the illness, and specific antidotes for ingested toxins like henbane.</meta>

And when the third day has come more favorably, indeed, 
Bring the medicine to the sick person again with persistence. 
You shall take six measures <term>siliquar[um]: the *siliqua* was a small Roman unit of weight, named after the seed of the carob tree</term> of castoreum <term>castoreum: a yellowish, pungent secretion from the scent glands of a beaver, highly prized in antiquity for treating nervous disorders</term>, 
And then remember to measure out two finger-widths [of liquid]. 
Join these with a portion of clear water with care. 
Let the patient drink this so that they may believe the poison <note>original: "virus"; here meaning a venom or liquid toxin</note> will do no harm. 
The fourth day of the illness often corresponds to the first; 
The fifth will bring a great thirst, for it follows the pattern of the second; 
The harmony of waters joins the sixth day to the third; 
The seventh day runs back to the first, and following that first order, 
The tenth day likewise runs on, contending toward the twelfth. <note>The author is referring to the ancient medical theory of "critical days," where diseases were believed to follow predictable numeric cycles. Treatment was adjusted based on which day of the cycle the patient was experiencing.</note>

## Against Ingested Poison

If caution cannot prevent the unspeakable poison, 
The next step is the effort to expel the cups already given. 
They say that the milk of a donkey and a gentle cow is beneficial. 
Most of these people take a little bit of betony <note>original: "betonicam"; *Stachys officinalis*, an herb once considered a "cure-all" in Roman medicine</note>, 
And if you likewise drip in the juice of ivy <note>original: "hederæ"</note> which entangles tall trunks, 
No drinks will be able to cause harm, 
Even those cups which anyone has "enhanced" with their own herbs <note>A euphemism for someone adding toxic plants to a drink.</note>. 
If someone has tasted henbane <term>hyoscyamon: *Hyoscyamus niger*, a highly toxic plant in the nightshade family that causes hallucinations and "madness"</term>, 
Once it has been drained away by goat's milk, one can soothe the mad rages.

## For Wounds Wasting Away Over Many Days

Wounds often waste away when they have become chronic over a span of days, 
And no cure seems to help, nor is any scar formed; 
But nevertheless, such a wondrous power of herbs prevails...

<summary>This page details the rhythmic cycle of medicinal dosing over a twelve-day period and provides natural antidotes—such as animal milk, betony, and ivy—to counteract ingested poisons and the delirium caused by henbane.</summary>
<keywords>castoreum, critical days, poison antidotes, donkey milk, betony, ivy juice, henbane (hyoscyamon), chronic wounds, Roman pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Liber Medicinalis" (Medical Book) of Quintus Serenus Sammonicus. Following the previous page's discussion on antidotes for ingested poisons and the treatment for drinking henbane, the text shifts its focus to external ailments: chronic ulcers, skin growths, and warts.</meta>

So that the cavity of an old ulcer <term>ulcus: a chronic or non-healing sore</term> can heal:
Boil down clipped horehound <term>marrubium: white horehound, a bitter herb of the mint family used since antiquity for its respiratory and skin-healing properties</term> and bathe the terrible wound with that liquid. Apply the ashes of a high-flying swallow, or the rennet <note>original: "coagula"; a substance used to curdle milk, here taken from the stomach of a hare</note> of a hare sprinkled with wine, or ivy boiled in wine <note>original: "baccho"; Bacchus, the god of wine, used here as a poetic synonym for the drink itself</note>, or dill seed. Or let the ashes of alder be bound with thick honey. Earthworms will be able to draw the wound together, or a simple pile of dock <term>lapathus: a genus of plants including sorrel, often used in ancient medicine as a cleansing poultice</term> cleanses ulcers. Let fat be mixed in, but let it be untouched by any salt <gloss>is mixed</gloss>. Furthermore, pine bark with wine and sulfur restores the limbs, now made smooth and clearly no longer thick with scabs,

---

Sometimes a wart <note>original: "verruca"</note> stands out with an ugly head.
To this, a true name once stuck—though some call it foolish—of him who alone restored the state by delaying <note>This is a learned historical pun. The Roman general Quintus Fabius Maximus was nicknamed "Verrucosus" because of a wart on his lip, and "Cunctator" (the Delayer) for his guerrilla tactics against Hannibal. The author is linking the medical condition to the famous statesman.</note>. 
The blood of a lizard will be able to cure this defect,
Or dog's urine applied with wet earth.
Or the juice of the herb called *lechor* <note>original: "lechor"; likely a corruption of *lichen* or a specific regional name for a plant</note>.
Spurge <term>titimallus: plants of the genus *Euphorbia*, whose milky, caustic sap was a standard ancient treatment for "eating away" warts</term> is likewise quite fit for such a medical treatment.
If a broken wart pours out immense blood,
The ashes of ground purple wool are beneficial,
Specifically that which was truly dyed with the blood of the shellfish <term>conchylium: the murex sea snail, used to produce the highly prized "Tyrian purple" dye</term>.

---

If by chance ugly skin-growths <note>original: "papillae"; here referring to small protuberances, pustules, or perhaps hemorrhoids</note> torture the mind,
And a hard plague that runs through the lower parts...

<meta>This page continues the medical poem by Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, concluding the section on warts and skin growths. The text then transitions from the ancient content to a personal note from the 15th-century scribe, Peregrino Allio, who was a known humanist scholar in the court of Ferrara.</meta>

It often casts out blood in the manner of a wound,
and indeed it breaks through the skin. But you, quickly bring relief <term>salutem: literally "health," but in a medical context, it refers to the healing treatment or remedy</term>.
The root of a wild leek <term>agrestis porri: wild leek, a plant often used in antiquity to treat skin conditions due to its acidic properties</term> added to it is useful,
along with <note>original: "Hemio"; this is likely a scribal corruption or a specific liquid measure, possibly an 'hemina'</note> and things healed when enclosed in a fragment of walnut shell <term>iuglandis: the common walnut, whose shells and husks were frequently used in topical poultices</term>.
You shall take Baetic salt <note>original: "Sal bethicum"; salt imported from the Roman province of Baetica in modern-day Spain, highly valued for its purity in medical preparations</note>, which will not be lacking;
For you shall mix sweet honey with black soot,
And when you apply it on top, the medicine drives away the pain.

->The End<- <note>original: "Τέλοσ" (Telos); Greek for "The End" or "The Goal"</note> Praise be to God.

Written in haste and speed by me, Pere -
grino Allio in Ferrara, 1461. <note>Peregrino Allio (c. 1435–1466) was a Renaissance humanist and poet. This colophon tells us he was copying this medical text during the Italian Renaissance, a time when ancient Roman medical knowledge was being rediscovered and preserved.</note>
